Complete Book

Liberal Democracy in Crisis

How liberalism lost its way, and why working-class values offer a path forward

Click any chapter to expand and read the complete full text.

Chapter 1

Liberal Democracy in Crisis

In 1989, Viktor Orbán championed democracy against Soviet oppression. By 2014, he built an "illiberal state." His transformation exemplifies liberalism's collapse from triumphalism to crisis.

▼

Liberal Democracy in Crisis

But the century that began full of self-confidence in the ultimate triumph of Western liberal democracy seems at its close to be returning full circle to where it started :... to an unabashed victory of economic and political liberalism. -Francis Fukuyama

I follow the Moskva Down to Gorky Park Listening to the wind of change. -Scorpions

June 16, 1989. Six coffins rest beneath the monumental steps of the Műcsarnok colonnade, a neoclassical art museum dressed in black crepe for that day. Five hold the remains of Prime Minister Imre Nagy and his comrades, executed by the Soviets in 1958 for leading the uprising to end Soviet rule in Hungary. So secret were their graves that it took seven years to find them. The sixth is empty: a mute casket for every unnamed victim of the failed revolution. A quarter million Hungarians fill the square, hopeful that this is a new day for their country and fearful that the events of 1956-58 will repeat. The last speech is delivered by a bearded young man representing Fidesz, the Alliance of Young Democrats, Viktor Mihály Orbán. With an unwavering voice, he bellows:

If we believe in our own strength, we are capable of bringing the communist dictatorship to an end. If we are determined enough, we can compel the ruling party to submit to free elections. If we have not lost sight of the ideas of fifty-six, we can vote for a government that will immediately enter into negotiations leading to the immediate beginning of Russian troop withdrawals. Seventy thousand Soviet troops still occupied Hungary at the time, but that was about to end, partly thanks to Orban's powerful call for democracy and independence from Soviet control. Orbán would then go on to serve as one of the youngest prime ministers in Europe. This was the background to Francis Fukuyama's declaration that the end of history had arrived and that a brighter future for liberalism and liberal democracy was upon us. Fukuyama wasn't alone in his sentiments. Many believed the world had changed, that the drive toward greater democracy and liberty was unstoppable. In 1991, the legendary German rock band Scorpions would give another voice to these hopes by singing about the failed coup that spelled the collapse of the Soviet Union-an event that seemed to bring Fukuyama's prediction one step closer to reality. Yet the widespread optimism has now given way to a sense of crisis, and Viktor Orbán is again at the forefront. By 2014, still in power, he had completely changed his tune. Gone were calls for liberty. Orbán instead proudly announced that he was "building an illiberal state" and "work- based society" while "defending European Christianity." He centralized executive powers in his hands, clamped down on freedom of speech, and closed media outlets and universities. Orbán is an inspiration to many antiliberal politicians across the world, including in the United States. Even if recently ousted from power, he has become a symbol of the downward arc of "liberalism." For a while, liberalism and liberal democracy inspired the world, delivering much of what people longed for. Then came the crisis. What happened?

The Meaning of Liberalism "Liberalism" has a muddled meaning in common parlance. Depending on the context, it can be used with left-leaning or right-leaning connotations, such as progressive versus classical liberalism. Its meaning shifts with geography. Liberalism denotes a left-leaning ideology in the United States but typically designates a right-leaning, free-market stance in Europe. Liberalism also refers at once to a political philosophy, to the institutions built upon it, and to the practices of activists who draw-sometimes loosely-on its ideals. This reflects the fact that liberalism is a big tent, defined by a core set of ideas shared by all its varieties. All liberals would agree on the desirability of basic individual rights and freedoms (including the right not to be coerced and the freedoms of expression, religion, association, and choice); constraints on power, especially political power; and broad protections for equality before the law. Liberals believe that progress is feasible and desirable, even though they may disagree on how "progress" should be defined. Most liberals, especially left liberals, would also add to this list a firm commitment to helping the weakest and most vulnerable members of society. This bundle of beliefs is central to liberalism around the world and is typically fused with ideas of democracy, hence defining liberal democracy as the archetype of liberal institutions. This was the background to Francis Fukuyama's declaration that the end of history had arrived and that a brighter future for liberalism and liberal democracy was upon us. Fukuyama wasn't alone in his sentiments. Many believed the world had changed, that the drive toward greater democracy and liberty was unstoppable. In 1991, the legendary German rock band Scorpions would give another voice to these hopes by singing about the failed coup that spelled the collapse of the Soviet Union-an event that seemed to bring Fukuyama's prediction one step closer to reality. Yet the widespread optimism has now given way to a sense of crisis, and Viktor Orbán is again at the forefront. By 2014, still in power, he had completely changed his tune. Gone were calls for liberty. Orbán instead proudly announced that he was "building an illiberal state" and "work- based society" while "defending European Christianity." He centralized executive powers in his hands, clamped down on freedom of speech, and closed media outlets and universities. Orbán is an inspiration to many antiliberal politicians across the world, including in the United States. Even if recently ousted from power, he has become a symbol of the downward arc of "liberalism." For a while, liberalism and liberal democracy inspired the world, delivering much of what people longed for. Then came the crisis. What happened?

The Meaning of Liberalism "Liberalism" has a muddled meaning in common parlance. Depending on the context, it can be used with left-leaning or right-leaning connotations, such as progressive versus classical liberalism. Its meaning shifts with geography. Liberalism denotes a left-leaning ideology in the United States but typically designates a right-leaning, free-market stance in Europe. Liberalism also refers at once to a political philosophy, to the institutions built upon it, and to the practices of activists who draw-sometimes loosely-on its ideals. This reflects the fact that liberalism is a big tent, defined by a core set of ideas shared by all its varieties. All liberals would agree on the desirability of basic individual rights and freedoms (including the right not to be coerced and the freedoms of expression, religion, association, and choice); constraints on power, especially political power; and broad protections for equality before the law. Liberals believe that progress is feasible and desirable, even though they may disagree on how "progress" should be defined. Most liberals, especially left liberals, would also add to this list a firm commitment to helping the weakest and most vulnerable members of society. This bundle of beliefs is central to liberalism around the world and is typically fused with ideas of democracy, hence defining liberal democracy as the archetype of liberal institutions. Democracy, as I'll explain, is neither at odds with liberal ideas nor an add-on but an integral part of liberalism's values. Liberalism demands self- government and the responsibilities that go with it. This book is as much about liberalism as a political movement and liberal democratic institutions as it is about liberalism as a political philosophy that undergirds the movements and institutions. I often focus on the left-leaning corner of the big tent (such as New Deal liberalism in the U.S. and social democracy in Europe) because this kind of liberalism has historically been an engine of material and social progress. It is also this left-leaning version of liberalism that has shaped the core of liberal democratic institutions and spurred the expansion of basic rights to the working classes, women, ethnic minorities, and other groups, and set the agenda for much of the post-World War II era in the industrialized world. Any movement that departs from this bundle of core commitments-especially from the freedom from coercion and freedoms of expression, religion, association, and choice-is not liberal. This includes fascism, authoritarianism, Marxism, communism, and leftist and rightist identity-based ideologies. It was this left-leaning liberalism that lifted aspirations, spurred movements on both the Left and the Right to borrow its ideas, and, for the most part, succeeded. It is also this version of liberalism that is in crisis today-both as a political movement and in the form of liberal democratic institutions. The data tell the tangible part of this crisis-most vividly for the United States, though similar patterns appear across much of the West. Over the last three decades people of all ages have become increasingly hesitant to affirm that democracy is the best form of government. The shift is particularly pronounced among Americans between the ages of eighteen and thirty-five. Americans also report shockingly low levels of trust in democratic institutions. As of June 2023, only about a quarter of Americans said they had a great deal or quite a lot of trust in the presidency or the Supreme Court-and just 8 percent said the same about Congress. Newspapers and media fared no better. Gallup polls dating back to the early 1970s show that people once placed far greater confidence in these bedrock democratic institutions. We are witnessing an erosion of democratic trust at an unprecedented scale. When the Pew Research Center asked Americans to describe the state of the country's politics in one word in 2023, the most common choices were "bad," "corrupt," "divisive," "chaos," "messy," and "polarized." The last one is particularly apt. In survey after survey, Americans report levels of polarization unparalleled in recent history. In the 1990s, about half of surveyed Americans reported holding an equal mix of left and right views. Today, there is much less common ground between Republicans and Democrats. In the 1990s, few Republicans and Democrats held very unfavorable opinions of the other side. The majority of voters in both parties now do. No less alarming are signs that Americans are growing less willing to compromise. Majorities in both parties report that they are against deals that would meet the other side halfway. But perhaps none of this should be surprising. After all, liberal democracy hasn't lived up to its promises.

"Democracy and Proper Drains" In the 1990s, democracy-fused with liberal ideas such as individual freedoms and equality before the law-became the only game in town, in the West and beyond. Even authoritarians had to dress their regimes in democratic robes, and putschists insisted that their coups were just an effort to hunt wolves. This acceptance of liberal democracy was predicated on three promises. Sharing: Democracy pledged shared prosperity, meaning that no group would be left behind and the fruits of economic and technological change would be divided between those with economic privilege and those without. This promise was so axiomatic to democracy that when British Chartists campaigned to extend the franchise to working men in the 1840s, they argued that "the question of universal suffrage is a knife and fork question, a bread and cheese question." Everywhere around the world, people's aspirations to shared prosperity spiked when talk of democracy started. For example, the critical role of labor unions and the Workers' Party in Brazil's arduous journey from military dictatorship to democracy in the 1980s can be understood in this light. Workers organized and demanded democratic rights in part because democracy was viewed as an effective path for higher wages and better conditions. The situation in Eastern Europe wasn't entirely different. While many were drawn to democratic institutions as a way of escaping the yoke of Soviet oppression, they were equally motivated by the hope of achieving the levels of economic prosperity witnessed in the West. Drains: The idea that democracy promised public services was pithily captured by the British Poet Laureate John Betjeman, who wrote: "Think of what our Nation stands for, / ... / Democracy and proper drains." Public services were so important because many nondemocratic regimes had all but completely neglected providing health, education, roads, and drains. Conditions in British cities in the early nineteenth century, before the process of democratization got underway, were abysmal. In major industrial centers, life expectancy at birth fell below thirty years-dropping to twenty-five in Manchester, twenty-six in Liverpool, and twenty-nine in Bristol. Sewage ran through the streets, and the stench alone made daily life nearly unbearable. Democratization was part of the reason why politicians started to care and British public services started improving. In Brazil, when poor, low-education voters were effectively enfranchised in 1998, there was a sea change in politics-and in the provision of public services. Several parties promised better infant and maternal care for the poor, received their support, and proceeded to implement these policies. Previously, these voters had been practically disenfranchised owing to cumbersome electoral procedures that made it almost impossible for illiterate people to fill out the complicated forms and cast their votes for their preferred candidates. In a country where almost a quarter of the population was illiterate, a sizable fraction was left out of politics. Correspondingly, parties cared little for the education, health, or welfare of the less well-off. Things changed in one fell swoop after new voting machines were introduced in 1998, enabling people to choose the candidates they wanted on the basis of pictures and without extensive writing. Political participation soared among the poor, and politics transformed as a result. Voice: Democracy also pledged unfettered voice and self-government. People were free to express their opinions, deliberate in town halls, call their representatives, stand for office themselves, and generally participate in the governance of their communities and lives. Their voice was reflected in the political decision-making process. This is what struck the young Alexis de Tocqueville so vividly when he observed the practice of American democracy in the 1830s. He summed it up in his masterpiece Democracy in America: "The American institutions are democratic, not only in their principle but in all their consequences." No other country or epoch may have quite the same participatory character as 1830s America, but the pattern is clear. In country after country, democratization was driven by previously disenfranchised people seeking a political voice and a chance to participate. Democracy was never perfect, and Winston Churchill's quip that it was "the worst form of government except for all those other forms that have been tried from time to time" was never too far from the minds of both voters and politicians. Democracy also sometimes veered away from liberal ideas and freedoms. Still, across the industrialized world, liberal democracy gradually advanced, delivering on these three promises -most fully after World War II, when democracy had matured and left-liberal ideas gained greater influence. Let's look at liberal democracy's track record, starting with sharing prosperity. The three decades that followed World War II witnessed rapid economic growth in the United States. What was truly remarkable was how equally distributed this prosperity turned out to be. Of course, inequality still persisted between geographical regions, between different levels of education, between races, between genders, between bosses and workers, and between the privileged and the rest. Nonetheless, all education groups took their share from growth, with workers benefiting as much as managers (in proportional terms) and those with lower education levels experiencing an even faster rise in their wages than the college educated. Women expanded their employment and saw their earnings relative to men increase rapidly during those decades, even if men returning from deployment took jobs back from Rosie the Riveter and got paid more than women. The gap between Black and white Americans also narrowed at an unprecedented rate, as schooling opportunities improved and labor market discrimination lessened. The story of shared prosperity wasn't just an American one. Growth was faster in countries-such as Germany, France, and Japan-that had to rebuild the cities and factories decimated by bombing, war, and occupation. European growth in these decades proved even more equitable than in America, thanks in part to the spread of the Scandinavian social democratic model and to the pivotal role of labor unions in the postwar settlement. Democracy also delivered drains. Public infrastructure in many Western nations in the middle of the twentieth century was still in a pitiful state. In 1947, more than half of houses in the U.K. had no bathroom, and as recently as 1960, four-fifths of houses had no refrigerator. The situation was a little better in the United States on average, but much worse for Black Americans and for those in the rural South. All of that has improved. By the 1970s, almost all households in the U.S. and the U.K. had indoor plumbing and a few other amenities; most neighborhoods in both countries could access city centers by public transport; and both American and British countrysides now had roads that linked them to highways, which meant much better transport for people and goods, and greater economic opportunities. Advances in health were equally striking. Inhabitants of the largest U.S. cities had some limited access to hospitals in the 1950s, while rural Americans barely had any. For example, in 1950, Boston, New York, and San Francisco each had more than eighteen general hospitals and Chicago had fifty-five, but there were swaths of the Midwest and the South where people would have to travel hundreds of miles to get any health care. The Hill-Burton Act, passed in 1946 to modernize and expand health care access, began changing that, kick-starting a hospital- building frenzy. By 1975, the United States had 1,500 more hospitals than it did in 1948, and many of those were in smaller cities and rural areas. Closing the gap in education between the haves and have-nots wasn't easy, but America found success there, too. In the 1940s, about 50 percent of American children graduated from high school. By the end of the 1960s this number had reached 77 percent and there were essentially no illiterate American children left. The rate of educational mobility-the likelihood that children of parents without a high school degree would graduate from high school-also increased rapidly from the 1940s onward. Liberal democratic governments delivered not just public services but also important regulations for protecting people. Since the beginning of the Industrial Revolution, the perennial problem had been ensuring that companies offered safe products to their consumers and safe work environments to their employees. Drugs that killed rather than cured, workplaces that were death traps, and myriad falsely advertised products were as common as dirt. Laudanum, an extract of opium, was frequently used and prescribed for children in the early 1900s in the United States. A 1901 medical book recommended one teaspoon of laudanum every three hours for diarrhea and other childhood ailments. Yet laudanum killed, plain and simple. It was the 1906 Pure Food and Drug Act that finally required that compounds containing opium, cocaine, heroin, morphine, and alcohol be labeled as such. The author Upton Sinclair worked incognito in a Chicago meatpacking plant for seven weeks, researching working conditions for articles he was planning on poverty and safety hazards in workplaces. The result, first published in serial form in 1905, was his masterpiece exposé The Jungle, which revealed the horrors that poor, uneducated, often immigrant workers had to endure without government protection. In 1913, for example, the number of industrial workers killed on the job was 23,000, corresponding to about 61 deaths per 100,000 workers. Against this backdrop, democracy's commitment was to protect the consumers and workers, and it started delivering on this commitment as well. The 1906 Federal Meat Inspection Act was a response to Sinclair's book. Other acts that similarly saved limbs and lives followed. Over time, worker deaths declined to about 4 in 100,000, a drastic improvement from their highs in the 1910s. Liberal democracy brought voice to the people as well. Americans participated in politics by going to town halls, debating with friends and neighbors, governing their local communities, and casting their ballots in elections (even if turnout rates in elections ebbed and flowed throughout this period). When the political scientist Robert Dahl set out to investigate "who governs" local politics in New Haven in the 1950s, the answer wasn't an established party or a well-defined elite. The involvement of regular people in politics was key for the governance of the city, just as Tocqueville had surmised more than a century prior. Democratic voice wasn't confined to the United States, either. At the end of World War II, British working-class men and women voted out Winston Churchill, despite his legendary leadership during the darkest days of the war. In his place, they installed a Labour government promising to put into practice the principles advocated in the wartime Beveridge Report, penned by the liberal economist William Beveridge. The report recommended a welfare state that would make the economy fairer, labor unions stronger, public services more widely available, and people's lives less precarious with national health insurance and social security. Governments with similar pledges were voted into office throughout Western Europe. Promises Broken By the 2000s, the tide had turned. Many voters were convinced that liberal democracy had reneged on its core promises. Economic growth after the mid-1970s looked nothing like the 1950s or the 1960s. The oil crises of 1973 and 1979 caused sharp economic contractions and debilitating stagflation (high inflation and unemployment). The economic slowdown was temporary and productivity started growing again in the 1980s. But wages didn't. U.S. median inflation-adjusted wages, which had increased by more than 2.5 percent per year between 1948 and 1973, plateaued. From 1980 on, wage growth came in at just 0.45 percent per year. Even average wages, pulled up by earnings at the top, fared not much better than the median. Median and average wages tell only part of the story. Manual workers and those with less than a college degree saw their wages decline, while professionals, managers, and workers in technical occupations continued to enjoy rapid growth. The richest 1 percent of U.S. households increased their share of national income from around 10 percent in 1980 to a striking 19 percent in 2019. Their share of the total wealth of the country was even more extreme: about 35 percent. The disparities transcended divisions like managers versus workers, the megarich versus the rest, or postgraduates versus high school graduates. Every dimension of U.S. inequality soared after 1980. The gap between major metropolitan areas such as New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, and San Francisco and smaller cities and rural areas expanded. The distribution of income between capital (business owners and financiers) and labor (workers) started moving in a decidedly more unequal direction, too, with capital capturing more and more of national income over the last several decades. The economic progress of Black Americans came to an end, and so did much of the "American dream," which had promised that people from all walks of life could rise up and would witness their children doing better than themselves. While this was partly true for individuals born in 1940, 90 percent of whom earned more than their parents did, for people born in the 1980s the progress completely stopped, with about half doing worse than their parents. Americans may have been slow to realize how the economy, and the norms of sharing within it, had changed. But they are now quite aware. A recent Pew Research Center survey documents an alarming statistic: Nearly three out of four Americans think that today's children will be financially worse off than their own generation. Shared prosperity has petered out in other countries as well, though not as spectacularly as in the United States. By the 1970s, inequality in America was already higher than elsewhere in the industrialized world-and in the decades that followed it surged more sharply than in these nations. Weak unions, a meager minimum wage, and thin norms against workplace inequality all helped drive this trend. American banks and multinationals, especially tech firms, were also more powerful and more inclined to pay huge salaries to their top employees. American manufacturing companies kept increasing the compensation of their executives, even as they laid off workers and offshored jobs. Public services suffered as well. Some of this was because low-hanging fruit was already picked. Building schools is expensive, but it's much easier than increasing the quality of education once the buildings are there. New hospitals are game changers and save lives. But making health care affordable, widely accessible, and effective is harder. Education has long been a thorny challenge for the United States, where a significant fraction of students came from immigrant families, faced racial discrimination, or grew up in poverty. In such conditions, schools could appear to fail even when they managed to help many of their pupils. But in recent years, they truly have come up short. Many indicators point to worsening educational performance over the last decades, even as (inflation-adjusted) spending per pupil rose from around $6,500 in the 1970s to almost $17,000 in 2019 (both in 2022-2023 dollars). By the late 2010s, only a third of fourth-grade students could read at the "proficient level" expected for their grade. The situation deteriorated further with the arrival of the pandemic. Parents begged to send their kids back to the classroom but were often ignored. Further drops in reading and quantitative proficiency followed. Health is the field where the failure of American democracy is in a league of its own. At the turn of the twenty-first century, the United States was spending about three times more on health care per person than countries like France, the U.K., or Japan. Yet by every indicator, Americans were less healthy and shorter-lived than their European counterparts. Life expectancy at birth in 2011 was 80.7 in the U.K., 82.3 in France, and 82.7 in Japan. In the United States, it was only 78.7. Worse, in a striking and unparalleled fashion, mortality rates among middle-aged white Americans started rising in the mid-2010s, even as spending kept creeping up. While opioid addiction and alcoholism played a role-making some economists refer to these deaths as "deaths of despair"-this was indicative of a more general failure in public health. Many Americans aren't even afforded basic preventive health care. In the late 2010s, one in four of them did not have access to a primary-care physician, and one in ten did not have health insurance. As a result, Americans suffer almost 25 percent more avoidable deaths than residents of the average industrialized nation. COVID added salt to the wound, with Americans experiencing outsize declines in life expectancy compared to other countries. While education and health attract attention, U.S. democracy faltered in less obvious areas, too. Public service interruptions with worsening road conditions and closures became commonplace, and once something went under repair, it would be years before it came back online. Americans saw on TV how the Chinese government could build bridges in months, while even simple repairs took years in their own country. By the 2000s, Italy had better and faster trains than the U.S. Potholes and congested roads became more jarring when public transport wasn't a realistic option for many. As congestion became worse in cities, many metropolitan areas launched new road-building programs and sometimes even new public transport systems, such as the Big Dig in Boston or the Los Angeles-San Francisco high-speed rail project. Yet in each case, initial cost estimates were exceeded severalfold and the completion of the projects was delayed by many years, sometimes by more than a decade. Planning for the Big Dig in Boston began in 1982, with an estimated cost of $2.6 billion (equivalent to about $8.7 billion today) and a projected completion date of 1998. By 1991, the project was still in the planning stage. Once digging started, cost overruns exceeded even the wildest expectations of its critics. By the time the project was completed in 2007, with a nine-year delay, the cost was about three times the original projection, making it the most expensive public works project in U.S. history. From the late 1960s to the 1980s, the inflation-adjusted cost of building a mile of highway increased threefold. This wasn't because inputs such as material and labor became more expensive but largely because of regulation-including the myriad permissions for public works and all the hoops that companies have to jump through to get federal contracts. For a simple repair of the Longfellow Bridge near MIT, where I work, these permissions included the following items, just to name a few: approvals from the Massachusetts Department of Transportation, Federal Highway Administration, Massachusetts Bay Transportation Authority, Massachusetts Department of Conservation & Recreation, U.S. Coast Guard, City of Boston, City of Cambridge, and Massachusetts Department of Environmental Protection. Deemed a historically significant bridge, the project also required approval from six federal, state, and local stakeholders under section 106 of the National Historic Preservation Act and needed to give them plenty of time to review all aspects of the proposed design that could impact the bridge's historic character. The project was additionally subject to environmental review under both the Massachusetts Environmental Policy Act and the federal National Environmental Policy Act of 1969. A task force composed of representatives from civic organizations, businesses, and the government was convened to get input from members of the public, which added dozens of forums and meetings. Planning and approval for the Longfellow Bridge repairs took four years. The actual repairs took another five, from 2013 to 2018-short in comparison to the planning period, but longer than the China Road and Bridge Corporation needed to complete the world's longest bridge, Danyang-Kunshan Grand Bridge, in 2011, which was ready for use after only four years of work. The Danyang-Kunshan spans more than one hundred miles, the Longfellow Bridge less than half a mile. Studies on housing construction have found the same pattern. With runaway costs due to federal and local regulations, it became increasingly difficult to build new housing projects, even as homelessness proliferated in most metropolitan areas. In San Francisco, for instance, population growth greatly outstripped the increase in housing supply, and the homeless population soared by 50 percent just between 2000 and 2018. It's one thing to make sure there is no lead in the water and confirm that prescription drugs aren't lethal. It's another thing entirely to require dozens of approvals and years of delays for a routine public works project-or to make somebody jump multiple hurdles just to shut off the water to repair a faucet in their building. One might also question the point of requiring hairdressers and massage therapists to meet licensing requirements as onerous as those of lawyers. The simple promise of reliable drains has turned into a Kafkaesque nightmare for Americans. No wonder they called democracy "messy." One sobering consequence of this self-enforced bureaucracy was the failure of President Joe Biden's signature infrastructure agenda. By the time Biden left office in January 2025, only about 70 percent of the projects had even begun and only a small fraction had been completed. It was a textbook case of shooting oneself in the foot. Regulations imposed by his own administration (governing who could be employed at what wage, how many hours they could work, what steps had to be taken to comply with antidiscrimination and environmental rules, and even which materials could be used) and paperwork, a lot of paperwork, applied to all federal contractors and ended up stalling the very projects President Biden hoped to advance. The narrative for a long time was that regulations were heavy and stifling in Europe and that this trend was accelerated by the overlapping jurisdictions of national governments and the European Commission. Yet the steady creep of U.S. regulations affecting public services, though sometimes overlooked, is second to none. Let us next turn to democratic voice, also in decline both in the U.S. and Europe. Nobody now expects that Robert Dahl would find regular people to be influential in the politics of cities like New Haven. Today, more than 85 percent of Americans believe elected officials do not care about what people like them think. They are right: Researchers have found that elected officials vote along the lines of the preferences of their richest constituents, ignoring the policy preferences of the bottom two-thirds. Many Americans thus came to feel that their voices didn't matter and were in fact being actively silenced. Some of this was because of falsehoods and propaganda on social media and talk radio, but there was also a grain of truth. Take the issue of immigration. Throughout the last several decades, about half of the U.S. population has been concerned about immigration, especially illegal immigration (even if those concerns were sometimes much amplified by false information and Americans still largely agree that some immigration is good for the country). Roughly 50 percent of white Americans report that there are too many immigrants from at least one of the major regions of the world, and about 54 percent believe that immigration should be reduced. Mainstream politicians haven't paid much attention to these concerns for most of this period. These perceptions became intensified with the visible rise of identity politics and discussions of racism and gender issues in school curricula. It is hard to identify the exact trigger. It may have been white, middle-class parents seeing the school curricula as their children took classes from home during COVID. It might have been discussions of systemic racism following the murder of George Floyd, which many white parents took as a personal accusation of racism. It may have been because of all the talk about "critical race theory" and "transgender bathrooms," so much amplified by right-wing influencers and political activists. In any case, the overall effect was clear: Many Americans felt that schools, civil servants, politicians, and activists were pushing for cultural agendas that they opposed. The perceived suppression of voter voice was also present in Europe, especially on cultural issues and immigration. In the 2025 federal elections, the virulently anti-immigrant Alternative for Germany (AfD) party approached 50 percent of the vote in parts of former East Germany, but other parties agreed that they would not enter into a coalition with AfD, and the mainstream media robustly criticized AfD, its ideology, and its supporters. In 2024, the rest of German parliamentarians even decided to ban MPs from the AfD from joining the Parliament's recreational soccer team. While these strategies may have been somewhat effective in slowing down the rise of this insurgent movement and defending the basic liberties of immigrants and ethnic minorities in Germany, they also meant millions of Germans felt their democratic voice was silenced. Similar dynamics played out in several other European countries. The bottom line was that for many voters in the West, democracy didn't quite live up to its promises. This doesn't mean that alternative systems would've performed better. The evidence is clear that democracy is much more responsive than other regimes to citizen voice. It typically deals better with instability, and it provides better health and education. Recent studies document that democracies are also consistently more likely to lead to faster economic growth than the alternatives, as I explain in chapter 4. There are important technological and social reasons for the underperformance of democracies in the postindustrial era, as we'll see. But this remains no consolation when the raison d'être of democracy-its promise of better economic, living, and political conditions for the masses- floundered.

Has Liberalism Failed Because It Has Succeeded? What we are witnessing isn't a failure just of democracy but of liberal democracy. It was liberal democracy that promised-and for a while delivered-shared prosperity and public services. It was also a core pledge of liberal democracy that it would provide voice, free voice, to the people. This is part of the reason that what we are living through is a crisis of liberalism and liberal democracy. The conservative thinker Patrick Deneen captured something that was already in the air in the late 2010s when he wrote in Why Liberalism Failed: "Liberalism has failed-not because it fell short, but because it was true to itself. It has failed because it has succeeded." Here, by "success," Deneen is not making a welfare assessment, but rather claiming that liberalism was able to impose its values on society. Deneen was neither the first nor the last right-wing commentator condemning the entire liberal project-while ignoring its earlier phenomenal successes. Tellingly, many of these right-wing critics of liberalism became more popular over the last two decades, as their long- forgotten ideas morphed into novel antiliberal philosophies, including new ethnocentric conceptualizations of politics, various renditions of authoritarianism, and new monarchisms. However, none of these antiliberal alternatives have correctly diagnosed what has gone wrong. Nor have the liberals themselves. Without such a diagnosis, it is impossible to know whether liberalism and liberal democracy are permanently maimed. It is equally hopeless to articulate templates about how liberalism should renew itself. A key thesis of this book is that liberalism started strong. It managed to put its ideals into practice-first indirectly as others reacted to liberal aspirations, and then directly by building liberal democratic institutions once in power. And it produced results to show for it. Then it became the establishment, embraced by most politicians, bureaucrats, and activists. Establishment liberalism did not adjust to new realities. Worse, liberals lost the plot and some of liberalism's values together with it. Liberalism's derailment was the result of several concurrent trends. For one, the postindustrial economy made it much harder for liberal democracy to deliver on its promises. Liberalism changed as the college-educated segment of society became more dominant, and this made liberal democracies less likely to seek solutions to the postindustrial challenges that heavily afflicted the less educated. This was a sin of omission. The sin of commission was that the postindustrial educated elite abandoned many of the key precepts of liberalism, including the commitment to self-government, community, and shared prosperity, which were critical for liberal democracy's past success. While both sins may have started with liberalism as a political movement, they also affected liberal democratic institutions and liberal philosophies. Liberalism's core tenets were justified when the right to dissent and other freedoms needed to be protected against mercurial tyrants or scheming tycoons. Its emphasis on equality before the law and constraints on power was inspiring when it was clear to most that, as Lord Acton put it famously, power corrupted and absolute power corrupted absolutely. Liberalism's political agenda of voice was effective and just in such a context. Its economic promises were both hopeful and realistic in the age of mass production, because individual liberties, markets, and economic growth could be natural bedfellows of shared prosperity and democracy. Mass production created a clear pathway from economic growth and expanding markets to higher wages and thus shared prosperity-so long as the underlying institutional structure supported worker voice and competition in the labor market. Democratic politics infused with liberal values then provided a safety net, redistribution, and public services that were essential for both a level playing field and functioning markets. Liberal democracy shone brightest when standing against fascism, Nazism, and communism, whose atrocities proved that unchecked power was no abstract threat to life and liberty. But with Mussolini, Hitler, and Stalin gone, and now itself at the helm, liberalism had to contend with its own dilemmas. From the very beginning, liberal philosophy and practice attempted a delicate balancing act when it came to the relationship between the less educated masses and the educated elites. Intellectual elites formulated the inspiring ideas of liberalism, going all the way back to the philosophers of ancient Greece and Rome, whose very existence and comfort depended on their economic privileges and the labor of slaves and other unfree workers. Many, like one of the founding fathers of liberalism, John Stuart Mill, thought that the tyranny of the majority to be feared was often the tyranny of the uneducated majority. This was part of the reason why Mill advocated more political say in public affairs for the educated. A dim view of the less educated was shared by many other early liberal thinkers, like Voltaire and Johann Gottlieb Fichte. It was scarcely conceivable to these liberal thinkers that the educated elite would emerge as a very powerful block in liberal democracy, ruling from the commanding heights of business, government, bureaucracy, and civil society. Liberalism's tensions paled in comparison to the agenda of constraining the power of unelected monarchs, authoritarian leaders, and industrialists as rich as Croesus (or at the very least as rich as John Rockefeller). They were nonetheless quite thorny. The main conflicts centered on intolerance and ignorance (though there was also another, intersecting problem of inclusion, to which I return in chapter 5). On the one hand, all ideas could be advocated. On the other, many in the masses advocated intolerance. On the one hand, everybody should have the freedom of expression and choice. On the other, some liberals quickly concluded that only the educated recognized the values of these freedoms and had the expertise to wield them for societal gain, so they should have more say in public decisions than those who were more ignorant. It was thus understandable for some to turn to social engineering as part of the liberal agenda. By social engineering I mean efforts to shape the values and thoughts of a population-forcefully and without critical thinking or debate. Social engineering can be top-down, led by the state, or spearheaded by powerful constituencies within society. Social engineering isn't unique to liberalism and liberal activists. Every regime, regardless of its ideological commitments, engages in some amount of it. Almost every political philosopher, going back to (the not-so- liberal) Plato, agreed that the inculcation and cultivation of certain values-and, yes, indoctrination-was essential for the flourishing of the institutions they preferred. Plato, just like Aristotle, favored state-controlled education, lest "ignorance, the root and the stem of every evil," prevail. Education, the bedrock of our modern society, was first and foremost a method of indoctrination. Religion may or may not be the "opium of the people," as Karl Marx opined, but it has always been a way of changing people's behavior via inculcation of morals and rituals, communal enforcement, and inducement of rewards in the after-death. So was education for the most part (except the after-death bit). Social engineering is a troublesome issue for the liberal agenda. Most liberals would subscribe, in the abstract, to the view that every school of thought, no matter how intolerant, should be respected and allowed to be aired. Nevertheless, the belief that those committed to liberalism could educate and convince the intolerant to become more open-minded was always in the background. What would be the limits to such convincing? Is it okay to have an education system that tries to inculcate the values of the liberal elites in everybody? What about civil society, media, and leading corporations pushing for the same values? Is it acceptable to pulverize the norms and traditions of communities that support intolerance? What are the limits to social pressure? Opprobrium? Ostracism? Put differently, the extent and form of convincing matters, especially for liberalism. Arguing for certain values-and doing so in a way that is consistent with critical thinking and debate and that brings people together under shared moral principles-is very different from social engineering efforts that impose such values without any room for disagreement. Although dilemmas about tolerating the intolerant and whether people should be turned into "better" free citizens were inherent to liberalism from the get-go, the challenges of adapting to a postindustrial society while liberalism itself was in control were entirely new. Postindustrial Economics A major cause of the failures of liberal democracy is to be found in what I will call-after a term introduced by the French thinker Alain Touraine and American sociologist Daniel Bell-the postindustrial age. By this term I refer not just to the economy being transformed by digital technologies, the waning role of manufacturing, and the growing importance of health, education, and administration, but also to the greater emphasis on cognitive skills and college education, both in workplaces and in social and political life. Liberal democracies achieved shared prosperity in the industrial age, partly because industrial production required a great deal of labor in factories for mass production, in the transport system to haul the inputs and outputs around, and in back offices to fuse together the design, engineering, production, and marketing functions. Greater demand for labor meant higher wages. Liberal democracy created a propitious environment for building and sharing knowledge, including scientific and technological knowledge. It also fostered the accumulation of practical knowledge about how to build norms and institutions suited to our evolving social and material conditions-and doing so in a democratic context. Rapid socioeconomic change could, for the most part, go together with stability and adaptation. One of democracy's key roles was preventing companies or business associations from using their power to suppress worker wages or force employees into dangerous or unhealthy working conditions. It achieved this through regulations as well as by allowing labor to organize. Democracy supported markets, and markets brought greater demand for industrial production and, within the framework bolstered by the labor movement, created more possibilities for wage growth. Emblematic cases of this type of democracy-market synergy included the social democratic model that took root in the 1930s in Scandinavia (which I discuss in chapter 4) as well as Franklin Delano Roosevelt's attempts in the same direction during the New Deal (chapters 4 and 7). This path was neither easy nor preordained. It was always a Goldilocks problem to determine the balance between labor and capital-labor organizations needed to be protected from being crushed but couldn't be so strong as to derail investment. The extent to which this happened was, of course, far from perfect and varied across societies. Compare, for instance, Sweden, with its strong unions embedded in a corporatist structure, negotiating wages and working conditions at the industry level since the 1930s, to the U.S., where unions have been confined to a weak bargaining position at the plant level since the Taft-Hartley Act of 1947. Another source of instability was technological change that could lead to too much automation, taking over tasks from labor and displacing workers, or other forms of disruption from new goods, services, and production techniques. But the United States and many European nations managed to plod along, partly due to luck and partly because the democratic institutional environment ensured that technological changes were not strongly biased against workers and did not threaten the foundations of shared prosperity. While nondemocratic regimes have occasionally delivered economic growth, their success has been uneven-and, as the Soviet case shows, often accompanied not by shared prosperity but by repression, suffering, and death. Lots of death. The industrial age was also an auspicious time for providing voice, self-government, and public services to the masses. Mass production resulted in a high concentration of workers in cities and factories. The repeated contact and solidarity (mutual understanding, regard, and care) forged through arduous physical work became the basis of the labor movement, creating a natural habitat for worker voice, both in workers' daily lives and in politics. Industrial production and worker organization were thus complementary. Cities additionally boosted self- government, because they created new pathways for people to participate in local and national politics and housed new political and civil- society organizations, contributing to popular mobilization. Industrial output growth made it feasible and affordable to build roads and infrastructure, and economic growth meant more tax revenues for governments, enabling them to invest in infrastructure, education, and health. Because many nondemocratic governments and some conservative-led democracies, such as Britain, had neglected the welfare and voice of the working classes, there was much to be done. Regulation was an important part of this bargain, since it helped ensure that the labor movement could flourish and corporations did not abuse their power vis-à-vis consumers. Regulations were even more central in ensuring equal access. Rules and due process were essential for equality before the law, which mattered more when there were public services, such as health care and education, to be distributed- particularly starting from a situation in which the poor did not have much access to these services. Then computers happened. The arrival of digital technologies reworked all facets of the bargain and unleashed a huge increase in inequality. Almost two-thirds of Americans, typically more tolerant than Europeans of economic disparities between rich and poor, now report that "there's too much inequality." Three of the changes brought by digital technologies are important in this context. First, computers began to enter workplaces, requiring workers with greater education and cognitive skills. Employers started going after brains rather than brawn. Second, digital technologies became a platform for an accelerated process of industrial automation, with numerically controlled machinery, semiautomated and automated advanced tools, industrial robots, and computerized workplace technologies spreading rapidly in manufacturing and parts of the nonmanufacturing economy. While computers boosted the demand for cognitive skills, automation reduced the need for manual skills, typically supplied by lower-education workers. Workers with college and postgraduate degrees and specialized cognitive skills, such as programming or advanced legal or managerial expertise, saw their earnings rise. Simultaneously, opportunities and wages declined for blue-collar and manual workers. Automation wasn't the only factor transforming the industrial structure of advanced economies; globalization and growing demand for services played major roles as well. Trade with low-wage countries was a potent driver of deindustrialization, as it meant that many industries, such as textiles, apparel, toys, furniture, food products, and simple electronics, were crushed by flows of cheap imports or survived only by offshoring much of their production process. Nevertheless, digital automation-especially via the use of advanced industrial machinery and robots-accounts for the bulk of the changes in the U.S. wage structure, as I'll discuss in chapter 6. Overall, rising demand for college-educated workers, automation of various manual and office tasks, and globalization and offshoring together fueled a massive increase in inequality, shattering the shared-prosperity promise of liberal democracies. All of this was exacerbated by the passivity of the postindustrial elites in charting a new course that wouldn't completely leave behind those with lower education. The third change brought by computers was equally fundamental, even if its effects are more variegated. The digital age increased the numbers and the social power of college graduates and ushered in the era of postindustrial politics. It also set the scene for liberalism's sins of commission.

Postindustrial Politics "Educated elite" is an overused term. But it started becoming a reality in the postindustrial era, and three interlocked processes altered the social and political role of highly educated individuals in modern society. The first was the sheer increase in the number of college graduates. In a democracy, however weak bottom-up participation may be, numbers matter. When only a few percent of the population were college graduates, their political power was limited. Beginning in the industrial age and then accelerating in the postindustrial era, there was a massive increase in the number of people with college degrees. In the United States, the acceleration started in the 1960s, and though it slowed down later, the significantly higher rates of college graduation among the very large baby-boom cohorts fundamentally altered the educational composition of the U.S. population. Today, over 40 percent of the U.S. workforce has a four-year college degree (up from less than 5 percent in the 1940s) and more than 70 percent have some college or an associate degree. Correspondingly, the college educated became a political force to reckon with. The second process was much more important. Starting once again in the industrial economy and then accelerating in the postindustrial one, there was a huge increase in the demand for college-educated experts in the commanding heights of the economy-the management of companies, the supervision of more technical or complex work, civil administration, the education and health sectors, media, and nongovernmental organizations. College graduates came to occupy almost all of these critical positions. The third process stood in contrast to the first two. It was driven by the growing divergence within the educated class. "Elite graduates" with highly coveted specialized skills, such as programming, strong quantitative knowledge, high levels of medical expertise, legal knowledge, or exceptional social skills, became wealthier and socially more influential. The rise of the Silicon Valley entrepreneurs, scientists, and engineers is just one such example. Set apart from these elites were the rest of the college educated, whom I'm going to refer to as the "mass college educated" to emphasize the group's much larger size and their lack of similarly highly valuable skills relative to elite graduates. The aspirations of the mass college educated could never be fulfilled. It was not realistic to expect all of the 40 percent of the population with college degrees (as in the U.S.) to earn the same lucrative salaries and reach the same social standing as top software engineers or the lawyers at the best firms. For many college graduates, the main option was working at lower pay in jobs for which they were overeducated, including, most famously, as baristas. And factions within the college-educated group didn't see eye to eye on many issues. There were major disagreements between, for example, the elite graduates of the tech sector and the left-leaning graduates of the Ivy League working in nongovernmental organizations and the civil service. But they agreed on most cultural questions-valuing diversity, viewing individual autonomy and group-level identity as critical components of freedom, and prioritizing cosmopolitan values and globalism against nativism. They also disdained what they considered to be intolerance and ignorance. These shared understandings not only reflected common values among the educated group but also were congruent with the economic interests of elite graduates. Sectors like finance and medicine, and especially Silicon Valley, have been centrally integrated into the global economy and have depended on access to talented immigrants. The cultural common denominator held together the two wings of the educated class, even as their numbers and clout grew. This meant an emphasis on cultural change both in the education system and in daily speech and behavior-by regulations, setting norms, or even thought policing. Social engineering is often attractive to elite groups because it confers on them greater status and social influence. Policing the values of society increases the demand for expertise and general education, and being in charge of setting these values is itself a key source of power. History offers many examples of social engineering as prestigious vocation-for example, for the members of the Catholic Church in medieval Europe, for scholars (the ulema) in eleventh-century Islam, for the Brahmans in the Hindu caste system, and for the mandarins in the imperial bureaucracy in China. Intense social engineering and regulations are natural bedfellows for two distinct reasons. The first is related to political scientist James C. Scott's pioneering analysis of state intervention in his book Seeing Like a State. Scott emphasized the need for top-down state intervention to make society "legible" by simplifying and sometimes discarding local information, practices, and norms-in the same way that a low-resolution map simplifies and eliminates variability in local topography. Such simplification is essential for regulation via simple rules and procedures. Analogously, social engineers need to ignore norms and variation in local communities in their efforts to bring all of them under a uniform set of values and regulations. The second synergy between social engineering and regulations is a consequence of liberalism's success. Liberals everywhere correctly recognized the critical role that regulations played for much of the twentieth century in curtailing absolute power and creating safer and better conditions for working people. They also, partially correctly, identified the association between procedures and equality before the law-if everybody follows well-defined procedures, everybody will be subject to the same laws, with no exceptions for the privileged. These two forces made it natural for postindustrial elites to presume that more regulation would be better-especially when it came to antidiscrimination, poverty reduction, safety, and the general process of normative change in society. The result was active social engineering coupled with an intense regulatory agenda. I do not mean to suggest that there wasn't a genuine desire on the part of many in the educated class to improve society. There definitely was. Enduring inequities and intolerance toward certain groups were real, and many well-educated liberals became convinced that altering people's values-toward greater tolerance of minorities and immigrants and toward a more sustainable economy-was vital for improving society. My argument is only that social engineering gained momentum from broader processes unleashed by the postindustrial economy and politics. These forces accelerated it, drove it in a counterproductive and more intrusive direction, and ultimately betrayed liberal values, while provoking backlash against liberalism itself. Postindustrial society undermined liberalism. Automation eroded the natural pathways to shared prosperity and solidarity. Regulation drifted away from those it was meant to protect. Social engineering became more intense and turned its back on some of the foundational liberal values, building up a powerful backlash. These were all children of postindustrialism and liberalism's sins of omission and commission.

"Walk the Streets as Other Men" With these sins, perhaps liberalism has run its course and we should turn to postliberal philosophies for guidance. Maybe liberal democracy is no longer the right framework for society to come together around inspiring objectives. A central argument of this book is that this isn't so: Liberalism is worth saving and remaking. In the next chapter, I make this case by developing a new theory of liberalism that occupies a corner of the big liberal tent (though some may say that it also stretches that corner a little). This is a theory of liberalism fusing protection of individual rights with community and centering on the quest for shared prosperity. My theory is based on four premises: (1) fallibility (the idea that every belief, proposition, or piece of knowledge could be wrong); (2) social learning in community (that humans share their information and produce collective, useful knowledge via communication within their extended, intersecting communities built on trust and shared history, norms, and interests); (3) gentle epistemic improvement (while incorrect beliefs can spread for a while, there are also reasons for more useful knowledge and more accurate descriptions of reality to gain an edge over time via debate, deliberation, and social learning); (4) desirability of collective knowledge (we can put knowledge to work to achieve our various social objectives and change our technologies, norms, and institutions for improving our lives). These four premises together lead to a number of conclusions-or pillars of liberal institutions and liberal democracy, as I am envisioning them-about how we should organize society. These are: (i) the vitality of experimentation (how we should encourage people to try new things so that society can build technical and social knowledge); (ii) nondomination and self-government (how power and institutions should be organized in a liberal society); (iii) economic growth (how improving our material conditions and sharing this improvement is central to the liberal agenda); (iv) soft communitarianism (how to make community an integral part of a liberal democracy while balancing its potential negative effects on freedom). In sum, (my version of) liberalism calls for giving individuals and communities maximal room and resources to experiment. Nondomination and self-government are about providing the resources for such experimentation and also enabling self-governing communities to try new arrangements. Economic growth and soft communitarianism are about sharing the knowledge generated by experimentation and turning collective knowledge into material and social betterment. Nondomination differs from the (classical liberal) principle of noninterference, which asks that individuals be free from state or other private actors' control over their thoughts and actions. Nondomination requires that nobody, not the state, not another individual, not a group armed with customs, has the arbitrary ability to restrict one's choices-of course, subject to the same constraints that noninterference maintains against behaviors that are directly harmful to others. The distinction here is between lack of intervention versus inability to intervene arbitrarily. Nondomination also assures some basic social and economic security so that people have the resources to try new things. For example, an individual who fears that they or their family will die of hunger if they are fired by their employer is in a situation of domination and could not be expected to experiment freely. Therefore, nondomination calls for some amount of redistribution and the provision of public services such as health and infrastructure to ensure a level playing field in the economy. Even more important, nondomination links up with soft communitarianism-it is impossible if people do not govern themselves. Laws, institutions, and norms always have power over people, and if you have no input into what is banned and what is punished, you would be in a situation of dominance. Self-government-the idea that individuals and communities should be involved in governing themselves-is central to ensuring that people are bound by laws and norms that they themselves have a say in. Self-government is also the best bulwark against concentration of power, which is inimical to nondomination, and is vital for social learning, since it enables communities to engage in their own institutional and normative experiments. Community, self-government, economic security, and other concerns relevant to the working class are thus central to the kind of liberal democracy I am advocating. These notions capture the essence of what the famous English poet and republican thinker John Milton had in mind when he wrote:

They who are greatest ... are not elevated above their brethren; live soberly in their families, walk the streets as other men, may be spoken to freely, familiarly, friendly, without adoration. My hope is not only to breathe new life into liberalism as a political philosophy but also to influence liberalism in practice-as a political movement. I call the practical implementation of my version of liberalism in the present "working-class liberalism." The emphasis on the working class is rooted in the fact that liberalism's current failures are related to its abandonment of the working class and its priorities. Working-class liberalism has strong communal roots, eschews social engineering, and prioritizes shared prosperity, jobs, and public services. (Throughout, by "liberalism" I refer to the left-leaning version of liberalism-as a political philosophy and a set of ideas-as it came to be developed and practiced. When I wish to speak of my version of liberalism, I specify this explicitly, or sometimes, with a slight abuse of terminology, I refer to my version simply as working-class liberalism.) I'm not proposing working-class liberalism as a concession that liberal democracy has to make in order to avoid backlash or generate buy-in. As I will explain, liberalism will be stronger and truer to its core justifications when it takes on this working-class character. Likewise, prioritizing working-class and communitarian sensibilities without liberalism wouldn't be an equally attractive philosophy. This is because there is a delicate relationship between community and freedom. Community is a natural seat of self-government and sharing of information. But, as I explain in greater detail later, it can also prevent the freedom of its members and abridge their ability to govern their own lives. The virtue of working-class liberalism is to fuse basic liberal values with working-class priorities, so that communities do not become stultifying cages that are detrimental to (some of) their members, and do not ultimately end up supporting political projects that are inimical to working-class interests.

The Call of Liberalism When liberalism fails, illiberal rivals rush in. We are witnessing increasingly bold attacks from illiberal movements against the liberal establishment and against the soft underbelly of liberal democracy. In many countries, including the United States, these attacks have succeeded, at least to a certain extent, and antiliberal forces are ascendant and are clamping down on speech, trampling on the rule of law, dismantling safeguards against abuse of power, and reintroducing myriad hierarchies and inequities. The threat of-or in fact actual-violence against liberals, minorities, and the disempowered may be just around the corner. In this book, I focus on the crisis of liberalism-rather than the sordid ideas and strategies of antiliberal forces-because I believe that these noxious currents are alive only as a result of liberalism's crisis and weakness. It isn't enough to criticize antiliberal alternatives. We have to focus on liberalism's failures, even when alternatives are much worse, so that we can remake liberalism as a political philosophy and as a set of institutions and practices encapsulating that philosophy. The path ahead is not easy. Forging working-class liberalism is a tall order in the best of times and much more challenging in today's polarized environment with little trust in institutions and elites. Still, we have no alternative but to try. If liberalism does not remake itself, it will be antiliberal, antidemocratic alternatives, such as new oligarchies, authoritarianism, ethnonationalism, deep-rooted hierarchy, or renewed extreme progressive movements, that will win out.

The Plan for the Rest of the Book In a nutshell, this book has two core arguments. The first is about the virtues of (my kind of) liberalism, which I derive from the social desirability of and need for experimentation and collective knowledge. The second concerns why shared prosperity and the successes of liberal democratic governance became a reality over the last two centuries and especially after World War II, and why they came to an end in the postindustrial era. These two arguments are interrelated: Defending liberalism is particularly important because, while liberalism lost its way in the context of postindustrial politics, it can still be rebuilt and majorly contribute to the social good. In the next chapter, I develop my theory of liberalism-the book's first core. I provide the historical and empirical justification for the four premises listed previously and then delve deeper into the inevitable tensions between some of these premises and the trade-offs that they produce. In chapter 3, I argue that, even though community is now viewed as a rival to liberalism, notions of community were critical to early liberal thought. I explain why these ideas were then abandoned as reasoning based on "general will" and "social contracts," derived from Jean-Jacques Rousseau's philosophy, became dominant on the Left and classical liberalism became dominant on the Right. I also discuss how a justification for a "social compact" supporting my version of liberalism can be built on deliberative foundations starting from communities. Chapter 4 recounts liberalism's successes. These are both political and economic. We owe the rise of limited government to liberal values. The type of economic growth and effective public service provision that many countries experienced in the decades following World War II were the fruits of liberalism. I provide both an overview of comparative evidence, assessing how countries that adopted liberal democratic institutions have fared in terms of economic growth, education, and health, and a more detailed discussion of the economic and social consequences of liberal ideas in the Swedish and Norwegian experiences. Additionally, I explain how well-designed regulations and government actions aimed at protecting weaker and discriminated-against members of society have achieved their objectives, including in the United States. Chapter 5 turns to the always-simmering dilemmas of liberalism: inclusion, ignorance, and intolerance. I argue that there are no ready- made solutions to these dilemmas, and they are inherently about trade-offs between freedoms and claims to self-government. Nevertheless, I also point out that certain conceptions of liberalism that intermix ideas from Rousseau and his followers were bound to develop the wrong approaches. I then spell out some ways in which the theory articulated in chapter 2 can help us identify the trade-offs and formulate practical responses. Chapters 6 through 8 develop the second core of the book: liberalism's failure to adjust to being in power. Chapter 6 explains how the economics of the industrial era transformed after the arrival of digital technologies. Chapter 7 explains how, together with postindustrial economics, we have also seen the emergence of a postindustrial politics in which the college educated have become dominant and separated from the rest of society, sowing the seeds of the deeper crisis of liberal democracy. Chapter 8 focuses on social engineering. I argue that social engineering is part of almost every political regime and it typically creates greater social power for the more educated members of a society, who then tend to become the drivers of further social engineering. The prospect of greater social power, along with the belief in their own enlightenment, is one of the reasons why a strong dynamic toward social engineering can develop and become amplified when educated segments of society are sufficiently influential. I use this framework to interpret postindustrial social engineering efforts and the backlash they prompted. Chapter 9 asks whether the liberalism I'm advocating is right for the age of algorithms. I argue that the failures of liberalism cannot be blamed on social media, as they ran deeper and started well before the arrival of Facebook and Twitter. Nevertheless, social media amplifies these problems by making it difficult to forge communities that foster trust within and between them. There is therefore a strong case for regulating social media. (I discuss how this can be done in a way that is consistent with freedom of expression in chapter 10.) Al's implications are more complex. First, if AI will soon reach superintelligence and become capable of doing everything humans can do, then there may be no point to human collective knowledge. I argue such superintelligence is neither realistic nor likely within the foreseeable future, and even if powerful AI develops, this will not obviate the need for human adaptation and social learning. Second, I explain how AI could damage experimentation, communication, and the accumulation of useful collective knowledge. On this basis, I suggest there may be strong reasons for regulating AI in a liberal society. Finally, I discuss how AI, if steered correctly, can be complementary to the expansion of collective knowledge and the rebuilding of shared prosperity. Chapter 10 describes how a liberalism without social engineering, with communal foundations, and based on a strong commitment to shared prosperity-a working-class liberalism-can be rebuilt. This renewed liberalism must enshrine a true appreciation of the fallibility of all human endeavors, the value of freedom of expression and choice, the central roles of shared economic growth and jobs in creating effective freedoms, and a robust emphasis on community and self-government. Finally, a word about how this book might be read. The current structure reflects my view of the most complete story of liberal successes and failures, and how to reimagine and remake liberalism based on a coherent set of ideas about the common good. Those who prefer to focus on the successes and failures, without delving into the justification of my version of liberalism, may wish to skip chapters 2 and 3 and go straight to chapter 4. Those who are mainly concerned about the current crisis may even jump to chapter 6 and consult chapters 2 through 5 later as a guide to some of the ideas that underpin my recommendations and how these ideas have played an important role in liberal success in the past.

Chapter 2

Why Liberalism?

Lady Montagu's discovery of Ottoman inoculation reveals how community generates collective knowledge. Yet communities also enforce restrictive norms.

▼

Why Liberalism?

The grand, leading principle, towards which every argument unfolded in these pages directly converges, is the absolute and essential importance of human development in its richest diversity. -Wilhelm von Humboldt

It is that the case for individual freedom rests chiefly on the recognition of the inevitable ignorance of all of us concerning a great many of the factors on which the achievement of our ends and welfare depends. -Friedrich Hayek

Lady Mary Wortley Montagu was no stranger to the speckled monster, as smallpox was known at the time. Just two years prior, it had swept in and stolen the life of her brother. As she lay in agony, beleaguered by pustules and high fevers leading to delirium, it was clear that the monster had come after her as well. Lady Montagu survived. Though pitted scars now marred her fair complexion, she was one of the lucky ones. As the premier killer of the premodern era, smallpox proved deadly for one in four people who contracted it. During the eighteenth century, it killed an estimated 400,000 every year in Europe, and accounted for 20 percent of all deaths in some areas. As smallpox was fiercely contagious, deaths were poised to increase exponentially as the world's population grew. When Spanish conquistadores brought the disease to the Americas, the speckled monster wreaked even greater havoc. Without acquired immunity, over 90 percent of the Native population may have been wiped out by smallpox. Populations that survived wore the disease's scars like Lady Montagu. So imagine Lady Montagu's surprise when she came across thousands of healthy people bearing no scars from the pox. She was observing the locals in Constantinople, the capital of the Ottoman Empire, where she had accompanied her husband, Edward Wortley Montagu, upon his appointment as British ambassador. She summarized her amazement in a 1717 letter:

I am going to tell you a thing, that will make you wish yourself here. The small-pox, so fatal, and so general amongst us, is here entirely harmless .... There is a set of old women, who make it their business to perform the operation, every autumn, in the month of September, when the great heat is abated.

The "great operation" that Lady Montagu observed was a primitive form of inoculation, often performed by Armenian and Greek women with no medical training. They used a large needle to make an incision in a vein and then poured in smallpox-infected pus.
