This was collective knowledge in action, cumulated over decades with the experience, wisdom, and experimentation of hundreds (perhaps thousands) of people, not just in Constantinople but throughout the Near East. Lady Montagu's genius was in observing and recognizing the value of this collective knowledge. Her breakthrough was one of social adaptation: learning from others and applying it to get her son and daughter inoculated. Inoculation wasn't a scientific breakthrough propelled by the newfound Enlightenment energy in Europe. In fact, it was neither a European breakthrough nor accepted by the European enlightened elite. The medical profession back in England largely rejected the idea, arguing that there couldn't possibly be anything to learn from primitive Muslims in the Ottoman lands, from a practice performed by "a few ignorant women," or from a woman with no training in medicine, even if she was nobility. It would take eighty more years for Edward Jenner to come around and convince the medical profession, by using the less dangerous cowpox, and for this practice to be christened "vaccination" (from the Latin word for cowpox pustules, "vaccinae"). This episode captures the variegated forms that useful information takes and how it is acquired, shared, and sometimes resisted. Recognizing this is a crucial step toward understanding what the right kind of liberalism brings to the table. In this chapter, I start with the relationship between community and freedom, which clarifies both the role of community in building and disseminating collective knowledge, and the obstacles it creates for freedom. This forms the basis for the four premises I introduce and defend, and the four implications-"pillars of liberalism"-I draw from these premises about how society should be organized.

Community and Freedom The story of inoculation provides a compelling example of the role of community. Community refers to groups of people who share trust and mutual regard, building on common historical, religious, ethnic, social, or work-related links, or because they have come together in associations based on shared aspirations and interests. In Tocqueville's imagery, communities are groups that share "habits of the heart," enabling them to communicate better and to build understanding and unspoken bonds. The information Lady Montagu gleaned was generated and propagated over time by a community. Yet this same community in the Ottoman Empire steadfastly opposed the dissemination of other valuable knowledge and the adoption of useful technologies such as the printing press. The European scientists who rejected the idea of inoculation had, at other times, been at the forefront of phenomenal advances in science and technology. We all belong to multiple, overlapping communities and are defined both by our individuality and by the norms, information, and values of our communities. Liberalism, especially any liberalism that focuses on the building of useful collective knowledge, must grapple with the role of community-as a source of information exchange, as an influence on our objectives and priorities, and sometimes as a formidable barrier to individual freedoms and to new knowledge.

Humans are nothing if not adaptable. Our ancestors endured murder, war, servitude, and the tyranny of kings, states, and customs. Many people still do. But we are also capable of building something better: communities with greater security, no servitude, broad freedoms in private life, and the potential for regular people to shape their public world. One of the most influential philosophers of the modern age, Thomas Hobbes, was among the first to raise such questions, articulating the notion of a "social contract," by which legitimate government emerges from the implicit agreement of the governed. Hobbes thought this could justify monarchy and authoritarianism, because the alternative was "continual fear, and danger of violent death; and the life of man, solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short." Anthropologists have documented plenty of cases for which even this description appears too sanitized. Small-scale societies that dominated Earth before the rise of powerful chiefdoms and states were often locked in incessant warfare between groups, and sometimes within groups. By some estimates, five people per thousand died by violence in these early societies, meaning the likelihood that a person would reach the age of fifty without meeting a violent end was only about three in four. It is impossible to expect people in such situations to enjoy any liberties. How can you be free when your life is in constant danger? How can you be free if you don't happen to be one of the strongest or most lethal members of your community and are subject to the whim or mercy of those who can do you harm? A debate still rages within anthropology about whether such violence was universal or characterized only some societies in certain epochs. Growing evidence suggests that several societies were more peaceful, although violence was considerably more common than peace in the premodern era. There is even uncertainty about how much of this violence took place through intergroup warfare. In several societies, such as the Yanomami in the Amazon basin between Brazil and Venezuela and the Gebusi in Papua New Guinea, intragroup murder rates were very high, because even routine conflicts could get out of hand. One thing is clear: People did not like such situations and found means of limiting violence. One way of doing so is to leverage community. Tight-knit communities, governed by strong norms and traditions, reduce violence by establishing procedures for conflict resolution. Consider sexual relations, which are a source of constant tensions. When sexual relationships are regulated by tradition, some conflicts can be avoided, and when conflict does arise, clear rules for punishing transgressions may make it more likely that it will be defused before spiraling into massive violence. Anthropologists encountering some African societies without any state institutions were at first surprised that these societies were able to ensure reasonably peaceful conditions in their members' daily lives. Much of this was because of the norms and traditions that they had developed over centuries. Norms can help prevent violence and bring a measure of predictability to life. But as James Robinson and I argued in The Narrow Corridor, societies that rely heavily on norms are apt to form a "cage of norms." The same traditions that prevent conflict also restrict people's choices and even thoughts. For example, reducing conflict by constraining the boundaries of sexual relationships clearly limits sexual freedoms. Communities enforce these norms by tradition (causing people to internalize them), peer pressure, the threat of ostracism, and sometimes violence for those who stray too far from them. Communities are a natural nexus of norm enforcement, because once people abide by certain norms, they have a desire to compel others to follow suit. Whatever the exact mechanism, freedom is the first casualty. In many small societies, the cage of norms ruled out the possibility of new economic activities. In nearly all of them, it established a rigid hierarchy-with elders or ruling chiefs and their families wielding more influence than the rest. The majority of these societies put women in a dependent position, with far fewer freedoms than men. Moreover, the threat of violence was never far away even when such norms were paramount. A set of norms called Pashtunwali still guides the lives of Pashtun tribes that occupy much of present-day Afghanistan and parts of Pakistan. A key code is "A Pukhtoon believes and acts in accordance with the principles of ... an eye for eye, tooth for tooth, and blood for blood. He wipes out insult with insult regardless of cost or consequence and vindicates his honour by wiping out disgrace with suitable action." In the context of the harsh conditions in which Pashtun tribes live-and the many everyday activities, like herding, that can easily spark conflict- Pashtunwali plays a key role in averting many disputes. Yet when conflict does break out, the principle of blood for blood often prevails, with dire consequences. The cage of norms is the price that many societies pay for regulating the myriad social interactions within a community that produce tension. The cage highlights the multifaceted nature of communities: The very thing that makes communities support mutual understanding and form stable norms also makes them barriers to individual freedom and choice. What's worse, such norms can be imposed on others who do not want them, do not benefit from them, or are even subjugated by them. The cage of norms was propagated and pressed on others as militarily successful groups expanded and conquered new territories, like the Indo- Aryans over a millennium before the Common Era, Muslim Arabs starting in the seventh century, and Pashtuns beginning in the fourteenth century. The spread of these norms crystallized, and sometimes intensified, rigid hierarchies-arguably, none more enduring than the caste system in the Indian subcontinent. Once states and state-like institutions emerged, human societies developed a different way of dealing with conflicts: third-party enforcement, backed up by the power and functionaries of the state. States had laws and these laws could be enforced. Today, if you cheat your business partner, it triggers not a blood feud but a court proceeding, and the rules are clear (or at least are supposed to be). The state's power ensures restitution if you're found guilty in court. You have to either pay up or serve time. In practice, many of these early states were highly despotic. Their laws were made by and for a narrow group of elites-members of the royal family, influential religious figures, large landowners, or feudal overlords. There was no equality before the law. Beyond this bias toward those with greater political power or social status, the primitive communication, transport, and information technologies of these states meant that laws were applied haphazardly and arbitrarily. All of this spelled little freedom for individuals across the globe. So perhaps we should accept the lack of freedom-freedoms of expression, religion, choice, and economic activities-as the default human condition. Yet the foundational ideas of liberalism maintain that we can do better. Liberalism rejects fatalism in favor of a belief in the possibility-but not the inevitability-of progress. Liberty is valuable and within reach. I develop this point by offering theoretical foundations for a new type of liberalism (as a political philosophy) and liberal democracy (as the institutional counterpart of that philosophy). I start with four premises that echo the themes in Lady Montagu's story and that are empirically or normatively appealing. Here I use the term "normatively appealing" to capture broad value judgments that most people would agree with, such as the claim that having greater material resources or improved health for everybody is better than not. The premises I propose are not entirely new. Some liberal thinkers have started from similar ones, and my account has some parallels with-but also important differences from-the arguments of the American mathematician and philosopher Charles Sanders Peirce and the Austrian British philosopher Karl Popper. However, the version of liberalism that emerges is different from what has become the established understanding of liberalism. In fact, my version stretches the big tent of liberalism not just by focusing on useful collective knowledge but also by centrally incorporating and confronting the role of community. I first introduce and defend these four premises as being either normatively self-evident or strongly supported by our current understanding of scientific, historical, and sociological facts. From these premises I derive four pillars that together define the kind of liberalism that I am defending. I started the discussion with community and freedom, because we will see the multifaceted relations between these two concepts in the premises and pillars of liberalism-and in the trade-offs liberal democracy faces. Liberalism, even though it starts with and celebrates individual rights and freedoms, cannot ignore the communal roots of individual behavior and values, and the role of community in building trust and reliable channels of information exchange.

Premise I: Fallibility Every belief, proposition, or piece of knowledge we hold could be wrong, even spectacularly wrong. This humility should be the starting point for any discussion of knowledge, given the tendency of human communities to hold beliefs and follow practices that later turn out to be only partially true, incorrect, or even downright misguided. Fallibility was a core belief of many early proponents of liberalism. John Stuart Mill wrote in the beginning of On Liberty in 1859:

Yet it is as evident in itself, as any amount of argument can make it, that ages are no more infallible than individuals; every age having held many opinions which subsequent ages have deemed not only false but absurd.

Two decades later, Charles Sanders Peirce built his own approach on the claim that "we cannot in any way reach perfect certitude or exactitude. We can never be absolutely sure of anything." This is the essence of his notion of "fallibilism"-the idea that knowledge is probabilistic, meaning that no theory or hypothesis can be known for certain. (In practice, we can make exceptions for certain mathematical laws, such as 2 + 2 = 4 in the decimal numeral system.) Friedrich Hayek emphasized the same point in his most important work, The Constitution of Liberty, published in 1960. He recognized that it was highly uncertain and, for all practical purposes, unknowable in advance how new knowledge would be created. He wrote: "Who will prove to possess the right combination of aptitudes and opportunities to find the better way is just as little predictable as by what manner or process different kinds of its knowledge and skill will combine to bring about a solution of the problem." This perspective is amply supported by the fact that many strong consensuses have risen and fallen over time. Beliefs that Earth was flat were widely held in ancient Greece, even though some of the central figures of Greek science, such as Pythagoras, Plato, and Aristotle, had already started piling up evidence against this belief. Eventually, the Greeks did accept that Earth was spherical. But flat-Earth theories persisted in many other parts of the world, including the Norse civilization in Europe, as well as in China and the Middle East. The geocentric model of astronomy-placing Earth at the center of the universe, with the sun orbiting around it-had even greater staying power. Aristotle placed the spherical Earth at the center of the universe, and his system was perfected by Claudius Ptolemy, producing the Ptolemaic model, which ruled supreme for almost fifteen centuries. When facts that contradicted the model were discovered, "epicycles"-ad hoc extensions or fixes-were added to make the theory fit them. Nicolaus Copernicus's scientific breakthroughs did not change the geocentric consensus and remained under constant attack by religious authorities. His masterpiece, On the Revolutions of the Celestial Spheres, could be published only shortly before his death in 1543. Galileo Galilei, who built on Copernican advances, faced even more severe persecution from the church and was forced to recant some of his findings. His Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems, which displayed the superiority of the Copernican system, as well as other books about the heliocentric model, were banned by the church. Dialogue remained a forbidden book by the Catholic Church until 1835. Charles Darwin's theory of natural selection similarly had to contend against well-established beliefs within the scientific community and broader society. Darwin himself was tormented by what he expected to be the reaction to his theory of evolution, and significantly delayed the publication of his findings. He sprang into action and released On the Origin of Species only after Alfred Russel Wallace presented a paper about his own similar theory of evolution, which he had formulated independently from Darwin's. In each case, widely accepted beliefs were wrong, and alternatives remained either unknown or unarticulated. Or when they were proposed, they could not get a toehold, and advocating them led to ridicule, ostracism, and even death threats. Incorrect consensuses around political and social matters are equally common. Early seventeenth-century Britain agreed that there was a divinely ordained way of organizing society, with a monarch at the helm, reigning over the nation just as a patriarch would rule over his family. A leading philosopher of the era summarized this view: "For as kingly power is by the law of God, so it hath no inferior law to limit it." He added, "It is unnatural for the people to govern or choose governors." The idea that people could have a say in how they are governed did not occur to many thinkers at the time. Advocating for this view could get you sent to the dungeons. It took the philosophies of Thomas Hobbes and John Locke, the English Civil War, and the ideas and experiences of radical groups that emerged during that war, such as the Levellers, Diggers, and Seekers, to cement the notion that sovereignty rested with the people. It was only after the establishment of constitutional monarchy following another civil war and the Glorious Revolution of 1688-89 that this idea started becoming second nature to British people. Building consensus around incorrect beliefs and shutting off alternatives is not confined to distant history, either. Alfred Wegener's theory of continental drift and plate tectonics, first published in 1912, was initially ridiculed and rejected by the scientific community. It was hazardous for your career to work on this theory until the 1950s and 1960s, when it started becoming accepted. Neural networks and deep-learning methods that have become the engine of recent advances in artificial intelligence were viewed as a dead end in the 1990s and 2000s, and it was difficult or impossible for scholars working on these topics to be published or get prestigious jobs. Few computer scientists and mathematicians would take them seriously at the time. Geoffrey Hinton was one of the stubborn researchers who stuck with it despite this. Hinton is now recognized as the grandfather of artificial intelligence and received the Nobel Prize in 2024. It took several algorithmic breakthroughs, far greater computational capacity, and a revolution in the availability of data for deep-learning methods to become the foundation of the AI revolution. In 2005, Katalin Karikó and Drew Weissman discovered how to modify mRNA (messenger RNA) so it wouldn't trigger an immune response. It was a foundational step toward mRNA vaccines, which have now saved millions of lives. The scientific establishment barely noticed. Karikó and Weissman couldn't get published in leading journals, and Karikó couldn't secure a permanent position or funding for her work. The significance of their discoveries was understood fifteen years later, after their modification became the centerpiece of the mRNA vaccines against COVID. The scientific method provides us with the means to test hypotheses and claims. But these are never perfect. Science also highlights that no amount of consensus is a guarantee that we know the truth. Just as Peirce emphasized, we can never be certain of any scientific fact or hypothesis, and new evidence could turn today's consensuses into tomorrow's disgraced theories. We need new ideas, more testing, and novel breakthroughs either to increase our confidence in the currently held consensuses or to reject them wholesale and kick-start new inquiries. Recognition of fallibility is the essence of scientific knowledge. Fallibility is even more crucial to "social knowledge," or our collective understanding of how to adapt to our ever-changing environment. This type of knowledge is no less central to our communities and welfare but is more difficult to acquire, and false agreements are more likely. As previously hidden sexual practices gained visibility in the twentieth century, many experts and laypeople alike initially regarded homosexuality as a disorder, advocating for its suppression or outright ban. The possibility that gay and lesbian people could live openly did not seem feasible or desirable to many. This consensus led to antigay norms and practices, with many experts and family members lining up to "cure" homosexuality. The consensus showed a lot of staying power, because it was difficult to articulate alternatives when laws and norms were unified in imposing this perspective, and there were no opportunities for gay couples to live freely or for gay communities to try arrangements that could produce new social knowledge. Building social knowledge is also difficult because the world continues to change around us. Hayek anticipated this when he argued that constantly evolving conditions will generate new things for us to learn socially: "Every change in conditions will make necessary some change in the use of resources, in the direction and kind of human activities, in habits and practices." Take the problem of social media. Even if people who lived in the late 1990s had had perfect wisdom about the problems they had experienced, they wouldn't have known how to respond to the new reality created by social media. They wouldn't have understood what it would imply for social dynamics and cohesion when so many people communicate via social media, get their facts from social media, and replace their in-person social networks with virtual networks organized over social media. Even after two decades of intense social media activity, we don't know the answers to central questions: How should we govern it? Should youngsters be discouraged from spending hours on TikTok and Instagram? Should we be worried about the growing association between social media use and mental health problems? Should we ban smartphones from elementary and middle schools so that children can learn in a social media-free environment? Answers will have to depend on extensive experience with social media and broad experimentation, at both the individual and the collective levels. There is likely no one-size-fits-all solution. Furthermore, the likelihood of collectively landing on some imperfect adaptation is high, partly because experimentation is curtailed by the unwillingness of individuals to cut themselves off from social media when their friends are hooked on it.

Premise II: Social Learning in Community A defining feature of human societies is the sharing of knowledge. If we do not share knowledge, and if we do not learn from others' better practices, then our ability to adapt to our circumstances and improve our conditions will be profoundly inhibited. This observation has major implications for the nature of liberalism, because without the trust and norms of communities, humans will not be able to share and benefit much from useful knowledge. Human institutions don't run on laws alone-they depend on shared understanding. That understanding doesn't appear out of thin air; it's built through social learning. When we talk about "common knowledge," we mean something deeper than just facts. We mean a collective sense of what's acceptable, expected, or off-limits. Norms-the often-invisible rules that shape behavior-depend on it. So do our institutions, from traffic laws to tax codes. Without shared understanding, neither norms nor laws can stand. Humans are consummate social learners. Children as young as three or four are already keen imitators, and imitation becomes a source of important knowledge about how to cope with the natural world and social life. Young children, chimpanzees, and orangutans have similar skills when dealing with problems that involve understanding causal relationships between inanimate objects (for example, how to perform tasks that will lead to rewards, such as snacks). But human children are off the charts when it comes to social relations. They are much more capable imitators and understand social relationships far better. Social learning is in the human genome, it appears. Learning requires community. All apes bond socially and learn from members of their group. But social learning is much more multifaceted and important for humans. People learn norms, facts, and interpretations from those they trust and those to whom they assign high prestige or social status. Learning is a social activity and cannot be uprooted from its communal foundations. This, too, is obvious from young children, who are keen observers of social status and turn their attention to individuals with high status as the targets of their learning. The role of community in social learning is also confirmed by recent research, which shows that when the local farming environment creates more room for social learning (because soil conditions are more similar across plots), close-knit communities are more likely to form. In the nineteenth-century United States, greater local soil homogeneity led to greater intracommunity marriage rates, more similar naming patterns across families, and less variation in religious and cultural practices. Hayek also emphasized the centrality of social learning when he wrote:

The successful combination of knowledge and aptitude is ... the product of individuals imitating those who have been more successful and from their being guided by signs or symbols, such as prices offered for their products or expressions of moral and aesthetic esteem for their having observed standards of conduct-in short, of their using the results of the experiences of others.

Note his emphasis on prices. Markets are hotbeds of social learning, as entrepreneurs develop new products and techniques, workers explore different occupations, and consumers discover their own tastes by trying various goods and services. One might think of the market as an impersonal place-almost the opposite of community. That would be wrong. Markets are networks of people bound together, where familiarity and trust matter greatly. Would you spend your money if you suspected that you wouldn't get the goods that were promised to you? Would you work long hours if you did not trust that your employer would pay you? The perspective that markets are like communities becomes even more plausible once you realize that markets themselves depend on myriad nonmarket transactions-between long-term employees and employers, between companies producing specialized inputs and their customers, and between members of communities where employment relations are often embedded. Markets are central to expanding collective knowledge because they provide both incentives for trying new things and the means of sharing information. Entrepreneurs are incentivized to try new methods of production thanks to markets that promise the prospect of profits from their ideas. Discoveries spread via the market as other firms replicate the success of entrepreneurs with new production methods. Prices also transmit information. Even more fundamentally, markets are themselves conduits for new things: There is no way of learning whether there is demand for a new good or service without the market. After the invention of the printing press, it became evident that some people needed lenses to improve their eyesight in order to read. Once the demand for such lenses was revealed, the market process led to important discoveries in optics, which then opened new markets for more powerful optical instruments. Markets are not the only conduits for groups of people to try new things. Each community can deal with the challenges it faces in a different way, leveraging its distinct norms and values and making different choices. For example, each school could make its own decision on whether smartphones and social media should be allowed during school hours or how social distancing should work during a pandemic. Other communities and society at large can then learn from these diverse experiences. One place where we can see the role of community in cementing successful adaptation is in small-scale societies. When Europeans first started studying the Indigenous communities of the Yucatán, they noticed a curious custom: a specific way in which the tropical tuber plant cassava (also known as manioc) was prepared. It had to be peeled first and then soaked for a long time before cooking. The water in which the plant was cooked had to be disposed of, and then the food preparation continued. These seemed like primitive, religious customs. Researchers later discovered these were indispensable ways of dealing with two highly poisonous, cyanide-producing sugars that cassava contained. If cassava is not properly processed, those who eat it can suffer from severe cyanide intoxication and even death. Thanks to social learning spanning generations, the Indigenous people knew how to deal with it and avoid such poisoning. Or consider the radioactive cloud created by the 1986 Chernobyl nuclear plant accident, which extended all the way to the United Kingdom. Elevated levels of radiocaesium (caesium-137, which is radioactive and dangerous) were detected in sheep. Authorities responded with a ban on slaughtering the sheep for meat, expecting the contamination to be short-lived. Sheep farmers objected, arguing that the contamination would last much longer. Their views were dismissed by experts. But it was the sheep farmers who turned out to be right, because their accumulated experiential knowledge meant they understood that the local soil propagated the caesium and made its effects much more long-lasting. Community is also critical for local knowledge, which varies from region to region and setting to setting. For example, the working conditions of different jobs will have a major local component, and often the most reliable source of this information will be the experiences of others in the community. The groups within which learning and information exchange happen can be more expansive, spanning many distinct communities or the entire society-otherwise, useful information would remain local. Specific communities coming together with shared understanding and some common identity at a higher (for example, national) level is critical for information to be shared broadly (and for compromises to be reached at the national level). Community is relevant for the acceptance and propagation of scientific knowledge as well. The big scientific breakthroughs of the Enlightenment leveraged the informal network that many scientists and philosophers formed throughout Europe. This network came to be referred to as the "Republic of Letters" (or "Commonwealth of Learning"), because its members communicated their findings and conjectures via beautifully written letters. It was the trust and open exchange of ideas across this far-flung community-ranging from London to Kraków- that fueled the era's early scientific breakthroughs, as its members built on one another's insights and dared to test new ideas together. Nevertheless, as Lady Montagu's experience with the scientific community of her time illustrates and the notion of the cage of norms underscores, all types of communities have mixed effects on social learning. While they can be an indispensable conduit for sharing, trusting, and using knowledge, community norms can also be a formidable barrier to the acquisition or deployment of knowledge. This is why liberalism must grapple with the role of community and navigate carefully the trade-offs between individual liberty and collective customs. It must also recognize that liberty can be realized only if it is embedded in community, or else communal cages of norms will stifle it.

Premise III: Gentle Epistemic Improvement Incorrect beliefs can spread and may sometimes become dominant. Nevertheless, there is a (sometimes weak) tendency for incorrect beliefs to fade over time-often under the influence of what David Hume called a "gentle force." This is especially the case when there is room for debate and open exchange of information and when the cage of norms is not too suffocating-or when there is an effective marketplace for ideas where different ideas can compete fairly. Incorrect beliefs either have logical flaws or contradict observed empirical patterns, and these contradictions open the door for skeptics to challenge the system and campaign for alternatives. This is a slow, laborious, and hazardous process. As a quotation attributed to Francis Bacon stresses, "truth is rightly named the daughter of Time, not of Authority." The flat-Earth theory and the geocentric model of astronomy were no fleeting matters, but they eventually subsided. People recognized that Earth couldn't be flat, as they could see the bending of the horizon from the fact that faraway ships' hulls disappeared before their masts, and they could observe Earth's shadow on the moon during lunar eclipses. A better understanding of planetary motion indicated that our planet wasn't the center of the universe, either. In both cases, prevailing theories were challenged by new facts, aided by better instruments to observe the skies. The understanding of infectious diseases developed because experimental methods confirmed the basic precepts of the germ theory of disease, and there was a complete acceptance of the theory when it was successfully applied to problems of public health. This, too, was a very long process. When first proposed in the 1910s and '20s, the idea of plate tectonics was widely mocked. But science marched on, and as evidence of continental drift mounted, the academic consensus eventually reversed itself. Today, our understanding of geology and assessment of earthquake risks completely depend on plate tectonics. These corrections took time, and proponents of wrong beliefs sometimes pushed hard. The resistance of entrenched theories inspired one of the most influential ideas in the philosophy of science. Thomas Kuhn argued that scientific progress happens not through steady accumulation of knowledge but by "revolutions"-abrupt shifts that occur when mounting evidence undermines the prevailing paradigm and a new generation of scientists begins to break away. As Max Planck put it, "A new scientific truth does not triumph by convincing its opponents and making them see the light, but rather because its opponents eventually die." Or, as it became formulated a little more vividly, "Science progresses one funeral at a time." But science does progress, even if slowly. As impressive as our scientific progress is our ability to acquire social knowledge, adapt to new environments, and produce new norms and institutions. Imagine how unthinkable it would have been to many in the early seventeenth century that a society could be organized differently from the hierarchical structure to which they were accustomed, with authority emanating from rulers and elites. These preconceptions did not disappear even after radical groups within Cromwell's New Model Army in the English Civil War, such as the Levellers, articulated notions of popular participation in politics and demonstrated the possibility of more bottom-up organizations. But by 1688, the idea of popular sovereignty had become widespread within England, and it was one of the main drivers of the establishment of a constitutional monarchy after James II was deposed in the next civil war. Improvements in social knowledge may be even harder than advances in science. Nevertheless, the tremendous changes in our social arrangements and views over the last two hundred years-toward greater democratic participation, freedom, and tolerance in many parts of the world-suggest they are possible, even if slow and prone to reversals. Community and science play important roles in the gentle process of improvement of our knowledge. Communities that are open to new ideas and have built trust among their members-just like the Republic of Letters-can more securely share information and more easily navigate inevitable conflicts. This facilitates the replacement of existing theories and beliefs with new ones. Without communication and trust, social learning becomes difficult and confrontational. The scientific method is vital as well. Once the scientific approach is accepted, recognizing and eliminating incorrect beliefs becomes easier. Community and science are sometimes at loggerheads-for example, when the Catholic community opposed the heliocentric model. But they can be synergistic, too. Even with the best tools, science would not be able to convince people of the fallacy of certain arguments if people did not trust scientists. When science is embedded in the community, such trust is more likely to be forthcoming. During COVID, for example, vaccination rates were higher in communities that had their own trusted primary care physicians. Parenthetically, the role of science in supporting learning from empirical observations is one of the reasons why the "postmodern" outlook, which started with French thinkers such as Jacques Derrida and Michel Foucault and then spread to corners of American academia, is so damaging to science and indirectly to liberty. My justification for liberalism wouldn't work if we pushed fallibility too far and denied the possibility that we can come closer to truth via empiricism and scientific inquiry. It also wouldn't work if people blindly followed authority. Liberalism requires us to find the happy medium between skepticism and credulity of authority.

Premise IV: Desirability of Collective Knowledge Knowledge, especially when shared and collectively held, is essential to human flourishing-as Humboldt emphasizes in the quotation at the start of this chapter, which was relayed by Mill in the epigraph to On Liberty. Knowledge is not an end in itself. It is an instrument for achieving society's diverse objectives. Collective knowledge can enable a society to reach greater material comfort, both by improving its capacity to produce goods and services and by facilitating adaptation to its changing circumstances. Social knowledge is also desirable because it allows the building of better norms and institutions-for more peaceful coexistence of peoples and communities and for ensuring that other types of collective knowledge are put to good use. It is thanks to our collective knowledge that we are so much healthier than our ancestors. Today, citizens of all industrialized nations, except the U.S., have a life expectancy at birth of over eighty (compared to less than thirty in Britain in the nineteenth century, as we have seen). Even in the poorest continent, Africa, the average life expectancy at birth is now sixty-three years, despite lack of clean water in some places and endemic shortages of health care facilities and hospitals across much of the continent. This is because scientists and doctors now understand how infectious diseases work. We have access to antibiotics that can prevent deadly infections. We have developed public health methods that produce and distribute vaccinations, offer guidance on avoiding dangerous or unhealthy substances, and ensure sanitary conditions. The last examples reiterate that by "knowledge" I do not just mean scientific information that can be used for making better tools and products. Understanding that you should wash your hands, or that sewage and drinking water shouldn't mix, is useful collective knowledge. So is recognizing that people can participate in politics and do not have to be subject to the will of an all-powerful monarch or ironfisted general. How to navigate social relations in increasingly complex human communities is also very useful collective knowledge. My expansive definition of knowledge follows Hayek, who stressed, "The growth of knowledge and the growth of civilization are the same only if we interpret knowledge to include all the human adaptations to environment in which past experience has been incorporated." Instrumental useful knowledge-deployed for achieving various objectives-must thus include ways of dealing with our constantly evolving social life and learning how to interact with institutions and technologies. A lot of this information is tacit, concerns our social relations, and is context-specific. That makes it not less but more important. An illustration of this type of collective knowledge is the polder model, which describes Dutch society in the Middle Ages. The Netherlands became remarkably prosperous, with low levels of inequality, strong cooperative norms supported by civic participation in decision-making, and a political culture that sought broad consensus for collective decisions. These were features rarely observed in other European societies during the Middle Ages. Several economic historians have argued that they were particularly well suited to the conditions that the Dutch faced, because their communities and agricultural lands were on polders-low-lying land under constant threat of being overrun by rising tides and overflowing rivers. Investment in public works, especially dikes, was crucial and required all hands on deck. These conditions also created a tendency for the community to agree on the most important challenges to their security and livelihood, laying the groundwork for a more cooperative approach to collective choices. The need to grapple with such threats underpinned the unique norms and institutions of Dutch society. How did the Dutch come up with these adaptations? There were no models to copy. Even if they carefully studied the institutions and norms of France or Germany, they wouldn't have learned the foundations of the polder model, since French or German economic and geographic conditions were entirely different. The only plausible answer is that the Dutch themselves had to experiment and learn from their own experience over time. This learning was no less critical for Dutch society than understanding techniques of production. Yet humans have been known to misuse all sorts of knowledge, both scientific and social. Spectacular advances in the understanding of the atom and atomic forces led to nuclear weapons and ultimately to the two atom bombs, Little Boy and Fat Man, that killed hundreds of thousands in Hiroshima and Nagasaki. Fritz Haber's discovery of how to effectively convert atmospheric nitrogen into ammonia was a turning point in the twentieth century. It enabled the mass production of synthetic fertilizers we now depend on to feed the expanding population of the globe. It also led to the production of more powerful explosives. Related ideas were put to work to create chemical weapons for both Allied and Central Powers in World War I, including by Haber himself. How can we be sure that collective knowledge is good for human objectives, whatever they are? There is no surefire recipe. But we need communities that are cohesive enough-but not too restrictive of their members' behaviors and thoughts-to enable the sharing of that knowledge. The desirability of collective knowledge is also amplified when this knowledge is embedded within an institutional context and society-wide moral principles that ensure it will be used for the common good, not to the detriment of most people. There are two major reasons why collective knowledge may be a mixed blessing for a society. First, it can be used in a manner that benefits only a few people at the expense of many. Second, there may be deep disagreements about which objectives we should try to satisfy with our expanded knowledge. Better institutions help with both of these challenges, because they build new pathways for sharing the gains and for better mediation and compromise between groups that disagree. We can see both roles of institutions in the history of the Industrial Revolution. The breakthroughs in production technology that started in the middle of the eighteenth century in Britain unleashed the Industrial Revolution. As a result, we live richer, healthier, and more comfortable lives than people three hundred years ago. But early industrialization created huge inequalities. Workers in the first decades of the nineteenth century labored very long hours in unhealthy, disciplinarian factories for low wages, even in occupations, such as weaving, that had previously enjoyed high earnings and high status. They could not have imagined that the productivity gains achieved with industrial machinery would result in higher wages. Nearly a century after industrialization began, there was still little evidence of widely shared gains. But as Simon Johnson and I document in Power and Progress, that changed in the middle of the nineteenth century-because workers organized, Britain democratized, and technology began to evolve in a more pro-worker direction. What made this possible was not machinery alone but the institutional context surrounding it-a context capable of reform and responsiveness to social demands. Although Britain was still far from being a full democracy and labor unions remained banned until the 1870s, the country was no longer a monarchy, and ideas of popular participation were already accepted. This made it increasingly difficult to exclude the middle class from the political process and to repress the nascent labor movement-though elites did try. That institutional context was critical for industrialization and technological advances to ultimately become beneficial for most people in the country. (But only in the country, not for most people around the world, some of whom suffered mightily as industrial machinery, new transportation and communication techniques, and better weapons enabled Britain to expand its colonial empire.) The problem of mixed objectives is no less central. We may have better collective knowledge-for example, the knowledge to develop online networks and social media. But how do we use them? Certain communities may prefer to allow unfettered access to social media, including for children, because they believe the benefits of better communication and connection outweigh the costs. Others may worry about the mental health consequences of social media. If society were to adopt a uniform policy, some communities would be better off and some others would suffer. The right institutional structure could help. In this case, communities themselves could be empowered to make their own decisions or find compromises. Community-together with its norms-plays a critical role in defining how societies adapt to and use knowledge. New knowledge can be coupled with a broad commitment to helping the less well-off and with institutions that can steer technology in a more equitable direction and redistribute some of the gains. When this happens, any negative effects of the deployment of collective knowledge are more likely to be contained, and paths for generating better outcomes and shared prosperity from this knowledge are more likely to be built. The right kind of institutional structure is critical for this process. If institutions enable communities to make their own decisions, this can also help build collective knowledge and apply it in ways that are best suited to each community's norms and preferences.

These four premises define my corner of the big tent of liberalism. They inform how we should organize society, and I next summarize their implications, which together make up the pillars of my version of liberalism and the liberal democratic institutions that would put these ideas into practice. In sum, since collective knowledge is essential for human societies to achieve their various objectives, we should seek social arrangements that support such collective knowledge. Fallibility and gentle epistemic improvement mean that this must rely on trying new things-experimentation-with the hope that better ideas will ultimately prevail. The form of desirable social arrangements and institutions follows from this need-so that society gives maximal room and resources for experimentation. To turn collective knowledge into material betterment necessitates a focus on economic growth. Finally, community is a key conduit of sharing knowledge and also enables groups to become part of the process of discovery of new useful knowledge by trying their own adaptations to a world in constant flux.

Pillar I: Vitality of Experimentation The four premises together imply that humans should attempt new things. Moreover, because our environment is constantly changing, we can rest on neither our laurels nor our past experimentation. By "experimentation" I mean the practice of actively trying new ideas and practices, both for a better scientific or technical understanding of the world around us and for better adaptation to our evolving social conditions. In this definition and in my emphasis on experimentation, I'm following Mill's call for "experiments of living," for example when he writes: "As it is useful that while mankind are imperfect there should be different opinions, so it is that there should be different experiments of living." Experimentation in science has a specific meaning, indicating that we are interested in "causal relations"-whether an act is the cause of a particular consequence. For example, if a virus is the cause of a disease, then without the virus, the disease would not have happened. The experimentation I am referring to here is broader, including working out how to adapt to social changes and discovering new products and production techniques. New things may be simply thoughts-some of us can contemplate avenues that others will not consider or will view as taboo. But thoughts alone are not enough. Actions are often needed to reveal greater truths and evidence, and thought without action is typically not as effective, because acting produces a more focused and more detailed understanding. As Scottish poet Robert Burns expressed, "The best laid schemes o' Mice an' Men, / Gang aft agley"-meaning that they falter when they encounter reality. Actions are also the main way in which the results of experiments can be observed by others. This is critical because the experiments that are particularly useful are those from which others can learn. Freedom of expression is important, too, since it enables the sharing of knowledge and because reactions from others sharpen and deepen human cognition, facilitating understanding. The role of experimentation in the acquisition of useful knowledge-and that it depends on action, not just contemplation-is well illustrated by children's learning strategies, honed by hundreds of thousands of years of evolutionary selection. Children of all ages continually try new actions to understand the social conventions, rules, and norms around them. Mere contemplation from their playpen would not be enough for this type of experiential learning. One domain where action is particularly important for building collective knowledge is economic activity. Collective knowledge includes how we identify the goods and services that our society values and how combinations of different techniques, skills, and innovations can be used for producing them. This requires not only freedom of expression but also freedom of economic choice-entrepreneurs launching new businesses, innovators thinking big, workers discovering their talents and aptitudes and investing in their skills, and everybody exploring their tastes and interests. Knowledge gained from economic choices and data often goes beyond what can be codified and quantified. Moreover, it is often a by-product of the activities and actions of individuals and communities: Will consumers warm up to a new product? Will individuals still want to switch to a product even if their friends do not? Will novel production techniques work, and if they don't at first, is this because they are hopeless or because they require more fine-tuning? Economic choice requires some degree of property rights, confirming the emphasis of early liberal thinkers on the security of these rights. Without property rights, economic decisions are distorted, because people cannot control the assets that are key for investment and they can't be sure that they'll enjoy the fruits of their investments and innovation. This supports property rights not because of a notion of "natural rights" or some principle that one's holdings of goods and assets are sacred. Rather, they are instrumental-a vehicle for effective experimentation and knowledge building. These rights must exist for us to benefit from the fruits of collective knowledge. This justification does not suggest that property rights are inviolable. If other needs-for example, the necessity to build railways that are important for business expansion or public service provision-trump the importance of property rights, a community or society should be able to induce people to sell their land. It also suggests that there is nothing wrong with moderate taxation of the returns from one's assets, so long as this does not turn confiscatory or strongly discourage experimentation or innovation. Since part of the reason why freedoms are valuable is that they enable us to try new things, the more unconventional and audacious one's thoughts (and the associated actions, within bounds of not harming others), the better. This is what Charles Sanders Peirce also concluded from his own justifications, especially when he wrote:

Upon this first, and in one sense this sole, rule of reason, that in order to learn you must desire to learn, and in so desiring not be satisfied with what you already incline to think, there follows one corollary which itself deserves to be inscribed upon every wall of the city of philosophy: Do not block the way of inquiry.

Heretical thoughts are particularly useful for society-provided they do not cause direct harm to others or their freedoms-because their heresy means that most people shy away from them, and also because even misguided thoughts improve the community's self-learning via the process of countering these thoughts rather than just suppressing them. This is why liberalism must be first and foremost about providing room for experimentation to individuals and communities.

Pillar II: Nondomination and Self-Government Classical liberalism defines freedom as noninterference, especially by state institutions and actors. In essence, I am free if the state or bureaucrats cannot tell me what to think, what not to think, what to do, and what not to do. (Libertarianism, which is built on the sanctity of individual autonomy and economic freedom and has become popular on the right and in some tech circles over the last several decades, goes even further and maintains that taxation and regulation are always violations of freedoms. This type of stance cannot be justified by the arguments here.) Noninterference is an excellent starting point and clarifies why coercion is inimical to experimentation. Yet it isn't sufficient for the freedoms that should be the foundation of experimentation. The concept of "nondomination" is much more appropriate. This concept goes back to the Roman Republic and is advocated by "republican thinkers" such as Quentin Skinner, Philip Pettit, and Frank Lovett. The notion of citizenship in the Roman Republic (albeit with all of its flaws and logical inconsistencies) was based on equality before the law and the rights that citizens enjoyed, as well as their responsibility to uphold the republic's institutions and way of life. Pettit argues that the essence of this is a notion of freedom that rules out living "at the mercy of another, having to live in a manner that leaves you vulnerable to some ill that the other is in a position arbitrarily to impose." In a more vivid description, he proposes that, as opposed to noninterference, nondomination for a horse requires not "free reins," where the rider holds the reins and decides not to use them, but "no reins"-the rider does not even have the option to change his or her mind and take control of the horse. Referencing the Roman Republic, Pettit expands on this notion as follows:

First, you must enjoy independence in relation to private individuals and associations; you must not be unprotected in relation to dominium or private power. And second, you must enjoy a certain independence in relation to the state; you must share in control of its doings in such a way that you are not unprotected in relation to imperium or public power. On the one side, you must enjoy personal independence, on the other political independence.

As I already hinted at the beginning of this chapter, the first of Pettit's freedoms has to be extended so that communities and their norms don't have such power either and so that the cage of norms does not become the most stifling constraint against freedom and experimentation. There are reasons to desire nondomination in society that go beyond those I discuss here. But clearly, domination is highly inhospitable to experimentation. If an individual is not truly free and depends, implicitly or explicitly, on the goodwill or whim of another party, then their freedom of expression and choice are circumscribed. There is another sense in which domination is an enemy to experimentation. If some groups are dominated, their perspectives will be lost in the new things that are tried and how they are evaluated. This is what W. E. B. Du Bois understood keenly in the context of the intense social discrimination against Black Americans. He wrote:

Only the sufferer knows his suffering and ... no state can be strong which excludes from its expressed wisdom the knowledge possessed by mothers, wives, and daughters .... The same arguments apply to other excluded groups. Nondomination, conceptualized in this way, is an argument against social engineering-top-down efforts to change and police people's beliefs and practices. Social engineering can come from the state, from norms, or from elites. No matter its provenance, intense social engineering will extinguish experimentation. Nondomination also calls for self-government, as emphasized by Pettit's second freedom in the above quote. By "self-government" I'm referring to bottom-up participation and methods of having voice and contributing to collective decisions, starting in communities and extending to the national level. Democratic participation and civil society mobilization are part of self-government, and so is the process of communities organizing around their priorities. In any society, people must abide by laws, institutions, and norms, and are punished for transgressions. The imposition of such rules can be a form of dominance, too, unless these rules are responsive to the views and priorities of the people bound by them. Self-government introduces a degree of responsiveness and acts as a bulwark against rules turning into means of dominance. Self-government also prevents concentration of power in the hands of a narrow segment of the population, making nondomination more likely in practice. There are other compelling reasons to favor self-government as well. But the reasoning from my argument is quite direct. Experimentation, especially audacious efforts to do new and sometimes controversial things, is discouraged when there is domination of one by others. Domination coming from the state is most clearly hostile to experimentation-think of Kafka's description of bureaucratic control in The Trial, where a bank clerk is arrested and prosecuted for an unspecified crime, which is thus impossible to defend himself against. One aspect of self-government, emphasized in Western democracies, is voting, which enables people to depose rulers. Voting constrains the ruler's power, which is often the source of the most abusive impositions. Citizens may additionally be allowed to make proposals and bring new ideas to the table, so that agenda setting is not monopolized by a narrow elite. Since at least the work of Montesquieu, it is also understood that effective democratic governance relies on institutional arrangements for separating the power of rulers into different components, so that political elites check one another. In the famous words of James Madison in The Federalist Papers, "ambition must be made to counteract ambition." In the United States, the judiciary can overrule executive actions, and the legislature was always conceived as the main branch of government that would limit what the executive could do. In the twentieth century, the administrative state emerged as another branch that can constrain executive abuses, because it has its own mission. For example, the Federal Reserve is tasked with controlling the monetary system and inflation, and would continue to do so even if the president asked for lower interest rates to increase his or her popularity. But the emphasis on self-government should remind us that democracy must be more than just electoral politics and the separation of powers. A passive notion of democracy, in which the only role of citizens is to vote out a president or prime minister who doesn't live up to expectations, wouldn't constitute a true check on power. An active citizenry is an essential part of self-government. Self-government must start at the community level but also go beyond the community, for example, when dealing with resources and priorities that are shared more broadly, such as roads and laws affecting multiple communities or issues impacting the whole society. Early liberal thinkers were quite insistent on self-government. Algernon Sidney, a colonel in the New Model Army in the English Civil War and an influential republican thinker, made this point vividly:

For as liberty solely consists in an independency upon the will of another, and by the name of slave we understand a man, who can neither dispose of his person nor goods, but enjoys all at the will of his master; there is no such thing in nature as a slave, if those men or nations are not slaves, who have no other title to what they enjoy than the grace of the prince, which he may revoke whensoever he pleaseth.

Self-government constrains power. But that's not all. Political participation is a form of community-level experimentation in the context of social life. We are all experimenting when we seek to understand the other side's argument, look for counterarguments, and find compromises. Community-level experimentation bolstered by self-government is vital for social adaptation, especially in a turbulent environment, because many of the adaptations we need are at the community level. Such experimentation would be impossible if communities did not have different priorities and values (because they would all mimic one another). It would be hampered if they did not have the capability to engage in self- government. Self-government also creates new pathways for sharing information, as individuals form new connections for deliberation and coalitions in political and social life. This discussion also clarifies that self-government can take different forms from standard "Western democracy." In small- and medium- scale societies, assemblies without formal elections have at times worked as a method of self-government, even if it isn't clear how they could function in modern, large nation-states. Tribal customs can sometimes be combined with methods of political participation. In the spirit of humility and recognizing our own fallibility, we should not rush to conclude that Western-style democracy is the only political organization that builds a foundation for nondomination. All in all, self-government can enshrine basic methods of controlling excessive power; it can enable the realization of community priorities and add to their cohesiveness; it can contribute an indispensable dimension of experiential and social learning by means of political participation; and it can unleash much-needed community-level experimentation. In this way, my arguments go against the popular view that democracy and liberal values are at odds with each other. I maintain that liberalism is synergistic with democracy and self-government. Hence, the notion of liberal democracy is neither an oxymoron nor the marriage of two disparate notions. In fact, liberal commitments to the freedom of expression and choice in the political domain would make no sense without some degree of self-government, since otherwise these actions would have no impact. Moreover, self-government is critical for nondomination in both private and public domains, and is also necessary for expanding the scope of liberties, allowing groups to make free collective choices. This is the sense in which the theory of liberalism here is, in essence, also a theory of liberal democracy, but based on a true commitment to self-government. Of course, there are cases in which democracy and liberalism can be in tension-for example, if a majority supported the suppression of the freedoms of some people. But this does not contradict my emphasis. Liberalism favors self-government that provides protection for basic liberties-not majoritarian conceptions of democracy, as I'll explain in chapter 10. Nevertheless, the vital point is that the kind of liberalism I am describing and defending here cannot exist without self-government. Liberalism is strengthened by democracy, and democracy by liberalism. Finally, nondomination also calls for a level playing field, which in practice requires some amount of redistribution to help those who are truly disadvantaged. Domination by the state can be stultifying for experimentation, but so will be domination by private actors or by the impersonal dynamics of the market. Nondomination requires opportunities, resources, and some basic safety. If your ability to fire me can send me into poverty and starvation, then you have domination over me. This, too, discourages experimentation. Pettit proposes the following "eyeball test" for nondomination:

The eyeball test requires that people should be so resourced and protected in the basic choices of life-for short, the basic liberties -that they can look others in the eye without reason for fear or deference of the kind that the power of interference might inspire. When you enjoy social, medical, and judicial security, and benefit from a suitable legal and economic order, you do not depend for your security on the indulgence and condescension of others.

This test applies well to experimentation. If I am not in a position to look you in the eye on equal terms, could I experiment in a way that goes against your wishes, beliefs, and interests? The eyeball test makes it clear that any type of social discrimination is detrimental to experimentation. If groups are discriminated against, they may not have the opportunity or the confidence to undertake the types of experimentation that can shake our incorrect consensuses. The same conclusion follows if the playing field is highly tilted. The eyeball test, in service of experimentation, also points to why certain regulations are critical: If the playing field that emerges from social and economic dynamics is so tilted as to make effective experimentation impossible, regulations that protect those with fewer economic resources or less social status can expand experimentation. A level playing field also argues for moderate redistribution and the existence of a social safety net. A society with huge inequality cannot be a good basis for effective experimentation. For example, if all wealth and social power are in the hands of a few tech billionaires, the rest may be too shy or too intimidated to try new things. Even if the rest are up to trying new things, the distribution of social status and prestige in society may be so distorted that only the billionaires' thoughts and actions will matter for society's key decisions. Redistribution should always be a tool for providing more resources and security for the less well-off. Whether it should also be used to actively limit wealth disparities between the top and the bottom depends on whether institutions allow billionaires to use their wealth to exert excessive power over others. In societies such as the United States, where wealth confers great status and institutions constraining the political influence of the superrich are weak, there would be a stronger case for doing so. Once again, the only or main justification for a level playing field and moderate redistribution isn't to support experimentation and collective knowledge building. Many notions of justice would strongly object to a tilted playing field, and some societies may prefer higher levels of redistribution in order to create a more equal allocation of resources. Nonetheless, nondomination for experimentation itself calls for a level playing field, entailing some amount of redistribution and support for the less fortunate members of society. An additional argument for redistribution follows from the "desirability of collective knowledge" premise. Recall that such desirability is predicated on the notion that a large number of people will not be made worse off because of the accumulation and use of collective knowledge, for example, in the form of new technologies that could destroy their jobs and incomes. Under such circumstances, redistribution could bolster the desirability of collective knowledge, because it ensures that economic losers are partially compensated.

Pillar III: Economic Growth The third conclusion from my four premises is support for economic growth. Economic growth is essential for the betterment of our material conditions and is intertwined with the accumulation and use of collective knowledge. It is also self-reinforcing with experimentation: Experimenting with entrepreneurial endeavors, new production techniques, and new goods and services drives economic growth, and economic growth expands opportunities for experimentation. Simultaneously, economic growth is critical for nondomination. The more resources society has-particularly if they are distributed equitably-the greater are the options for people to make decisions without being dependent on others or on the state. It is important that economic growth is not the sole criterion for a successful society. If it were, growth that generated huge inequalities, and even growth that increased the incomes of only a very small fraction of the population, would be condoned. This isn't so, according to the premises I have introduced. The desirability of collective knowledge is predicated on sharing the gains. Moreover, if economic growth creates huge inequalities, it becomes increasingly difficult to contain emerging power imbalances. One dimension of the failure of American liberal democracy I emphasized in the previous chapter is that as the country became more prosperous, many groups felt disempowered because they failed to benefit much or at all from economic growth. They came to see the social and political power of the billionaires and the educated elites that the growth process produced as inimical to their own dignity and freedoms. Hence, it is essential that economic growth be embedded in an institutional context that provides at least some checks against extreme inequalities and seeks feasible pathways to shared prosperity, for example, by creating good, well-paying jobs for people from different communities and backgrounds. Depending on its rate and nature, economic growth could also have adverse consequences (such as environmental degradation) or face natural limits in the far future. All the same, my case for liberalism firmly supports the "right kind of growth," with the capacity to become shared. This is particularly so in our current context, where billions of people around the world live in or near poverty and where there is still considerable demand for greater prosperity and good jobs in the industrialized world. In sum, regardless of some of the tensions produced by the process of economic change, one shouldn't ignore the critical role of growth in liberal ideals. The fact that economic growth has fueled inequality-especially in the United States-doesn't mean we can afford to abandon it. For the medium term, no viable alternative exists. Growth based on new goods and services could also be less harmful to the environment. Most importantly, if we want to aspire to nondomination and free experimentation, and even more so if we want to remain true to the idea that collective knowledge is useful for improving our lives, we have to embrace economic growth and build the right institutional structure so that it becomes shared and sustainable.

Pillar IV: Soft Communitarianism The fourth pillar that follows from this chain of reasoning is about the relationship of the individual to their community. Experiments are nearly useless without social learning-if nobody other than the experimenter learns from them and changes their beliefs and practices, there will be little contribution to collective knowledge and to the achievement of society's objectives. Since communication and trust are critical for social learning, we must recognize the essential role that communities play. Moreover, as I have already stressed, experimentation by communities themselves is vital for producing social knowledge. Finally, self-government must start at the local level, with communities. These arguments call for a type of communitarianism-valuing and respecting communities in their choices and different forms. I call this conclusion soft communitarianism, because, as we have seen, communities can also become a powerful obstacle to experimentation. Communities, even more so than many other social institutions, depend on norms. But norms both enable and constrain. In fact, at the heart of liberalism resides a vexing trade-off between the essential roles that community plays-in building social meaning and shared understanding, contributing to the dissemination of useful knowledge, and enabling self-government-and the restrictions it imposes on individuals' and other communities' freedoms and involvement in self-government. There is no perfect recipe that emerges from these premises, and there shouldn't be. There are trade-offs and edge cases that necessitate more nuance. Liberalism is a framework-rather than a set of cast-in-stone policies and rules-that can help us navigate these trade-offs, but ultimate decisions will have to be made by self-governing societies.

Working-Class Liberalism These four pillars together define the liberalism I propose in this book-and I refer to the practical implementation of these pillars today as "working-class liberalism." It is a liberalism that elevates experimentation. It defends the freedoms of expression, association, and choice-with these freedoms conceptualized as nondomination. It is suspicious of excessive power of all sorts, including those that come from intense social engineering, and it calls for self-government as a counterpoint to such power. It stands against discrimination and tries to use redistribution and its safety net to enable less fortunate and less well-off members to benefit from society's resources, and ultimately to contribute to economic activities, social learning, and collective knowledge building. It valorizes economic growth and flourishing-and especially good jobs for people with different backgrounds and skills. And it recognizes and respects community as the nexus of social meaning, trust, communication, and the sharing of useful knowledge. Liberalism itself must be humble and should always be conceived as a work in progress-so being reimagined and rebuilt during times of turbulence, caused by outside factors and those of its own making, is not against its character. Liberalism is a framework within which trade- offs have to be made, ideally via deliberation, as I'll argue in the next chapter. The pillars I am defending create boundaries for that framework, without dictating definite solutions to difficult social problems (such as the degree of redistribution or the exact line between harmful acts and freedoms). These are the pillars that liberal democracy de facto followed during the times when it achieved great success, as I explain in chapter 4. They are also the pillars that were abandoned by liberal activists and elites in the context of postindustrial politics. Experimentation and the variation in values and priorities that bolster it became discouraged; ideas associated with nondomination were ignored; self-government fell by the wayside as the college educated acquired more social power and started favoring a top-down technocratic outlook; a social engineering agenda created a new cage of norms discouraging variation in cultural attitudes; shared prosperity and good jobs were downgraded as priorities; and community was forgotten. In the next chapter, I explain why the core notions articulated here are in tension with efforts to justify liberty from a social contract, and how contractarian arguments lay the groundwork for top-down schemes and the crisis of liberalism.

Chapter 3

Liberalism Without Social Contract

Ireland's citizens' assemblies demonstrate deliberation-based democracy can resolve divisive issues. Social-contract theories risk becoming unaccountable, top-down schemes.

▼

Liberalism Without Social Contract

Liberty means more than freedom from the arbitrary power of Governments. It means freedom from economic servitude to Want and Squalor and other social evils; it means freedom from arbitrary power in any form. A starving man is not free. -William Beveridge

I am uneasy to think I approve of one object and disapprove of another; call one thing beautiful and another deformed; decide concerning truth and falsehood, reason and folly, without knowing upon what principles I proceed. I am concerned for the condition of the learned world which lies under such deplorable ignorance in all of these particulars. -David Hume

Dublin Castle, first raised as a Viking fortress in the thirteenth century, still commands the highest ground in the city center. Long dubbed the nerve center of Irish power, its gilded columns and royal-blue ceiling have framed every presidential oath. In 2016, it was filled not by politicians or visiting dignitaries but by ninety-nine citizens pulled at random-door to door, county by county-so the room would resemble Ireland itself. Justice Mary Laffoy was tasked with chairing this Citizens' Assembly, which she described as an "exercise in deliberative democracy." No party whips, no bishops. Just ninety-nine Irish citizens interested in tackling one of the most divisive topics facing Ireland: the repeal of the Eighth Amendment, which banned abortion. Each table paired six citizens with a notetaker and a quiet facilitator. One participant, Louise Caldwell, an entrepreneur, recalled the mood as "friendly but serious .... If one person was speaking too much they were gently reminded that we needed to hear all opinions, while quieter members were encouraged to speak." Every voice mattered. Outside, the streets were seething. Twenty thousand had marched through rain the month before, chanting "Repeal," while pro-life groups displayed graphic photos of fetuses.
