Over the course of five weekends spread across five months, the Citizens' Assembly heard obstetricians discuss sexual violence, lawyers parse fetal-rights clauses, and campaigners recount late-night flights to Liverpool clinics. Then the assembly debated intensely-and voted. The ultimate vote within the assembly was 64 percent for repeal. Eight months later, it was the country's turn to vote in a referendum, which revealed 66 percent support for repeal. What many had considered unthinkable, or at least impossible without splitting the country down the middle, happened: Ireland repealed the abortion ban, with considerable legitimacy and acceptance from diverse communities. Since then, Ireland has reconvened citizens' assemblies on issues ranging far and wide, including its aging population, fixed-term parliaments, and even biodiversity loss. In a chamber built for monarchy, the Irish republic has debated the most vexing decisions and survived.

What Ireland did-generate buy-in from a large fraction of the population and thus legitimacy for collective decisions on contentious issues -is rare and even inconceivable to many. Instead, liberal and illiberal thinkers since the Enlightenment have gravitated toward overarching principles that can be imposed on human behavior and society, justified by some unbreakable imperatives or a hypothetical social contract that defines what is good for people to do. We can see this same division in what liberalism has come to stand for today. It may be identified as "classical liberalism," associated with John Locke, Montesquieu, Adam Smith, Jeremy Bentham, John Stuart Mill, or Friedrich Hayek, emphasizing noninterference, especially from state institutions or the majority (though Mill's later writings are closer to the ideas I proposed in the previous chapter and Smith's thinking is much richer, as I describe below). The philosopher Isaiah Berlin called this "negative liberty" because it specifies what the state cannot or should not do to individuals. This stance is what Europeans refer to when they talk of liberalism. Or it is associated with ideas going back to Jean-Jacques Rousseau and implicitly or explicitly accepted by progressive liberals in the U.S. and Europe. This version instead aligns with Berlin's concept of "positive liberty," claiming that true freedom is impossible without the state and society actively working to enable individual capabilities and flourishing-and, when necessary, imposing these freedoms on individuals and communities. The justification for this positive or "social" liberty is imagined to be in a social contract and the "general will" it represents. These traditions overlap on some of their key principles. However, they also reflect substantial differences, and each diverges significantly from my emphasis on community and nondomination in building useful collective knowledge. Neither tradition celebrates community. Both are suspicious of self-government-for classical liberals, self-government and democracy may bring the tyranny of the majority, and for progressives, democracy and community-level priorities are often at odds with the general will. This chapter takes a deeper dive into political philosophy in order to formulate an alternative foundation for (working-class) liberalism, based on community, deliberation, and seeking broad buy-in from the entire society and from diverse communities-just like the Irish decision- making process on abortion. It is only natural that my corner of the broad liberal tent, which emphasizes self-government, should seek general acceptance from society rather than justify itself with general ethical principles. As I will show, community was important for early liberal philosophers, and ideas of nondomination have made way for noninterference and progressive notions of social liberty. I then present my objection to social-contract theories-that their justifications easily morph into unaccountable, top-down schemes that can be imposed on people without deliberation or recourse. These tendencies also provide the background for the crisis of liberalism by preparing the ground for social engineering, as I explain in greater detail in chapter 8.

Liberalism and Community Community is not in contradiction with liberalism. Liberty needs community norms that are consistent with broad freedoms and mutual understanding, regard, and trust to build useful collective knowledge. The role of community was recognized by early liberal thinkers going back to the Roman Republic. Community, especially the mutual understanding, regard, and obligations it cultivates, was a common theme in the writings of Cicero, who argued:

We ought ... to contribute our part to the common good, and by the interchange of kind offices, both in giving and receiving, alike by skill, by labor, and by the resources at our command, strengthen the social union of men among men.

Not just Roman Republicans but also ancient Athenians before them put as much emphasis on the civic duties of free citizens as on their rights. Surviving documents about Athenian citizens stress their duties to the Athenian community. Serving in the Athenian legislature and certain executive responsibilities were not options but requirements. Contributing to the defense of the city-state was also nonnegotiable. In his famous Funeral Oration, the Athenian politician and ruler during the Peloponnesian War, Pericles, declared that "When you realize her greatness, then reflect that what made her great was men with a spirit of adventure, men who knew their duty, men who were ashamed to fall below a certain standard." He added, "We do not say that a man who takes no interest in politics is a man who minds his own business; we say that he has no business here at all." For Romans, liberality also meant mutual helpfulness-accepting the duties of giving and not just of receiving. Cicero and the later Roman philosopher Lucius Annaeus Seneca stressed the importance of giving and returning gifts and favors within a community. As Seneca wrote,

The true felicity of life is to be free from perturbations, to understand our duties towards God and man: to enjoy the present without any anxious dependence upon the future. Not to amuse ourselves with either hopes or fears, but to rest satisfied with what we have, which is abundantly sufficient.

And these principles continued to shine through in the beliefs of Renaissance thinkers like Erasmus and Machiavelli. Similar sentiments about duties are common even among thinkers associated with classical liberalism, such as John Locke and Adam Smith. Locke argued that people should be taught to be "kind, liberal, and civil," and selfishness should be strongly discouraged in community. Smith asserted: "He is not a citizen who does not wish to promote, by every means in his power, the welfare of the whole society of his fellow-citizens." His book The Theory of Moral Sentiments can be understood as an attempt to develop foundations for a just, liberal society that recognizes the communities in which we all live. Despite his fears about the tyranny of the majority, John Stuart Mill also acknowledged the importance of community, writing, "Human beings owe to each other help to distinguish the better from the worse, and encouragement to choose the former and avoid the latter."

"Man Loses by Social Contract His Natural Liberty" When Enlightenment philosophers attempted to formulate foundations for liberalism, however, community fell by the wayside. Most Enlightenment thinkers instead looked for overarching principles to guide how society should be organized. The towering figure of the Enlightenment, Immanuel Kant, sought universal principles to guide moral life within a society. He believed these principles needed to come in a deontological form, promoting certain behaviors and discouraging or even banning others. Here, "deontology" refers to the effort to define what is right and what is wrong on the basis of absolute rules that determine the ethical principles of society. The justification for banning some behaviors is precisely that individuals might be tempted by them-there would be no reason to ban something that nobody would do anyway. Hence, deontology has to be interwoven with restrictions on freedom. This is what Kant anticipated when he wrote: Everyone must admit that if a law is to have moral force, i.e., to be the basis of an obligation, it must carry with it absolute necessity; that, for example, the precept, "Thou shalt not lie," is not valid for men alone, as if other rational beings had no need to observe it.

This thinking also formed the basis of Kant's categorical imperative-the idea that an individual should "act as if the maxim of thy action were to become by thy will a universal law of nature." Or, put differently, "Do unto others what you want them to do unto you," similar to what the Golden Rule of several religions maintains. By its nature, such a deontological stance does not encourage fallibility or doubt. This Kant understood well. After all, how could the categorical imperative be categorical if people doubted it? How could you force them to follow it even when they were tempted to deviate from it, if such doubt were widespread? This is why Kant argued strongly that "the basis of obligation must not be sought in the nature of man, or in the circumstances in the world in which he is placed, but a priori simply in the conception of pure reason." Though different in approach, the same certitude is present in Jean-Jacques Rousseau's philosophy. Rousseau's key concept was the "general will"-the will of the society as a whole, not that of an individual. Rousseau was adamant that individuals had to submit to this general will. Common good required that the general will should rule supreme over individual choice, or as he put it:

What man loses by the social contract is his natural liberty and an unlimited right to everything he tries to get and succeeds in getting; what he gains is civil liberty in the proprietorship of all he possesses.

The reference to social contract here is important.

The Conceit of Social Contract Thomas Hobbes's notion of social contract is one of the most central ideas in modern philosophy. In developing this notion, Hobbes built on the ideas of earlier thinkers such as Marsilius of Padua and Machiavelli but gave them new resonance and scope. The premise is inspirational: A legitimate government should be the one that people could be argued to have given their support. Yet the words "could be argued to" hide an important conceit. For Hobbes, this meant a person ceded their natural rights in exchange for peace and security through a covenant or social contract, subjecting their "own will to that of another one." The social contract could thus involve people becoming subjugated by a powerful monarch or leader despite their wishes otherwise. In Hobbes's words, "Covenants, without the sword, are but words and of no strength to secure a man at all." Little doubt is left about where Hobbes's preferences lie for upholding the covenant! John Locke's argument for popular sovereignty was also contractarian, even though he disagreed with Hobbes about the possibility that a social contract could subjugate a person to somebody else's will or whim. As Locke summed it up:

A Man, as has been proved, cannot subject himself to the Arbitrary Power of another; and having in the State of Nature no Arbitrary Power over the Life, Liberty, or Possession of another, but only so much as the Law of Nature gave him for the preservation of himself, and the rest of Mankind.

But even for Locke, the social contract wasn't an actual contract and didn't result from a process of deliberation. Although an individual couldn't subject themselves to the arbitrary power of another, Locke added:

The Liberty of Man, in Society, is to be under no other Legislative Power, but that established, by consent, in the Commonwealth; nor under the Dominion of any Will, or Restraint of any Law, but what that Legislative shall enact, according to the Trust put in it.

Where does that consent come from? Locke, too, had to turn to the conceit that one can be argued to have put their trust in a social contract or in a procedure for delegating legislative authority. This became troublesome when English peasants were required to give up their rights to communally used lands-thus setting up private property rights in agriculture, something with which John Locke agreed. This was what Parliament decided, but peasants did not explicitly consent to it. Nor did they have any recourse against it. Why is this different from people agreeing to subject their freedom to the power of a state or a tyrant? The same tensions came back again and again, most powerfully in Rousseau's philosophy. On the surface, Rousseau agreed with Locke's insistence that individual freedom should be the starting point, stating:

Every man being born free and his own master, no-one, under any pretext whatsoever, can make any man subject without his consent. To decide that the son of a slave is born a slave is to decide that he is not born a man. Yet, at once, Rousseau turned the tables and asserted that true freedom and the common good required subjugation to the general will, even if the general will went against an individual's wishes:

As long as several men in assembly regard themselves as a single body, they have only a single will which is concerned with their common preservation and general well-being. In this case, all the springs of the State are vigorous and simple and its rules clear and luminous: there are no embroilments or conflicts of interests; the common good is everywhere clearly apparent, and only good sense is needed to perceive it. Peace, unity and equality are the enemies of political subtleties.

In case any doubt remained, Rousseau summed it up:

Whoever refuses to obey the general will shall be compelled to do so by the whole body. This means nothing less than he will be forced to be free.

But then what stops the presumed social contract from decreeing misery, obedience, or annihilation of some group as our general will? No wonder Isaiah Berlin counted Rousseau as an enemy of liberty. Nonetheless, it was tempting to follow Rousseau at a time when great inequities existed and absolutist kingdoms were suffocating all sorts of freedoms. And so the French revolutionaries of 1789 hailed his call. The Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen stated in its article 6:

The law is the expression of the general will. All citizens have the right to contribute personally, or through their representatives, to its formation. It must be the same for all, whether it protects or punishes. All citizens, being equal in its eyes, are equally admissible to all public dignities, positions, and employments, according to their capacities, and without any other distinction than that of their virtues and their talents.

Many of the revolution's leaders and deputies were devotees of Rousseau, arguably nobody more so than its most famous leader, Maximilien Robespierre. Robespierre surmised that Rousseau's general will justified the guillotine for the enemies of the revolution. In a famous speech to the assembly when the death penalty for the king was being debated, Robespierre stated: The general will is not formed in secret meetings or around ministerial tables. Everywhere and always, the minority has the right to make the voice of truth heard, or what it perceives as such. On this earth, virtue is always in the minority.

In other words, the general will did not have to follow the majority. Then who would decide it? Robespierre was sure that virtue would, adding, "Without virtue, would the earth not be peopled by tyrants and slaves?" The root cause of the problem is the conceit of social contract: an effort to bypass the question of who decides what the general will is and who should have the overwhelming power to ensure obedience to this will. Rousseau himself? A philosopher-king? The general assembly during the French Revolution? Its Committee on Public Safety? Or just Robespierre? Unbeknownst to the leaders of the French Revolution, that same idea would soon justify even more significant departures from freedom. Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels formulated one of the most stinging criticisms of the inequities of their time in The Communist Manifesto. Based on their diagnosis, Marx and Engels jumped to a notion of the common good that overrode the interests and freedoms of some people-just like Rousseau's general will. Appealing to the bourgeoisie (property holders, industrialists, and the privileged), Marx and Engels stated:

But don't wrangle with us so long as you apply, to our intended abolition of bourgeois property, the standard of your bourgeois notions of freedom, culture, law, etc. Your very ideas are but the outgrowth of the conditions of your bourgeois production and bourgeois property, just as your jurisprudence is but the will of your class made into a law for all, a will whose essential character and direction are determined by the economical conditions of existence of your class.

Under their philosophy, true social liberty can be achieved only by abolishing bourgeois freedoms. Evidently, this wasn't just a mild form of Berlin's positive freedoms. It was a radical reshaping of society, justified by what their understanding of the general will required (even if Marx and Engels never explicitly referred to a social contract or the general will). This conceit of the social contract and general will anticipates some of the travails of liberalism in the late twentieth and early twenty-first century-how it would grapple with its fundamental dilemmas (chapter 5), how it would struggle with adapting to being the establishment philosophy (chapter 7), and how it would try to resolve its dilemmas by imposing its values on others wholesale, without any attempt at deliberation (chapter 8). In every application of the social contract lies a contradiction: If it is the general will that supports true freedom, then true freedom must entail violations of individual freedoms conceived (or misconceived) differently from those enshrined in the general will. Social liberty and the general will, backed up by a social contract, could routinely require that individual freedoms be disregarded and everybody be made to abide by and enjoy freedoms as articulated by the general will. Individuals and communities may wish to have distinct norms and values. But if the general will dictates that true freedom is different from these wishes, the general will should impose it on them. One way out would be to strive for compromises via communication, deliberation, and debate, as the Irish Citizens' Assembly attempted to do. Under the general will, however, there is no room for deliberation or debate, and people cannot have any recourse against the general will. One might object that there are social contract conceptions and justifications for progressivism that are consistent with liberalism. John Rawls, whose 1971 book A Theory of Justice is foundational to twentieth-century liberalism, was much more committed to liberal ideas than Rousseau was. But I will argue that the same sort of "presumed to have agreed" reasoning creates tensions for Rawls's philosophy, too. Rawls attempted to derive certain left-leaning liberal notions, including a simultaneous justification for freedoms and equality, from contractarian foundations consistent with liberalism. Rawls's idea is that people should agree on what is just, fair, and socially desirable "behind a veil of ignorance"-without yet knowing how rich or talented they are or what their social position will be. For example, we can imagine that decisions about discrimination toward a specific group will be made when people do not know whether they will belong to this group. Rawls reasoned that in such a situation people would opt for no discrimination because they would not want to expose themselves to the risk of suffering from said discrimination. From this reasoning, Rawls derives his most important conclusion: Social arrangements should be judged by how they improve the lot of the least well-off members of society. Hence, we should enthusiastically embrace a new tax that helps the least well-off, even if it takes a very heavy toll on those enjoying greater riches. What I just described is Rawls's "difference principle," to which he adds a "liberty principle" granting everybody access to basic liberties, in order to ensure that the difference principle does not end up taking away people's fundamental liberties. In later work, Rawls also grapples with the issue of "shared moral principles" or "public reason," which I discuss below. Nevertheless, the problem with using contractarian arguments, including Rawls's, as the basis of general ethical principles isn't that different from the one afflicting Rousseau, Marx and Engels, and other enthusiasts for the general will. In practice, nobody is ever asked their views about justice when a social contract is being designed or behind a veil of ignorance. It may appear oppressive that people are presumed to have agreed, without debate or the right to object, to a scheme or policy that is harmful to their actual interests. For those living in a real society, not behind a curtain, it would be little comfort to hear that their condition has been justified by a philosopher or statesperson claiming they would have assented to such a social contract behind the veil. Given the chance, they would readily tear up that social contract-and the veil. The root problem is that social contract theories, interpreted as the basis for general ethical rules, go against self-government. They don't seek the actual consent of individuals and communities to public choices by a process of deliberation, communication, and debate. Rather, they attempt to circumvent any opposition by invoking high-level precepts without broad buy-in from the population. There is an alternative interpretation that Rawls himself offers for his contractarian philosophy-as an input into a debate on shared moral principles. According to this perspective, social contract principles and trade-offs behind the veil of ignorance could be arguments used to convince people about policies, institutions, and shared moral principles (and so could Kant's categorical imperative or religious precepts such as the Golden Rule). However, in this case, such reasoning should be used mainly in the context of deliberation and cannot justify actual policies or impositions of values on people-for example, becoming the basis of laws and how policymakers judge different options. So we are back to the same question: Does liberalism need a social contract? Might there be a more grounded version of such a contract that does not invoke general principles that people are presumed to have agreed to? Could we have an agreement that people actually consent to via a process of deliberation-and without needing to be cloaked behind a philosophical veil?

No Free Lunch for Liberalism Nineteenth-century classical liberal ideas were, in some sense, a response to the contradiction inherent to social contract theories, which maintained that social liberty could completely override individual freedoms. A natural reaction was to swing to the opposite extreme, defining liberty solely as noninterference-ignoring the self-governing roots of freedom and overlooking how other forms of power, like poverty or the threat of hunger, can strip choice of any real meaning. Hence, Jeremy Bentham's liberalism makes no appeal to capacities of individuals to exercise their freedom or the political preconditions of that freedom. John Stuart Mill's On Liberty followed Bentham, as well as Alexis de Tocqueville. Tocqueville's most original observation was how a community's consensus, even if dressed up in democratic garb, could stifle freedom. Tocqueville spent a lot of time on this in the second volume of Democracy in America. In his subsequent analysis of the French Revolution, he also worried about revolutionary zeal killing freedoms. John Locke's earlier work, linking freedoms to property rights and economic choices, was also foundational here. Popular sovereignty and political participation were central to Locke's thinking, but he derived these from notions of natural liberties, contractarian ideas, and principles of noninterference-perhaps because these were the least controversial places to counter authoritarian conceptions, such as the divine right of kings, that were paramount at the time he was writing. Noninterference received a big boost from Adam Smith's powerful arguments in The Wealth of Nations. Smith's philosophy was rich and certainly not oblivious to the importance of community and considerations of the common good, as I have already noted. He argued in his other monumental work, The Theory of Moral Sentiments, that "the wise and virtuous man is at all times willing that his own private interest should be sacrificed to the public interest of his own particular order or society." Smith's brilliant insight in The Wealth of Nations was to anticipate that economic liberty (people pursuing their own economic interests without interference), when coupled with competitive markets, would lead to the common good. There was an "invisible hand" of the market that could turn selfishness into a servant of the common good. In his words:

It is not from the benevolence of the butcher, the brewer, or the baker, that we expect our dinner, but from their regard to their own interest. We address ourselves, not to their humanity but to their self-love, and never talk to them of our own necessities but of their advantages. Nobody but a beggar chuses to depend chiefly upon the benevolence of his fellow-citizens.

Thus, Smith was, at one fell swoop, proposing a free lunch: Noninterference in economic decisions would both deliver good economic outcomes and remain faithful to liberty and social values. Or put differently, the common good would result from "allowing every man to pursue his own interest his own way upon the liberal plan of equality, liberty and justice" (italics in the original). Hence, provided that markets were competitive and there were reliable legal institutions, people rarely needed to depart from their interests-with economic noninterference and competitive markets, their actions would already serve the common good. In describing Smith's invisible hand, I use the word "anticipated" deliberately: Smith offered no rigorous proof for his claims. What he had were powerful intuitions and a wealth of keen observations. A more thorough exploration of these ideas would have to wait for later economic thinkers, starting with the French mathematician Léon Walras in the nineteenth century, and then Gerard Debreu and Kenneth Arrow in the twentieth. These economists established what is now known as the "first welfare theorem." This theorem starts with a number of premises-that individuals are selfish but rational (capable of making calculations about their self- interest), markets are competitive (no monopoly), information is perfect (nobody is fooled by prices or the quality of goods that they are offered), and there are no "externalities" (people's actions create neither negative nor positive effects on others that do not work through markets). Under these assumptions, the theorem establishes that selfishness of all participants (firms and individual consumers alike) leads to a competitive equilibrium with desirable social properties. In particular, in a competitive equilibrium, you can't make anybody better off without making somebody else worse off. Hence, in some sense, society is doing as well as it can from a welfare perspective. This is what Smith's invisible hand delivered. The first welfare theorem was a gift to the philosophy of noninterference. It wasn't just Locke, Bentham, and Mill's normative vision backing this approach. Economic theory now seemed to supply the hard logic behind it. Any government meddling in business decisions risked undermining the theorem's elegant mechanics. Even aid to the poor became suspect, for fear it might distort incentives. After all, why extend a helping hand when the invisible one was already on the job? The trouble is that, like most elegant theories, the first welfare theorem and the magic of the invisible hand rest on strong assumptions. And these assumptions are no more compelling than any other justification for noninterference. Communities are about mutual regard, not just self-interest. Monopolies are everywhere, and most forms of experimentation and learning in community generate externalities-whenever your knowledge contributes to my own understanding, this is a type of externality. Additionally, as we saw in chapter 1, incomplete and unreliable information about new goods and services was a key stumbling block for the market economy early on-and it's no different today. It isn't just its empirical foundations that are problematic; the first welfare theorem is not as strong as it might first appear. For example, an allocation in which a small group of aristocrats enjoy all the benefits while everybody else starves isn't inconsistent with the first welfare theorem-since reducing starvation may involve making some aristocrats a little less well-off. So, while the case for noninterference drawn from Smith's work is elegant-even captivating-it doesn't settle the debate. What tipped the scales in favor of noninterference was something else entirely. Smith's ideas were of their time and for their time. Support for liberties in Britain was entangled with middle classes and industrialists striving for economic success. Many in Britain were worried about the conditions and the moral fiber of the poor-what was sometimes termed the "Condition-of-England question," as articulated by the conservative Victorian-era Scottish writer Thomas Carlyle. These concerns were real but often not powerful enough to make the middle classes support political reform or large-scale redistribution as a way of improving the economic and social conditions of the lower classes. Even extending voting rights to people who did not hold property seemed a dangerously radical step to many Brits and certainly to Carlyle, who described such efforts as "the bitter discontent grown fierce and mad, the wrong condition therefore or the wrong disposition, of the Working Classes of England." Nondomination and self-government for the working classes would have been the furthest thing from the minds of Victorian elites and middle classes. Classical liberalism was thus synergistic with British political economy in the nineteenth century. "Political economy" here refers to arguments about who wins and who loses from societal and economic choices, how the politically powerful influence these choices for their benefit, and how political power is accumulated and exercised and by whom. Political economy factors play a major role in the successes and failures of liberalism, as we'll see. Nondomination Versus Noninterference Let us dive a little deeper into why classical liberals shied away from nondomination and opted for noninterference as their notion of freedom. Recall that nondomination calls for the capacity to undertake free actions and try new things without being subject to the arbitrary power of any person, norm, or institution. Noninterference concerns itself only with the removal of explicit barriers, as highlighted by classical liberals' and Hayek's philosophy as well as Berlin's emphasis on negative liberties. This distinction explains why, despite some parallels between Hayek's philosophy and the premises I used to justify liberalism in chapter 2, Hayek and I end up in quite different places regarding several key features of liberalism. Classical liberals', Hayek's, and my arguments share a basic core, encapsulated by this quote at the start of one of Hayek's chapters of The Constitution of Liberty, often attributed to Oliver Cromwell: "Man never mounts higher than when he knows not where he is going." Liberalism is about exploration, experimentation, and freedom to do new, unconventional things. Like classical liberals, Hayek emphasized the importance of limiting coercion to enable individual choice and flourishing. He defined coercion as "when one man's actions are made to serve another man's will, not for his own but for the other's purpose." This definition is broad enough to include the kinds of domination that happen merely with the threat of coercion or economic domination. But Hayek concluded from these principles that the biggest threat to freedom is from state intervention and from the administrative state- especially in his influential book The Road to Serfdom. Hayek believed this for several reasons. The first is that Hayek did not link his ideas to the notion of nondomination and thus to issues of self-government. Rather, he saw them as an affirmation of the classical liberal tradition's emphasis on noninterference, and he remained suspicious of democratic processes. Consequently, like other classical liberals, Hayek did not recognize that certain capacities were essential for trying new things and that significant inequalities and inability to engage in self-government could be major impediments to true freedom. Second, Hayek, again like classical liberals, was (rightly) worried about redistribution or interventions distorting incentives and discouraging new economic activity. But such worries should not have favored noninterference over nondomination. As I have already argued, nondomination does not dictate simple solutions but provides a framework for understanding and navigating the trade-offs we face. For example, how much state intervention should be tolerated in the economic activities of citizens so that others can have more resources to engage in their own free experimentation is a decision that comes with costs and benefits, and it should be decided by a society's own deliberative process. Hayek's strong case for noninterference thus amounted to making this choice on the basis of some overarching principles. Third, Hayek observed that rules imposed by an administrative state could easily metamorphose into strictures and were, in any case, unlikely to lead to proper experimentation, even if the administrative approach was "scientific." This is why he was so emphatic about adopting a broader notion of collective knowledge. Yet he did not note the possibility that a very unequal distribution of economic power and opportunities could also lead to similarly adverse consequences for the accumulation of both scientific and social knowledge. Finally, Hayek was writing during-and in some sense in reaction to-a time when the administrative state was rapidly building strength and the progressive liberal agenda was promoting some highly interventionist notions. Today, the administrative state and (some of) these notions have become an integral part of our lives. In a way, we have learned to live with them and may be in a better position to recognize their costs as well as their benefits. This does not invalidate Hayek's concerns, but it does enable us to take a more nuanced position than his outright rejection. We know that the administrative state is necessary and will be around and thus needs to be made consistent with basic liberties. Our societal choices should be based on trade-offs that recognize the benefits and costs of this administrative state. The bottom line is that neither Hayek's arguments nor core liberal principles justify noninterference in preference to nondomination. Sure, redistribution and intervention distort incentives, but this is just another factor to weigh when evaluating trade-offs. The administrative state can be stultifying, but when structured within the institutions and values of a liberal society, it can also facilitate nondomination and experimentation. Progressive ideas can lead to excessive intervention, but that doesn't mean that we should let the pendulum swing too far to the other side and ignore the reality of nondomination. Nor is there any reason for divorcing freedom from self-government.

From these philosophical considerations, we are again back to the same opposing camps advocating for liberty. One corner champions a more progressive understanding of liberty, conceptualizing social freedom quite differently from what an individual may want to do independently. These ideas require the state (or elites or activists or some other political process) to decide, enable, and build that freedom, often supported by a deontological principle (as Kant did) or a social contract (as in Rousseau). In another corner, we have the classical liberal tradition, which is all about noninterference, arguably the opposite of the progressive notion of liberty. The third corner is nondomination-requiring that no person, norm, or institution should have the ability to dominate others, and individuals and communities should have the capacity for self- government. One deceptively attractive aspect of both progressive and classical ideas on liberty is that they do not confront trade-offs centrally. In the former, the general will knows what to do. In the latter, we could define the individual's private sphere and enshrine noninterference for all choices within this sphere. Trade-offs like the extent of taxation or limits on some people using their wealth to dominate others' choices are not centrally confronted. Trade-offs go beyond economic questions. Even if Adam Smith's invisible hand applied to the economic domain, liberalism is a set of values, and like all ideologies, it imposes its values on people and communities-for example, those of tolerance and education, as I discuss in chapter 5. Hence, liberalism has always had to contend with-and will have to contend with-resistance from those who culturally oppose liberal practices as well as from those who lose out economically from the application of liberal ideas. These two themes will play a central role in my account of the successes and the subsequent crisis of liberalism.

"Freedom from Economic Servitude" Despite the rise of noninterference ideas and progressive conceptions of liberalism, sensibilities associated with nondomination did not simply evaporate. People who worried about creating a better environment for individuals or a better foundation for communities gravitated toward republican ideas, even if they did not typically emphasize their historical and intellectual origins. William Beveridge, a self-described liberal and architect of the British welfare state, powerfully argued that noninterference wasn't enough if poverty, squalor, and destitution still persisted. Liberty required "freedom from economic servitude," as Beveridge's quote in the epigraph emphasizes. It was out of these ideas that he provided the template for postwar reforms such as universal free health care, unemployment insurance, sick pay, pensions, and a minimum wage. These were then embraced and put into practice by the Labour government that people elected on the basis of its promise to build a welfare state. Ideas related to nondomination also became the implicit organizing agenda of social democratic politics in Scandinavia, after their worker parties abandoned Marxism, and for parts of Franklin Delano Roosevelt's policies (see chapters 4 and 7). As we will see in the next chapter, liberalism based on nondomination led to the most spectacular successes in terms of shared prosperity, democracy, and freedoms. From Scandinavia to the rest of continental Europe and the United States, when people wanted to make liberal democracy live up to its promises-shared prosperity, effective public goods provision, and voice for the people-they didn't depend solely on noninterference. They had to mix in some nondomination. But what if, instead of nondomination, they went to full-throated social freedoms and general will? They did that, too. For example, the Bolshevik Revolution claimed to be supporting the common good while implementing the mandates of the true general will, as Marx and Engels laid out in The Communist Manifesto. The result? Let's just say it didn't work out too well. But the temptation remained, and as I argue in chapter 8, other agendas motivated by versions of the general will, including social engineering, aren't entirely different. We have now come full circle. Building useful collective knowledge both in science and in our social lives requires a form of nondomination, and these ideals cannot be met by a vague appeal to the general will or by strict adherence to noninterference. Remaking liberalism requires both a philosophical foundation and a practical blueprint for nondomination. This nondomination should also be fused with self-government at the community level. Liberalism has failed because it has departed from these principles and veered toward Rousseau-style progressivism based on the conceit of social contract. Remaking liberalism is essential if we are to avoid embracing abhorrent authoritarian alternatives. This is, at once, a philosophical challenge and a practical one. But how can liberalism replicate what Ireland achieved on the question of abortion and become accepted by different communities with different values and priorities? To address this question, it is useful to understand where support for, and resistance to, liberalism often comes from.

Who Is Afraid of Liberty? A lot of people, it turns out. Even in tight-knit communities, there are bound to be very different social and cultural preferences. It's naive to expect that any social arrangement could get everybody's wholehearted support. Even more fundamentally, there are political economy reasons to resist liberalism, as there are to resist democracy. Take the problem of the Levant Company, which was granted a monopoly for trade with the Ottoman Empire and the Middle East throughout the seventeenth century in England. The company prospered and at its peak commanded a fleet of over one hundred ships. In 1661, it was granted the ability to make laws pertaining to Mediterranean and Ottoman trade. It could set tariffs, as well as its own prices, and use force to settle disputes. Like all monopolies, the Levant Company increased prices and hogged lucrative opportunities. Why not give others the freedom to compete with the company? If its monopoly had been broken up, English consumers and rival firms would have benefited. Yet it was only in 1825 that the company was finally disbanded. The explanation for the company's remarkable durability lies in political economy: The company's powerful merchants and governors, who stood to lose their attractive profits, were allied with politicians who wielded even more considerable influence. Simply put, a political system that benefited from its existence would not disband the company. It is for similar reasons that political economy will be crucial to the future of liberalism as well. There have been many efforts to bypass political economy in discussions of liberalism or democracy. Perhaps the most well-known is "modernization theory," which claims that democratic and liberal values naturally flower as societies become more prosperous and educated. In the words of the political scientist Samuel Huntington, "Becoming a modern society is about industrialization, urbanization, and rising levels of literacy, education, and wealth." Let this process happen, and democracy and liberal sensibilities will follow. It was this theory that underpinned the common belief in the 2000s that Chinese institutions and culture would become more like those of the West as the country developed economically. Modernization theory was instrumental in forming and bolstering the policies that welcomed China as an emerging power into the world trading system on favorable terms and allowed the rapid transfer of technology thereto. President George W. Bush was channeling modernization theory when he said: "Economic freedom creates habits of liberty. And habits of liberty create expectations of democracy .... Trade freely with China, and time is on our side." Of course, China did become much richer and more educated, but no more democratic. It is arguably less liberal today than it was in the 2000s, with many more restrictions on speech, media, universities, and civil society. Likewise, it is unlikely that Saudi Arabia or Kuwait will turn into liberal democracies as they become wealthier due to higher oil prices. Systematic statistical analysis corroborates these examples, showing no evidence that economic growth makes democracies more likely to emerge or survive. Rather, as I discuss in the next chapter, causality runs the other way around, with democracy influencing economic growth. There is no free lunch-free of politics-when it comes to the origins of and support for liberalism and democracy. Political economy is also part of the reason why democracy is often in peril. Although democracies perform better than autocracies, especially for the less well-off members of society (more on this in chapter 4), democratic institutions are in constant danger, as we know only too well from recent events. Autocracies benefit autocrats and their allies, and weakening democratic norms creates corruption or power- grabbing opportunities for other influential actors. That's not all. Liberalism always creates cultural tensions, because it alters norms and institutions, even when it is fully respectful of the traditions and priorities of different communities-and it sometimes spectacularly fails in paying such respect. We have also seen democracies lose public support if they do not live up to the aspirations they raise and the pledges they make. So there is a social as well as a political economy reason why, from time to time, societies turn to nondemocratic alternatives. If you live by promises of betterment, then you are likely to die by such promises-or their breaking. In sum, the experimentation and dissent that liberalism creates are not to everybody's taste, and political economy and social considerations may abate the enthusiasm for liberalism. This makes it all the more critical for liberalism to be built on a societal agreement-a social compact -rather than defining itself as a set of values that is obviously right and is condoned by some general will or a hypothetical social contract. Social Compact for Liberalism What would a social compact for liberalism look like? I'm using the term "social compact" to emphasize that this shouldn't be justified by an appeal to a social contract that imposes general ethical principles. The basic idea is simple: A social compact for liberalism shouldn't be based on the conceit of presumed agreement in the context of a hypothetical social contract or behind a veil of ignorance. What is the alternative, then? My answer is that a practical social compact should leverage self-government and get buy-in from a significant fraction of the communities making up a society. As the Irish aspired to, a practical social compact should be an "exercise in deliberative democracy" with an acceptance of basic liberal values. "Buy-in" doesn't mean a unanimous agreement, which is neither realistic nor desirable (recall that social learning thrives on variation of viewpoints and approaches). Rather, it should mean that a majority of the people in a society are willing to abide by the principles and institutions of liberalism. What a "significant fraction" should be is something we have to discuss and debate, but realistically, it cannot be 95 percent of all communities or all citizens, and it cannot merely be a plurality. In the spirit of David Hume's quote at the beginning of this chapter, we should approach such things with humility-recognizing our ignorance and fallibility-rather than seeking some ironclad principles. The details of how to secure such buy-in are not fully clear, and will not be unless one experiments with different ways of achieving it (there again, experimentation!). Of course, experimentation itself can be problematic. Does it involve just experimenting with ways of getting consent from different communities for what is done at the national level? At the local level? Just information exchange? Or does it also involve experimenting in such a way that certain practices and values are imposed on communities despite backlash in the short run in order to find out how these reactions will evolve? No free lunch! One factor is important in building buy-in for a social compact. Political economy considerations notwithstanding, the shared understanding and mutual regard that local and national communities inculcate can bolster cooperation. They can convince people to deviate from their strict self-interest and respond to arguments about the common good. People don't need to be behind a veil of ignorance to accept some sacrifice for the common good or to help those who are suffering. In the Irish referendum, it was such arguments based on shared moral principles that convinced a significant majority of the population that the abortion ban, which was not just cruel but dangerous in cases of unwanted pregnancies, should be repealed. In the United States, the federal recognition of same-sex marriage with the Respect for Marriage Act, signed into law in 2022, was the end point of a similar journey. Same-sex marriage was deliberated and received support from people with different backgrounds and ideologies, starting with 2012 referenda in Maine, Maryland, and Washington, once the case for gay marriage was articulated. From there, other states passed similar laws, culminating in the Respect for Marriage Act. Shared moral principles, such as providing the same opportunities to gay couples that straight couples enjoy, are critical for this type of cooperation. One can conceive of the role of political philosophy-including ideas based on treating others as we would want them to treat us, or contractarian arguments, especially those based on liberal principles like John Rawls's-as providing input into building such principles via debate and deliberation. These moral principles can then construct the framework through which further deliberations and agreements on the main outlines of a social compact take place. Liberalism has an advantage in such deliberations, because, as I argued in chapter 2, it has a veritable claim to generate better means for society to achieve its objectives. Note that community and self-government play important roles in this process, as the Irish referendum underscores. Shared moral principles are more likely to emerge when people see themselves as having agency in choosing such principles and when they feel that they are part of both local communities and a broader shared identity. In turn, a shared identity can be rooted in notions of belonging to a higher-level entity, such as citizenship in a republic, or some type of patriotism. In contrast, identities that divide a nation into conflicting and noncompromising parts would make shared moral stances and agreement on a social compact very difficult or impossible. The reference to a republic is important in this context. While autocratic governments can sometimes inculcate strong nationalist feelings, these do not bring diverse communities together. Nor do they serve liberal ideals. In sum, if local community dynamics are consistent with communication and empathy with other communities and a higher-level shared understanding, then they can support compromise and the adoption of policies consistent with some basic moral principles. Political philosophy, and especially liberal philosophy, could become a guide for constructing such moral principles and deliberating about them. The approach to policy that these considerations suggest should go along the following lines: Start with a commitment to soft communitarian aspects of liberalism, so that communities are confident about being heard and being able to participate in self-government to the extent possible; always explain the justifications for the proposed policies and more broadly for liberal principles, including those concerning the building of collective knowledge both for technical and social matters; whenever possible, articulate this case based on the common good (but without using this as an excuse for policies that favor one group over others); seek compromise with different communities on the basis of this common good; prioritize economic growth and the building of institutions for sharing economic growth, so that most communities can receive tangible benefits from the liberal agenda; always remain open to criticisms and different perspectives; always note and take seriously objections; and to the extent possible, incorporate feasible suggestions and amendments so that the commitment to soft communitarianism means something. How these elements can be fused together and the trade-offs navigated should not be viewed as being cast in stone. Liberalism, as I have already emphasized, should be humble and understood as a framework for supporting freedoms, building collective knowledge, and navigating various trade-offs, rather than as a precise set of rules that can be easily applied in all cases. I have stressed the importance of experimentation for social adaptation-how different individuals and communities with different values can live with one another and with our evolving institutions and technologies. It should be no different for liberalism itself. The trade-offs between the communitarian elements of liberalism and protections for individual rights and for the disadvantaged should be decided by a process of self-government, taking into account the implications for collective knowledge and for other priorities. Here, too, humility is central, and liberalism has to learn from different choices and experiments by communities. The principles I advocate in this chapter are not just philosophy. We'll see next that the liberal democracy that succeeded across a range of metrics over the last two hundred years bore the hallmarks I described here-based on nondomination, self-government, community, and deliberation, rather than top-down social engineering.

Chapter 4

Liberal Success

Post-WWII liberalism created unprecedented prosperity, freedoms, and self-government. Shared industrial growth transformed lives through institutional protections and working-class dignity.

▼

Liberal Success

But most of all, the Great Society is not a safe harbor, a resting place, a final objective, a finished work. It is a challenge constantly renewed, beckoning us toward a destiny where the meaning of our lives matches the marvelous products of our labor. -Lyndon B. Johnson

As liberty consists only in being subject to no man's will, and nothing denotes a Slave but a dependence on the will of another; if there be no other Law in a Kingdom than the will of a Prince, there is no such thing as Liberty. -Algernon Sidney

Amid the sweltering Alabama heat in July 1917, Lowndes Adams wrote to his friend Mershell Cunningham Graham in Detroit, "I am sick and tired of this blooming place," citing that "slavery is still existing in the South." But in Detroit, Adams volunteered, "you can breathe a little freedom." He was right. Born to former slaves sometime around 1888 in rural Alabama, Mershell had bleak prospects from the get-go. He seemed destined for a life of poverty, backbreaking sharecropping work, constant harassment, and even violence. Mershell dropped out of school after the eighth grade and would later have to teach himself to read. But Mershell's life was soon transformed. Recruiters from the Ford Motor Company came to Alabama, telling young men like Mershell: "If you come to Detroit, we've got jobs for you." They enticed laborers by offering transport and jobs: "Anybody who's here at 4 o'clock on the train going to Detroit, we have a ticket for you and we'll get you settled up north." Mershell was "so anxious to get out" that right away "he went home, packed his bags ... got on the train." Ford was looking for unskilled workers to fill the many positions that were opening up as the company was expanding production and taking the first steps toward mass production. In the words of Mershell's granddaughter, "The factories were always working, people were working

double shifts .... That allowed for a very middle-class existence for people who were working at those very difficult jobs." Mershell started as a machinist and never looked back. He stayed at Ford for all of his working life. Mershell joined the middle class in part due to liberalism's success-in creating an economic environment in which industries could use new technologies and expand and would pay decent wages to recruit workers, even those with little education. Ford's decision to offer high wages -instead of squeezing workers-grew out of the very society liberalism was building: a system in which labor possessed a voice, enjoyed legal rights, and operated under the watchful eye of democratic oversight (even if achieving this was a lengthy and circuitous process in the U.S.). This turn of fate would echo across generations. Mershell's daughter was able to attend decent public schools in Detroit and subsequently Wayne State University. She became a teacher, an occupation that was expanding as public investment in schooling increased. Mershell's granddaughter, Pearl Cleage, also attended public schools in Detroit, including an all-Black high school, which she later described as "wonderful." It prepared her for college with advanced courses and inspiring teachers. She graduated from Howard University, one of a handful of institutions that aimed to promote social mobility for Black Americans. Pearl loved writing plays. With the growth of privately and publicly funded programs promoting arts and culture, she became a world-famous playwright and a teacher. The arc of Mershell's and Pearl's lives-and thousands of others like theirs that transpired in the decades before and after World War II- would have been unthinkable without the economy and institutions that liberalism built.

As political philosophies go, liberalism is arguably the one that has had the most positive effects on human well-being and flourishing. This chapter, like Mershell's life, tells this story. There are six main categories of successes of liberalism in history, which I will take up in sequence in the rest of the chapter:

· Individual freedoms: Liberalism promoted basic freedoms, so that at least some individuals could express thoughts and make social and economic choices freely. . Self-government: Constrained governments and practices of self-government emerged out of the political economy and ideas of liberalism, so that political power was recognized as resting with the people and laws that they make, and not being completely delegated to a ruler. Those in power, be they monarchs or elected representatives, were considered to be ruling on behalf of the people. They were therefore subject to institutional and legal constraints and had to contend with people's participation in politics. · Public services: Liberalism enabled the rise of capable governments, which used some of their power to provide public services, socially useful law enforcement, and a safety net. . Imagining an end to subjugation: The initiation of the process of liberation for previously subjugated people, including slaves, women, and citizens of nations colonized by Europeans, was, even if delayed, an outgrowth of liberal ideas. · Liberal science: Scientific progress-rapid improvements in scientific knowledge, undergirded by practices to verify and spread that information reliably-was enabled by liberal ideas and institutions. · Gifts of mass production: Liberalism bolstered the twentieth-century industrial compact, whereby economic growth was coupled with shared prosperity and noticeable improvements in the lot of regular people like Mershell. His story is about markets but also about institutions that created a modicum of a level playing field.

I do not mean to suggest that liberalism-and its institutional counterpart, liberal democracy-came close to solving all problems; there is still much more to be done on each of these issues. Nevertheless, the spectacular and broadly shared economic growth, coupled with democratic governance and expanding individual rights in almost the entire industrialized world in the three decades following World War II, was historically unparalleled. This growth experience, as well as the other elements of success listed above, was built on liberal ideas, particularly those associated with nondomination, and a political economy that supported them.

Individual Freedoms The notion that an individual should be free in making his or her choices, even with the weaker notion of noninterference, would not have occurred to many who lived in the millennium after the collapse of the Roman Republic. While the Roman Empire, which followed the republic, preserved the talk of liberty, gone were the days when one could truly defend-as Cicero did-citizen rights, freedoms, and duties of self- government. Then things got even worse. Even the most basic aspects of freedom were hard to come by in medieval Europe-and were rarer still in China or the Middle East. Jan Hus, an impressive theologian who anticipated many of the ideas of Martin Luther and tried to use them to reform the church, was lured by church leadership to the Council of Constance in 1415 (convened to address various heresies and the multiple claims to the papacy) with the promise of getting a hearing. He was duly arrested and executed. The Council of Constance also ordered John Wycliffe, an earlier, deceased critic of the church whose ideas influenced both Hus and Luther, to be exhumed and his remains, together with his books and other possessions, to be burned. William Tyndale, an outspoken scholar who translated the New Testament to English from the Hebrew and Greek texts for the first time, was executed by strangulation for heresy in 1536 near Brussels. He had previously been forced to flee England because of his disagreement with the annulment of Henry VIII's marriage. Wycliffe, Hus, and Tyndale were no exceptions. The number of people executed and killed in battles due to the charge of heresy in the Middle Ages was at least in the tens of millions. Economic freedoms were equally circumscribed. Most of Europe was divided into different classes of people, and each had their role, imposed on them by norms and, if necessary, force. English social life and hierarchy fit this mold. The clergyman William Harrison stated categorically in his Description of England in 1577 that "we in England divide our people commonlie into foure sorts," distinguishing the aristocracy, the middle classes, freeholders of land, and laborers. The first category, "the gentlemen," included those "whome their race and blood or at least the vertues doo make noble and knowne." The middle class was a broad category, including city dwellers of very different occupations (and there were often impregnable boundaries between these occupations). The third were freeholders of land or "yeomen," who had greater prominence and social status because of their property holdings. The fourth included all sorts of laborers, servants, landless peasants, husbandmen, and others, who had, according to Harrison, "neither voice nor authoritie in the common wealthe, but are to be ruled and not to rule other." These different sorts were highly stratified, with a clear sense of who was superior and who was inferior. They obviously had very different lifestyles, but also different manners of dress, different ways they expected to be addressed, and distinct seats reserved for them at church. Giving freedoms of any sort to common men and women would have been the furthest thing from the minds of the gentlemen, one of whom described these common people as "a Beast with many heads" and "always apt to rebel and mutiny ... on the least occasion." Better to keep them under firm grip. And many of England's institutions did just that. Even aid to the poor was a method of social control and was to be given only "on terms which explicitly enjoined behaviour in strict conformity to the mores of the ratepayers." This wasn't simply because the prosperous ratepayers (taxpayers) wanted to avoid mutiny. It was also because they were convinced that horrid social consequences would follow from any freedoms given to the poor, who were "guilty of excessive drinkinge, profane swearing, pilfering and other scandalous crimes or are Vagrants or are Idle Persons." Nobody in the sixteenth century would have thought that liberty was the manifest destiny of the English-or the European people. By the eighteenth century, however, there was much talk of freedoms in North America, England, and Europe, in large part due to the spread of liberal ideas. As early as the middle of the seventeenth century, Colonel Thomas Rainsborough, a commander in Parliament's New Model Army during the English Civil War and a representative of the Levellers, claimed: "I do not find anything in the Law of God that the lord shall choose twenty burgesses, and a gentleman but two, or a poor man shall choose none." Thinkers as varied as Richard Price, Francis Hutcheson, Adam Ferguson, David Hume, Adam Smith, and Thomas Paine took it almost as axiomatic that some form of individual freedom was desirable, even if their ideas and conclusions differed on details. The American Revolution's notions of liberty derived from the same sources. Many of the influential figures of the era viewed ancient Greek and Roman thinkers as their teachers and avidly read English republican texts, such as those by Algernon Sidney, mixed with the writings of Locke and Montesquieu. This informed the Declaration of Independence's reference to "certain unalienable Rights." The French Revolution had many causes, many facets, and even more consequences, but most participants did genuinely believe that it was about "liberty, equality, and fraternity." The first article of the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen, clearly influenced by the American Declaration of Independence, was even more eloquent: "Men are born and remain free and equal in rights." It would be an exaggeration to say that people in Europe and the United States suddenly had plenty of liberty in their personal or economic lives. It would definitely be a fantasy to believe that common laborers had any meaningful freedoms-certainly if freedom is interpreted according to the notion of nondomination. Yet the extent to which such freedoms were recognized, and sometimes even practiced, was still remarkable by the historical standard of the Middle Ages. The evolution of the economic order and the resulting changes in politics enabled this increase in liberty. Without the undoing of feudal bonds and the tight norms of rural life, and without the emboldening of commercial and industrial interests, such liberty would have been impossible. The opening of trade routes to Asia after the rounding of the southern tip of Africa, the Cape of Good Hope, by the Portuguese explorer Bartolomeu Dias in 1487 and the discovery of sea passages to the New World fundamentally altered politics in Europe, especially in England and the Netherlands. In England, for example, because this Atlantic trade was not yet fully monopolized by the monarchy, its rise enriched independent merchants and their domestic allies, who were already in conflict with the crown over issues of taxation and government-granted monopolies. Merchants and overseas colonial interests then pushed for greater individual freedoms (even if mostly for themselves, not for everyone) and unleashed a series of institutional changes bolstering such freedoms. Acceptance of the desirability and feasibility of freedom was also a success of liberalism. People like Jefferson and Madison, who penned the Declaration of Independence and the Bill of Rights in America, and the deputies of the National Constituent Assembly in France were influenced by liberalism. So were many in the middle classes who were receptive to their message.

Self-Government Early attempts in the broad direction of individual freedoms, popular sovereignty, and a little bit of political equality in ancient Athens, the Roman Republic, and the Italian city-states during the Renaissance were commingled with nascent ideas from my corner of the liberal tent. We have already seen the early Roman origins of the notion of nondomination and its dependence on citizens taking part in making the laws that would apply to them. Many of the liberal thinkers of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries thought that individual freedoms and politics more generally ought to be based on a government of laws, backed up by self-government by the people and built on political equality and popular sovereignty. Richard Rumbold, a soldier on the parliamentary side in the English Civil War, later executed for his radical activities, got to the heart of this:

I never could believe that Providence had sent a few men into the world, ready booted and spurred to ride, and millions ready saddled and bridled to be ridden.

The idea of popular sovereignty was most famously articulated and defended by John Locke. As Locke put it, "freedom of men under government is ... common to every one of that society, and made by the legislative power erected in it. A liberty to follow my own will in all things where the rule prescribes not, not to be subject to the inconstant, uncertain, unknown, arbitrary will of another man." "Life, liberty and estate" and their defense "against the injuries and attempts of other men" were critical, Locke argued. All of this had to be undergirded by self-government, as Locke understood when he stated: "No one can be ... subjected to the political power of another, without his own consent." It is noteworthy-and consistent with my interpretation of the central role of self-government-that England's establishment of a government of laws predated the flourishing of individual liberties. A government of laws, in turn, was brought about by similar political economy forces: the disappearance of feudal bonds and the strengthening of independent merchant interests, especially with the rise of Atlantic trade. The first step in the political economy of English self-government was the Civil War of 1642-51, but the establishment of a nonmonarchical, law-based government in that context was at best short-lived. The leader of the parliamentary side in the Civil War, Oliver Cromwell, quickly declared himself lord protector after the war and ruled with dictatorial powers until his death. The constitutional government that emerged after the Glorious Revolution of 1688-89 was more important for the emergence of self-government, even if it did not live up to anything like equality before the law or broad-based political participation and soon made way for a Whig oligarchy of sorts that persisted for much of the eighteenth century. All the same, these experiments with self-government paved the way for more audacious attempts, including in North America, where a major impetus for the uprising against the British was colonists' belief that they had the right to live by laws. This was also the crux of the idea of "no taxation without representation" and thus the American Revolution. Joseph Priestley captured the essence of this issue and the American rebels' perspective when he stated:

By the same power, by which the people of England can compel them to pay one penny, they may compel them to pay the last penny they have. There will be nothing but arbitrary imposition on the one side, and humble petition on the other.

In other words, the problem wasn't that the tea tax (of three pence per pound) was high or onerous. Rather, the imposition of the tax without their consent infringed on the colonists' freedom. It will not escape the reader's notice that this philosophical stance is very close to that embedded in the notion of nondomination: Self-government is critical so that nobody can exercise arbitrary power over others. The tea tax was so incendiary to American colonists because it was a repudiation of the notion of a government of laws, made by self-governing people. The same ideas made their way to the French Revolution. The second article of the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen read:

The aim of every political association is the preservation of the natural and imprescriptible rights of Man. These rights are Liberty, Property, Safety and Resistance to Oppression.

The emergence of a government of laws wasn't just about the balance of power between different sides in England and France. This process was heavily influenced by liberal notions of individual freedoms and nondomination. Liberal ideas were foundational to the constitutional monarchy that emerged in the Glorious Revolution, to the American Constitution and Bill of Rights, to the French Revolution, and to the expansions of democracy in Britain starting after 1832. Those who drafted the key documents, those who inspired the drafters, and the many more who supported them all believed in some version of liberalism. The role of such liberal ideas is visible in what John Adams wrote in a letter to Jefferson:

What do We mean by the Revolution? The War? That was no part of the Revolution. It was only an Effect and Consequence of it. The Revolution was in the Minds of the People, and this was effected, from 1760 to 1775, in the course of fifteen Years before a drop of blood was drawn at Lexington.

To say that a government of laws would not have emerged without liberalism would not be an overstatement. But equally, the story of liberalism cannot be told without democracy-and vice versa.

In fact, throughout the last three hundred years, liberalism's travails were interwoven with the struggle for democracy. This is not to deny the tensions between democracy and liberalism that were so important for Tocqueville and Mill. All the same, as I have already emphasized, for the liberties being upheld to have their full influence on experimentation and discovery of new knowledge, we must think of them as nondomination, including a degree of self-government. Hence, democracy can never be too far from liberty, as Richard Price put it plainly in 1778:

Liberty, therefore, is too imperfectly defined when it is said to be "a government of laws and not by men." If the laws are made by one man, or a junto of men in a state, and not by common consent, a government by them is not different from slavery.

Consistent with this interpretation, liberalism's successes in history are intermingled with democracy. Democracy, not as an absolute ideal but as a process, cannot be separated from the type of economic growth that the United States and a number of European nations have experienced. Britain's economic growth in the nineteenth century would not have taken the form it did, had it not been for the slow march of democratization that started in 1832 with the First Reform Act. French and German industrialization would likely not have overcome many of the barriers that were so apparent in the eighteenth century, had it not been for the destruction of the foundations of the ancien régime in both countries spurred by the French Revolution. In the nineteenth century, illiberal, absolutist European nations such as the Habsburg and Russian Empires resisted industrial technology, railways, and reforms that expanded the market. They lagged in economic growth and maintained deep inequalities, especially in their backward agricultural sectors. Nations that reformed feudal relations and introduced a modicum of democratic participation based on liberal ideas of nondomination were much more likely to industrialize and increase prosperity, both for elites and workers. We see a similar pattern throughout the twentieth century. Democracy is nowadays much maligned, in part because it has not quite lived up to its promises. It is natural for many people to blame it-and liberal democracy in particular-when they see China rapidly build modern infrastructure while the United States is bogged down by intrusive laws, interest groups, and activists. Yet the evidence is clear. Democracy for the last seventy years has not just been the harbinger of basic liberties-including individual freedoms, human rights, the right to participate in politics and express opinions, and greater economic freedoms. It has also brought better economic outcomes. Countries that democratize tend to increase their average income per capita by about 20 percent in the next twenty-five years relative to their trend growth before democratization. The same pattern is true when one focuses on countries that switch to democracy because of regional political waves, such as the collapse of military dictatorships in Latin America from the 1980s onward. Looking at regional waves is useful, because one might worry that countries that democratize are somehow different from those that remain in autocracy, and focusing on their performance relative to nondemocracies might be like comparing apples to oranges. Instead, those caught up in regional waves often have induced switches to democracy without planning or preparation. Systematic analysis of the data shows that such induced democratic experiments do quite well compared to their former autocratic regimes as well. Overall, democracies grow faster, invest more in health and education (especially for the less well-off), provide more public services, and are more likely to pass reforms that foster economic competition. Educational outcomes improve and infant mortality declines sharply following democratization. Conversely, when countries abandon democratic institutions, economic growth slows. A common narrative has emerged over the years that you need authoritarian leaders to launch industrialization. Yet the global evidence doesn't give much support to this story. True, South Korea began industrialization under the repressive regimes of Syngman Rhee and General Park Chung Hee, and Taiwan started it under the tight grip of the Kuomintang, the party of the former Chinese warlord and dictator Chiang Kai- shek. It is also true that Brazil built some new industries under its military dictatorship. But South Korean and Taiwanese growth accelerated rather than slowed down, and these economies became technologically more sophisticated and more competitive after they transitioned to democracy. Brazil also grew more rapidly after the military fell. In many cases, economic growth became more broadly shared after the transition to democracy (though there are also examples in which income inequality increased significantly after democratization, such as in South Africa and Eastern Europe). The charms of liberal democracy have not gone unnoticed by the people. Those who live under democracies that are successful in terms of economic growth, the provision of public services, and limiting inequality report higher support for democratic institutions and less appetite for military or authoritarian regimes. Successful democracies therefore breed their own support. And when democracy's success in these dimensions starts faltering, so does the support it receives from the population.
