The need was particularly acute because many of the workers-to-be were immigrants, who, in the view of the educated native-born population, required even more schooling to become civilized, obedient, and capable. The following passage from The Massachusetts Teacher was typical of the views of many educational reformers in the United States:

Will it, like the muddy Missouri, as it pours its waters into the clear Mississippi and contaminates the whole united mass, spread ignorance and vice, crime and disease, to our native population? If ... we can by any means purify this foreign people, enlighten their ignorance, and bring them up to our own level, we shall perform the work of true and perfect charity, blessing the giver and receiver in equal measure.

William Forster was explicit that the same issues motivated his 1870 Elementary Education Act: "At a time when we have just added millions to the citizens who have the right of electing representatives," it is necessary "to instruct them in the duties of citizenship." These liberal politicians in Britain, France, and the United States were not majorly deviating from the beliefs of liberal intellectuals. Immanuel Kant repeatedly stressed the importance of education and argued that its overriding objective must be to make people "subject to discipline; by which we must understand that influence which is always restraining our animal nature from getting the better of our manhood." Jean-Jacques Rousseau, always ready to sacrifice individual freedom to obedience to some general will, agreed: If there are laws for the age of maturity, there ought to be laws for infancy, teaching obedience to others .... Public education, therefore, under regulations prescribed by the government, and under magistrates established by the Sovereign, is one of the fundamental rules of popular or legitimate government.

Even John Locke, typically wary of impositions on individuals from states and sovereigns, thought that education must be deployed to make people submit to authority, writing, "He that is not used to submit his will to the reason of others, when he is young, will scarce hearken or submit to his own reason, when he is of an age to make use of it." John Stuart Mill largely concurred with this assessment as well and considered public education to be an essential tool for making the masses participate in civic, liberal life. There were others who advocated a different kind of education. The American philosopher John Dewey, for instance, called for a more participatory form of education, based on questioning of evidence and diverse viewpoints, a scientific attitude, and critical thinking (a term that he coined). Avoiding indoctrination was a major selling point for such an approach. Dewey celebrated people who questioned existing ways of thinking and argued, "Every great advance in science has issued from a new audacity of imagination." But Dewey's arguments, though highly respected in intellectual circles and even evaluated in a major 1930 experimental study, remained on the fringe. The common schools, funded and managed by local communities across the United States and championed by Horace Mann, inculcated the values of their communities at the primary education level but did not venture into critical thinking. Nor did the high school movement, which, over the three decades starting in 1910, spearheaded the huge increase in educational attainment in the country. The dilemma of how to educate the ignorant remained. John Stuart Mill and his followers celebrated the possibility that people could deviate from the most established norms with their thoughts and expressions-this was, after all, what liberty and "experiments of living" were about. But the buck stopped with those who did not have education. So, implicitly, there were two standards. If you were well educated and argued that all power structures had to be brought down or even screamed "Down with the system!" that was fine for liberals. They could disagree with you, but you deserved the protection of freedom of expression. But if people with little education shouted the same slogans, that would damage the fabric of liberal society. Dealing with ignorance wasn't confined to schools. If ignorance was an impediment to the realization of a liberal society, then it would be justified to fight it, not just with the education of children but with other forms of instruction and persuasion for adults. Once again, the line where an exchange of ideas ended and imposition started remained blurred. Article 11 of the Declaration of the Rights of Man and of the Citizen was already grappling with this issue when it stated: The free communication of ideas and opinions is one of the most precious of the rights of man. Any citizen may therefore speak, write and publish freely, except what is tantamount to the abuse of this liberty in the cases determined by Law.

Hence, abuses of freedom were recognized and would have to be weeded out, by imposition or other means. The dilemmas here could scarcely have escaped the attention of these formidable early liberal philosophers. The way out of this conundrum for many liberal thinkers and politicians was that they didn't view their brand of education as indoctrination. Even if taught without critical thinking and without much room for questioning and debate, it was fine to impose on children-and thus on future citizens-the values of republic, nation, and liberal society. How else would such a society survive? But what if people didn't want this education? What if they understood it as indoctrination? What if communities opted for different types of schooling with different values? What if education became a dividing line in terms of values and beliefs in society? What if indoctrination came to threaten liberalism's own values, preventing both individual- and community-level experimentation?

Tolerating the Intolerant If issues around education and ignorance were thorny for liberalism, those around tolerance were downright impossible. Repression and violence fueled by intolerance, which Voltaire targeted, were the easy cases. These could never be accepted in a liberal society. But intolerance confined to thought or speech posed a more complicated challenge. Liberalism of all sorts-regardless of whether it is justified by the arguments I made in chapter 2 or others that have convinced liberal thinkers over the ages-celebrates the freedom to think different thoughts, express them, and act upon them. Hence, it embeds a form of tolerance. But what about thoughts and actions that are steeped in the deepest form of intolerance? Liberalism in a Christian society should tolerate those who express non-Christian thoughts, say, those from a different faith. But what if that faith completely refused to tolerate any other type of thought, including Christianity? What if the intolerance in question spilled into a call for suppression of and violence against women or minority groups? These questions become particularly troublesome when dealing with the cage of norms in communities. As I argued in chapter 2, every community has its own restrictive norms. When these norms straddle-or even blatantly penetrate-the boundary of intolerance, how should society handle these communities? Even more troublesome is that some degree of intolerance may be baked into the cake of cohesiveness for communities. As the American philosopher Robert Paul Wolff put it, "The more warmly a man says 'we,' the more coldly will he say 'they.'" On the basis of this, Wolff recognized: "One solution to the problem of intolerance, of course, is to loosen the ties which bind an individual to his ethnic, religious, or economic groups." So where should we draw the line between an attack on the fundamental norms of communities and the quest for tolerance? As we saw earlier in this chapter, John Locke started grappling with these issues in A Letter Concerning Toleration. Despite his great open- mindedness and clarity of thought, the issues remained as thorny after his contribution. Locke was very eloquent when he stated that it was

the Refusal of Toleration to those that are of different Opinions, (which might have been granted) that has produced all the Bustles and Wars that have been in the Christian World, upon account of Religion.

He argued that tolerance was "an absolute and universal right"-except when it came to Catholics! Thomas Jefferson concurred with Locke's call for tolerance, writing, "It does me no injury for my neighbor to say there are twenty gods, or no God. It neither picks my pocket nor breaks my leg." But Jefferson, like Locke, drew the line at those who would threaten the republic. Voltaire was, of course, even more adamant than Locke and Jefferson that the intolerant could not be tolerated. Many modern liberal philosophers have followed in Voltaire's footsteps when he called intolerance the "monster of fanaticism" to be stamped out. One is Karl Popper, whose thinking has many parallels to the ideas I offered in chapter 2. Popper's philosophy was based on openness and the need for new things. It's no surprise, then, that he recognized explicitly how important tolerance was to liberal society:

If we do not tolerate [intolerant minorities], we seem to deny our own principles: we seem to make concessions to intolerance, and so become hypocrites. If we do tolerate them, we may become responsible for ending democracy and toleration.

After much deliberation and careful thinking, however, Popper landed more or less where Voltaire did: "We need not tolerate even the threat of intolerance; and we must not tolerate it if the threat is getting serious." The progressive philosopher Herbert Marcuse went even further in this line, as indicated by the quote at the beginning of this chapter. One difficulty here is that there is no generally agreed-upon definition of intolerance. Take the idea that there is an innate social hierarchy based on race, gender, or IQ. Somebody who subscribes to this view, refusing to engage with any liberal ideas, and who puts their beliefs into practice by mistreating those they view as inferior, could be deemed intolerant. If a community holds these views and inculcates them in its younger members, we could consider this as a form of that community's cage of norms. Yet if liberalism regards this as intolerance and prevents these views from being aired, blurriness sets in. Couldn't members of this community see the liberals as the intolerant force silencing them, as T. S. Eliot humorously captured in his quote at the beginning of this chapter? Couldn't they think that it is liberalism's cage of norms that is being imposed on students and communities?

The dilemmas of liberalism concerning ignorance and intolerance do not have perfect solutions. Liberalism must advocate its values, including tolerance and respect for science and scientific findings. But how it does this is essential. One path leads to social engineering-indoctrination without any room for questioning, critical thinking, or deliberation. Another is suggested by Mandell Creighton in a letter to Lord Acton, when he asserted: "My liberalism admits to everyone the right of his own opinion and imposes on me the duty of teaching him what is best." Here "teaching" means communicating different perspectives in ways that are consistent with critical thinking and respect for different points of view. As I explain further in chapter 10, there are good reasons for Creighton's strategy to be preferred: Liberalism claims (and is right to claim) that its values are better for people and communities, so it should be able to convince people without indoctrination and imposition. Liberalism can do this more effectively when it works with community and self-government, rather than sidelining them and seeing them as its enemies.

"The General Will Is Always Just" In the notion of liberalism I advocate, just like the one harking back to republican ideas, individuals and communities are supposed to be free as they engage in their own governance, so long as in the process they do not violate the basic individual freedoms of their members and others. The problem of indoctrination is partly a problem of lack of self-government. When adults and children are given information and are encouraged to question it, this is a form of self-government-they have agency in forming their beliefs. When people are indoctrinated, there is no self-government. The ideal of self-government becomes even clearer if we consider the problem from the perspective of communities, not just individuals. Self-government for a community means its members can participate in local rules and decisions and have input into those made at a higher level of government that then impact their community. It doesn't mean that the community's wishes cannot be overridden. In a federal structure with multiple, diverse local communities, there may be irreconcilable differences, for example, concerning national defense or other countrywide decisions. Self-government in this case requires deliberation and input from all communities-not strict adherence to the exact wishes of each community. When it comes to issues of inclusion, ignorance, and intolerance, the same principle should apply. Communities should have input on whether their traditions are to be ignored or sidelined (within the boundaries set by liberal society; see chapter 10). Self-government can also be very useful indirectly in this endeavor: It can enable communities to crystallize their preferences and allow them to communicate their input in a more effective form to state or national governments. Yet this type of thinking, based on the principles of self-government, hasn't been sufficiently embraced by liberals. I believe this is because contractarian ideas, tracing back to Rousseau, have been more influential. These have pushed for a seemingly more straightforward approach to issues of ignorance and intolerance. Contractarian interpretations tend to see liberalism as being underpinned by a social contract, meaning that somehow people have given their consent to liberal society. If so, liberal principles-however they are interpreted by politicians or leading cadres-can be imposed against those temporarily dissenting from them or refusing to abide by their most defining aspects. In sum, in the progressive liberal tradition going back to Rousseau, the strong tendency has been toward action, including social engineering if necessary, to bring everybody to the level of tolerance and wisdom that liberal society encapsulates (or pretends to encapsulate). As Rousseau anticipated:

It follows from what has been said that the general will is always just and always aims at the common good; but it does not follow that the deliberations of the people are always right. Man always aims at his good, but he does not always know what it is.

Nevertheless, the dilemmas of liberalism are true dilemmas, meaning that they do not have one-size-fits-all solutions. Self-government is critical because it enables people themselves-individually, at the community level, and beyond-to navigate the trade-offs. If self-government is sidelined, dilemmas become much more fraught. Of course, self-government is not a panacea either, because it can intensify communities' cage of norms and sometimes be at odds with the most basic freedoms of certain individuals. But it is the most obvious starting point for a liberalism committed to experimentation and community.

Dilemmas of a Changing World Liberalism always presented dilemmas. But the social changes of the last half century have brought them to a boiling point. This is for several reasons. First, we have been living through a period of rapid cultural and social change, which means positions that were nearly universally held in the past may subsequently be considered intolerant. For example, according to the General Social Survey, between 1973 and 1991, consistently around 80 percent of Americans reported that homosexual relations were "always wrong" or "almost always wrong." By 2010, this number was down to 49 percent and then fell further to 36 percent by the end of the decade. Support for gay marriage was only 22 percent as recently as 1988 but has since increased to 72 percent. There are two remarkable things about these numbers. One is how quickly they have changed, making society much more tolerant regarding a very important issue for the lives of millions of people. But equally remarkable is how many in the United States are still uncomfortable with homosexuality or gay marriage. Today, many liberals agree-as do I- that this is an intolerant position. The majority position of the previous generation has thus become a marker of bigotry, meaning that people who have essentially the same views as their parents would now be considered intolerant, while their parents held the consensus opinion of their time. Would Voltaire and Popper's position of trying to extinguish intolerance still apply in this case? Second, inclusion made matters more complicated. Many more and much more diverse groups are now part of liberal societies. Over the last three decades, the inflow of migrants from very different cultures and religions, such as those emigrating from the Middle East to Europe, has created an additional fault line. Immigrant communities have to be cohesive to survive, and the fact that they often live in tight-knit neighborhoods, sometimes even in enclave-like conditions, solidifies this cohesiveness. Although an exact comparison is difficult, the cultures of Middle Eastern immigrants seem more dissimilar to those of their European hosts, relative to the case of southern European immigrants to the United States in the early twentieth century, whose inclusion generated such troubling backlash, as we have seen. On the one hand, it is a strength of these immigrant communities that they have strong norms and religious beliefs and practice. For example, in France, North African immigrant communities show significantly stronger rates of intergenerational transmission of religious beliefs than other groups. While only about 67 percent of children raised by two Catholic parents in France later identify as Catholic, 91 percent of those raised by two Muslim parents identify as Muslim. This pattern appears to have become more pronounced during the last two decades. The share of both first-generation and second-generation immigrants from North Africa wearing a veil increased considerably between 2008 and 2019 (from 23 percent to 37 percent for first-generation immigrants and 12 percent to 16 percent for second-generation immigrants in France). Exactly the same norms, however, create stifling cages for their members, especially women. They also conflict with the tolerance of native- born Westerners toward other minorities. For example, the pressure for young Muslim girls to veil has been a lightning rod for many secular liberals in Europe. When perceived as a heavy imposition on the most basic freedoms of girls and women, the veil is an example of huge intolerance. When viewed as a choice of Muslim women and girls, the tables are turned-or so it may appear. Isn't it intolerant for those outside these communities to interfere with the free choices of women to wear a veil or a burka? Isn't it their choice if they want to be in a subservient position to their husbands or brothers? Veiling and associated practices are the tip of the iceberg. The norms of communities that encourage or impose veiling are highly patriarchal, reducing women's power and ability to participate in economic activity (and even sometimes to have their own social networks). Veiling may be intertwined with such impositions. Education is another fault line. It should be a redline if, in the name of tradition and preservation of their way of life, communities prevent the education of their youth. But what about if they opt for their own educational system, with values that conflict with liberal education? Is this justified if all community members, including the young, express a preference for this type of education? Reality is complex because these choices, like many others, are made in the cage of norms. Ostracism and exclusion from the community are there if people deviate from these behaviors. Often, the threat of violence is likewise nearby if one attempts to break out of the cage too forcefully. All of this is in turn bolstered by the indoctrination of women via the family and the community. This implies that when women choose to veil or decide not to work outside of the home, this is a choice-but it may be a choice made within a semicoercive environment and against a background of intense indoctrination starting in one's childhood. The situation is even starker when it comes to some of these communities' attitudes toward gays, Jews, or atheists. So where should we draw the line on these groups' intolerance? On their indoctrination of the next generation? Is there a pecking order, with indoctrination that serves liberal values being preferred over that of illiberal attitudes? I discuss in greater depth how liberalism may try to navigate these dilemmas in chapter 10. For now, I should note that dealing with the reactions of native-born communities to immigrants is equally challenging. As we have seen earlier in this chapter, native-born communities are sometimes eager to exclude newcomers, and all the more so when the newcomers look different, speak different, and believe different. How do we even begin dealing with such intolerance? Is intolerance justified when it is fueled by reference to the even more acute intolerance of immigrant communities? Or is that just a fig leaf hiding intolerance toward new groups? The third reason liberalism's dilemmas have recently come to a boiling point is that many of these social changes have coincided with economic turbulence and insecurity, driven by technological changes and globalization. Economic threats may send communities in search of scapegoats-or at the very least, may make them less generous of spirit. Globalization affects not just jobs but also culture-by facilitating the emergence of new, cosmopolitan values and priorities, especially in major cities, embracing new global connections and allegiances. Over the last half century, globalization has helped sever the links between cultural elites and many of the communities that needed to be convinced to tolerate and learn to live with various social changes. It also opened the door to demagoguery from far-right leaders such as Marine Le Pen in France that "rampant globalization is ... endangering our civilization." Compromise and adherence to principles of self-government became much more challenging. Finally, these dilemmas could stay in the background when liberalism had a clear and urgent mission, contesting and trying to constrain power in the nineteenth century, and when it became the rallying cry against the authoritarian abuses of the first half of the twentieth century. Questions of ignorance and intolerance paled in comparison to expanding the franchise or battling against Mussolini, Hitler, and Stalin. This was no longer true in the second half of the twentieth century, when much of the industrialized world became organized around principles of liberal democracy, and the atrocities of Nazism and communism receded from the collective memory. The dilemmas were much more severe for establishment liberalism than they could ever be for liberalism in opposition.

The dilemmas of liberalism were thus already set for a showdown in the 1980s. Norms and values were in flux. The groups with the most progressive values and who were most willing to challenge existing traditions and intolerance were now ascendant. And all of this would play out against the background of increasing inequalities in the postindustrial economy, new turbulences, globalization, and changing social hierarchies. The dilemmas that once were found only on the desks of philosophers were now causing tangible cracks in the coalition that had come together to support liberal democracy in the West.

Chapter 6

Pathway to Crisis

Lancaster, Ohio exemplified mid-century industrial prosperity where workers and families thrived together. Deindustrialization shattered this social compact, leaving communities abandoned.

▼

Pathway to Crisis

The concept of a post-industrial society is not a picture of a complete social order, it is an attempt to describe an axial change in ... social structure. -Daniel Bell

If machinery could do all the work that labour now does, there would be no demand for labour. Nobody would be entitled to consume any thing who was not a capitalist, and who could not buy or hire a machine. -David Ricardo

In its thirtieth-anniversary issue in 1947, Forbes magazine featured quaint Lancaster, Ohio, as the "all-American town." Lancaster had it all. A dynamic glassmaker, Anchor Hocking, offered good jobs. The city boasted a vibrant community to boot, and the residents were plainspoken and down-to-earth people. Workers and managers lived in the same neighborhoods, fraternized and went to church together, and sent their kids to the same school. Anchor Hocking and another glassmaker, Lancaster Lens, bolstered the community not just because of the stable jobs they offered but also by contributing to the life of the town in every way, so much so that Lancaster came to be known as the "glass city." "Anchor wives," the spouses of the workers in the glass companies, were the ones organizing vaccination drives, demanding improved sidewalks, and getting involved with schools and after-school activities. But Forbes also had this to say about Lancaster's residents:

They sometimes wonder what would happen if the glass factories moved away, but there is no reason for thinking that they will and as long as they remain and continue to manufacture low-cost glass tableware Lancaster can look forward to a relatively even economy that will not be unduly disturbed by either war or depression.

Alas, the factories did move away. The glass city proved to be as fragile as the name suggested. By 1980, Anchor Hocking's exports faced fiercer competition in overseas markets, and foreign glass also started competing against domestic wares in the United States. This was the backdrop against which Carl Icahn, the famous corporate raider, made an offer to acquire the company. The offer did not succeed, but it was a sign of things to come. Anchor Hocking started a restructuring effort, and ultimately another outside conglomerate, Newell Company, bought it up. Both office and factory workers were laid off by Newell, and new equipment was introduced to replace tasks that experienced workers used to perform. Jobs at the glassmakers now paid less and were much scarcer. Suddenly, the bedrock of Lancaster's cohesive community was no longer there. The relationship between Anchor Hocking and the town changed fundamentally. Between 1987 and 2004, the company had six CEOs, but none lived in Lancaster or had any links to its local community. In the words of a union worker at Anchor: "It wasn't like the old days, when Anchor was just Anchor, and your kids went to school with [managers'] kids." In the absence of stable jobs and with managers and other high-income residents moving out, social problems started multiplying. Crime increased and the town, like many others in the Midwest, was overrun and ripped apart by heroin. Lancaster's story since the mid-1980s illustrates not just how the shared-prosperity model that existed in the decades following World War II came undone in many small towns but also the toll this took on community life and morale.

-

This chapter discusses how, throughout the industrialized world, the emergence of a postindustrial economy created tensions similar to those Lancaster experienced. I start by explaining how the industrial compact outlined in chapter 4 was already fragile well before the rise of digital technologies. Manufacturing played a central role in the shared prosperity the compact delivered. But computers and automation reduced manufacturing employment and undid this compact, with other sources of sectoral reallocation, globalization, and monopolization of the economy playing a supporting role. What followed was a postindustrial economy-more automated, more service-centered-with a different geography of jobs and a very different politics. As Daniel Bell anticipated, it was already making strides toward a very different social structure. I conclude by explaining why the changes I am describing in this chapter were not preordained, and different choices about technology and institutions could have been made.

The Industrial Compact The industrial compact of the postwar years wasn't quite as harmonious or stable as Forbes implied. The presence of labor unions meant that disagreements and strikes were frequent occurrences. A Life magazine article in December 1946 featured the drop head "a major US problem: labor." To put this in perspective, there were an average of 395 strikes involving more than one thousand workers annually in the early 1950s (as compared to about 15 in the late 2010s). From management's perspective, weakening unions was a boon, not a bug. Fortune magazine's claim in 1946 that "the threat and promise of laborless machines is closer than ever" was music to the ears of many business owners and managers. Democracy, which bolstered the whole compact, could also malfunction or be slow. By the late 1940s, simmering disagreements papered over by war were resurfacing. McCarthyism and all of the ugly politics of Left and Right were just around the corner. At that time, many Americans still felt there was little real gap between Democrats and Republicans. Many identified with Gore Vidal's quip "There is only one party in the United States, the Property Party ... and it has two right wings: Republican and Democrat." A famous theory of politics, formulated by the economist Anthony Downs, predicted that electoral competition between the two parties would make both converge on the same platform. To some, this was stability. To others, it highlighted the paucity of choices open to Americans. In our more polarized age, many would be envious of these earlier times, and Downs's model is now mentioned more as an example of soundly refuted social science theories. European democracies generated different dynamics, since their proportional representation system did not give rise to a two-party system. The refrain there was more often about unstable coalition governments or industrial strife with the more militant trade unions. But complaints about politics were as common in Europe as they were in Anglo-Saxon countries with majoritarian electoral systems. There were also frequent economic downturns. The United States had official recessions in 1948-49, 1953-54, 1957-58, 1960-61, and 1969-70 before the much more disruptive 1973-75 economic crisis, triggered by the tripling of crude oil prices in a three-year span. Economic ups and downs were even more pronounced in continental Europe and the U.K. for much of the second half of the twentieth century. In the 1970s, Europe followed the U.S. into deep recession and equally troubling stagflation-a toxic combination of high inflation and high unemployment. Nevertheless, most Americans believed the economy would still improve in the long run. For example, between 1983 and 1991, almost two- thirds of families reported that they believed their children's lives would be better than their own. Americans had come to believe that economic betterment was their manifest destiny. That was the heart of the industrial compact-businesses would prosper, but some of this would be shared with labor, because companies needed to hire more employees to expand and produce for the mass market. As productivity went up, so did wages. By the 1980s, however, this compact was already coming apart at the seams due to powerful technological and economic forces. We just weren't aware of it. By the time most people woke up to these changes, the world had already become postindustrial. With the collapse of the industrial compact and the shared prosperity it bolstered, the political economy of liberalism was derailed. A major part of this collapse was related to deindustrialization-the disappearance of industry jobs because of automation brought by digital technologies, rapid globalization, and secular changes in the sectoral composition of American and European economies. With these changes, the geography of economic activity shifted away from smaller cities, further straining the recipe that had previously bolstered community and delivered wage growth for workers of different skills and vocations. All of this culminated in a major political and social upheaval, activating the crisis of liberalism.

The Manufacturing Anchor Manufacturing played a major role in the pre-1980 economy, for both economic and political reasons. In 1953, the share of American workers employed in manufacturing was 32 percent. From then on, it was a steady and then an accelerated decline. Today it stands at 9 percent. Manufacturing isn't only core to the economy as the producer of all physical goods and machinery on which we depend. It also anchors key elements of the industrial compact: mass production, higher wages, technological progress, and trade unions. We can think about certain services produced for the mass market, such as streaming or entertainment. But services generally involve greater customization, and streaming is no exception. What makes Netflix and similar platforms so appealing is that they enable people to pick and choose what to watch. As of December 2023, Netflix offered more than eighteen thousand movies and shows to subscribers. Customization in manufacturing has come a long way since the days of Henry Ford's Model T (only in black!), but it still doesn't compare to the degree of customization in health care, education, or personal services. The T-shirts, shoes, and electronics that we buy have been produced for a large customer base in big batches (handmade, customized Ferraris are the exception that proves the rule). In a hospital, physicians, nurses, and technicians attend to the specific problems and symptoms of each patient separately. Mass production has been mostly a manufacturing phenomenon for the entire twentieth century. In the 1950s, manufacturing paid 22 percent higher wages than other sectors. Manufacturing was also special in offering secure and well- remunerated jobs to workers without a college degree. For noncollege workers, manufacturing jobs paid an even larger premium relative to other sectors than for college workers and were also distinctively secure. "Lifetime jobs" coexisted in the U.S. labor market with a high degree of churn (meaning that some other workers were losing, changing, and getting jobs frequently). In 1983, there were about ten million workers with a high school degree or less who had been in the same job for more than fifteen years. About three million of these were in manufacturing. Manufacturing is also a hub of research and development and innovation activity. New techniques that reduce costs and improve the efficiency of production have been much more important for manufacturing than for services. The equipment for making cars, electronics, or machinery is completely different from what it was four decades ago. Take the automotive sector. In the 1950s, it relied on extensive human labor for thousands of tasks scattered over vast factories. Ford's Rouge complex, for example, occupied a sprawling 2,000 acres in Dearborn, Michigan. The complex housed 120 miles of conveyors and 100 miles of internal railroad tracks to move material and semifinished products around internally. As of 1948, it employed 100,000 people. Many of these workers performed short, repetitive manual tasks, each typically taking no more than a few minutes. A fraction of these jobs relied on "able- bodied men" because of their physical and strength requirements. There was already advanced equipment in the 1950s, including overhead cranes, pneumatic tools, and hydraulic presses, but much of production still required unskilled and semiskilled workers to do things by hand. Developing a new car part, however small, was a complex and fraught activity, requiring hours of manually drafting a new design, followed by craft manufacturing of the new part and finally testing, which could send the whole team back to the drawing board. Today, car factories are unrecognizably transformed. Robots and other automated equipment are everywhere, performing the majority of previously manual jobs, including welding, painting, sealing, assembly, and some heavy lifting and carrying tasks. Maintenance, quality control, and safety checks are thoroughly digitalized. So is new-part design, which presently requires not much more than an engineer working with computer-assisted design equipment. Several carmakers have introduced artificial intelligence tools, not just in their back offices but also for quality control on the factory floor. While new equipment is also widespread in services such as education, health, and retail, its effects have generally been more limited because service sectors are more labor-intensive. For instance, the current share of labor in manufacturing value added is 46 percent. This means that out of every $100 of revenue generated in manufacturing, $46 is paid to labor, while the remaining $54 goes to capital (including payments to debt holders and profits to owners). The share of labor is much higher in services: about 84 percent in health and 90 percent in education. The greater share of labor in services reflects the crucial role that labor plays in these activities and limits the possibility of using machinery to increase output. Ensuring high-quality services is another challenge, especially when production is scaled up. The labor intensity of production, need for customization, and concerns about quality have been limiting factors for productivity growth in services. The data bear this out. Inflation-adjusted annual growth of output per (quality-adjusted) hour worked-"labor productivity"-in manufacturing averaged 2.2 percent between 1950 and 1980. In health and education, average labor productivity growth during the same time period was considerably slower: only 0.75 percent in health and 1.4 percent in education. Manufacturing is distinctive in terms of industrial politics as well, because it brings together large numbers of people working physically and sharing solidarity in relatively stable jobs. This facilitates organization, as eighteenth-century writer and radical John Thelwall noted: "Every large workshop and manufactory is a sort of political society, which no act of parliament can silence, and no magistrate disperse." Indeed, in the 1950s, the share of unionized workers in manufacturing was 51 percent. In the rest of the private sector, it was 30 percent. Unionization also spreads from manufacturing to the broader economy. For example, nonmanufacturing workers, such as nurses, are more likely to engage in collective bargaining when there are manufacturing labor unions nearby, encouraging their organization. Unionization emanating from the manufacturing sector has broader political implications as well. Recent research shows that countries with a greater share of manufacturing employment over the last two centuries have had better-organized labor movements and, largely as a result of this, have democratized faster and with greater stability. None of this means that the space left vacant by the disappearance of high-paying, stable manufacturing jobs cannot be filled by others in services in the future. It is possible to have service jobs for noncollege workers that are stable, pay decent salaries, and encourage them to have voice via labor organizations, as I discuss in chapters 9 and 10. But the reality is that such jobs don't currently exist. It shouldn't come as a surprise, then, that when American manufacturing employment declined, so did prospects for shared prosperity. In contrast to the robust growth of median wages between 1949 and 1973 (see chapter 1), from the late 1970s onward, median wages all but stopped growing-increasing only 0.45 percent per year, even though labor productivity in the economy continued to rise, with an annual average growth rate of over 1.5 percent from 1980 to the present. Inequality has also worsened since the late 1970s. Workers with postgraduate degrees still enjoy reasonable earnings growth. Between 1979 and 2007, just before the financial crisis, their inflation-adjusted wages grew by about 1 percent every year, on average. Meanwhile, during the same time period, men with a high school degree or less saw their wages fall almost every year. The wage gap between workers with a college degree and those with a high school diploma increased from 56 percent in 1979 to 92 percent by 2007. Simultaneously, the distribution of income between capital and labor became significantly more unequal. In 1980, the share of labor in U.S. national income was 58 percent, with the rest going to capital. Since then, the national share of labor has fallen to 52 percent and the share of capital has increased to 48 percent. As the pay of top executives and people with highly specialized expertise, such as computer programmers, surgeons, and top lawyers, surged, and as returns to capital income rose, the share of the wealthiest households also skyrocketed. The share of national income earned by the richest 1 percent of U.S. households rose from around 10 percent in 1980 to a striking 19 percent in 2019. The share of the more elite 0.1 percent shows an even more remarkable rise-from 3.4 percent to 8.8 percent. Manufacturing and wage trends are not one and the same, and there isn't a simple line from manufacturing decline to inequality or low wages. But there are important connections. I also caution that my emphasis in this chapter on the contrast between workers without a college degree and those with a college or postcollege degree is a major simplification. Not all noncollege workers are the same. Some have very valuable artisanal skills, while others excel in entrepreneurship. The heterogeneity among college workers is even greater. American workers from the Ivy League and other highly prestigious universities have much higher earnings than those who graduate from state universities or for-profit colleges. Those in STEM (science, technology, engineering, and mathematics) also tend to outearn humanities and social science graduates. And workers with postgraduate degrees earn a sizable premium relative to those with just a college degree. But the college/noncollege comparison is parsimonious, and the fact that it has become so common reveals the way in which American and European society has divided itself between these two groups.

"No Demand for Labour" The industrial economy was transmuted by several interrelated changes, which I discuss in this and the next several sections. I begin with the most consequential change: advances in digital technologies, which prompted many companies to install computers, computerized equipment, and then more advanced digital tools, such as industrial robots and software systems. I then discuss how the same technological changes transformed the sectoral nature and the geography of the economy. Computers have had three distinct effects on the economy. First, early office computers required workers with numeracy and broader cognitive skills and more than basic education; they enabled mainly the college educated to become more productive and perform new tasks. Hence, computers are what economists call a "skill-biased" technology-meaning that they help skilled, educated workers more than the unskilled or the semiskilled. This kind of technological change can boost inequality and unleash a range of additional effects. While this has been a piece of the story, it is not the main driver of the epochal change in industrial society. The second effect is closely related: Computers and the associated information revolution increased the importance of managerial and administrative roles, as the need for technical occupations expanded. Computers also generated more data and increased demand for people who could process this data en masse-in practice, those who had college education, especially in science, technology, quantitative fields, and business. The third impact was most transformative: Digital technologies became a fertile platform for automation. By "automation" I mean the substitution of technology (machinery or algorithms) for tasks previously performed by labor. Automation plays an important role in wage and employment determination. It tends to sideline workers by reducing the tasks that rely on labor, and as the economist David Ricardo anticipated two centuries ago in the quote at the beginning of this chapter, if automation is excessive, there may be "no demand for labour" left. As a result, automation decreases the share of income that labor, as opposed to capital, captures. Indeed, we see a much more pronounced rise in the share of capital in value added in industries that have invested more in automation technologies. Automation is not a recent phenomenon. The early stages of the Industrial Revolution in Britain were fueled by the automation of textile production, and power looms displaced skilled handloom weavers in the early nineteenth century. New machinery caused wages of weavers to fall to about one-third of their original value. Charles Babbage, a leading British mathematician and inventor, was already dreaming of general- purpose automation tools in the middle of the same century, though his hopes ultimately came up short during his lifetime. Automation that could take manufacturing tasks away from workers got a big boost from digital tools that allowed flexible programming of machinery, such as numerically controlled drills. This type of automation was critical to businesses interested in cutting costs by reducing both employment and their dependence on labor. Numerically controlled machinery was a huge breakthrough, even though most such machines were initially just existing tools augmented with a control mechanism to move their motors according to instructions fed from punch tapes. As digital computers advanced, these control mechanisms improved significantly, and new machinery custom-made for numerical and then computer numerical control (CNC) was introduced. By the 1960s, numerically controlled mills, lathes, cutting tools, spindle systems, presses, and drills had become industry standard. Nevertheless, numerical control has clear limits, because machines cannot operate autonomously and it is feasible only for simple, repetitive tasks that can be performed in a stable environment. For instance, numerical control couldn't perform most of the complex manual tasks, such as welding, painting, and fitting, that were ubiquitous in most manufacturing sectors. This phase was followed by software systems that automated various office tasks and activities related to inventory control, recordkeeping, and planning. Another turning point came with robots capable of performing manual tasks. These robot dreams-and nightmares of dangerous humanoid machines-go back to antiquity and were the talk of the town for much of the first half of the twentieth century. But in the minds of post-World War II managers, robots were an attractive means to automate work and reduce reliance on workers. Globally, there were 0.6 industrial robots per one thousand industrial workers in 1993. This number rose to about 3.5 per one thousand by 2014. The surge in robotics investment in the United States was similarly pronounced. In 1993, the United States had about 3.5 robots per thousand industrial workers. It increased to 15 in 2014, and by 2020 it had already reached 25.5. Manufacturing industries such as automobiles, metals, petrochemicals, electronics, and machine tools were fundamentally transformed by robots. Automation is central to my account, because it severs the link between mass production and shared prosperity. Recall that in the industrial compact, as companies reached larger markets and increased their production volume, they also hired more workers, generating both jobs and, via labor market competition, higher wages. With automation, this latter link was broken. Firms could expand and output could multiply without any need to hire more workers. Worse, existing employees could be laid off, and workers specialized in now-automated tasks would see their earnings and career prospects falter. Which is exactly what happened in the U.S. The labor share in value added in the manufacturing sector declined from 74 percent in 1981 to 46 percent in the mid-2010s (much larger than the decline in the aggregate economy that I mentioned previously, which was from 58 percent to 52 percent). This change was not uniform, either. While some manufacturing industries, such as apparel, had stable labor shares, the industries that were at the forefront of new robot installations, such as motor vehicles, chemical products, electrical equipment, and primary and fabricated metals, had sharper drops in their labor share and also cut down employment. The effects of the decline in the labor share were not equal across all employees. Manufacturing used to employ workers with diverse skills in jobs ranging from blue-collar to technical occupations and back-office, engineering, and managerial positions. The burden of automation fell on blue-collar employees, and especially those engaged in tasks that were being automated by advanced digital equipment and industrial robots. These workers were much more likely to lose their jobs-or experience significant wage declines if they kept them. Recent estimates suggest that between 50 and 70 percent of the surge in U.S. wage inequality over the last four decades can be accounted for by the effects of automation, and that much of the decline in the wages of low-education demographic groups, such as young men with a high school degree or less, can be explained by the spread of automation tools. The outcome was nothing short of the disappearance of good jobs for workers without a college degree. Nowhere is this clearer than in the automotive industry. U.S. car companies used to be at the forefront of offering good jobs to noncollege workers, providing a clear pathway to upward economic mobility and the American dream. The sector paid about 40 percent higher wages to Americans without a college degree than the rest of the economy. This completely changed after the 1980s. While in the 1960s the auto industry employed 625,000 workers with a high school degree or less, by 2010 this number was down to 371,000. Wages and benefits were also much less generous. This was largely because industrial robots and other sophisticated automated machinery were now performing the tasks that noncollege workers used to do, including painting, welding, precision work, assembly, and other heavy manual activities. Another distinctive feature of automotive companies was their willingness to train employees without a college degree so that they could perform the necessary tasks. Since the 1980s, U.S. auto producers have cut back on hiring and training noncollege workers and, just like many other industries, are seeking more qualified and more highly educated applicants. Blue-collar and clerical jobs in other industries were rapidly automated as well. These jobs, which used to provide opportunities for upward mobility to people from disadvantaged backgrounds, have been the main target of automation by robots and software throughout the U.S. economy. In the 1970s, 37 percent of U.S. workers were employed in "middle-class" occupations-defined as occupations in the middle third of the distribution ranked according to average pay and including most blue-collar and clerical jobs. By 2016, this number had fallen to 29 percent. As these jobs disappeared throughout the economy, so did many of the opportunities for workers without a college degree. Workers who used to occupy these jobs have been pushed toward lower-paying positions, such as construction work, cleaning, or food preparation. Automation spelled the end of the industrial compact. Nevertheless, it raised productivity by substituting cheaper machinery for more expensive labor. As a result, even as noncollege workers were left behind, the economy continued to grow. Furthermore, workers performing tasks that were complementary to new machinery-especially those in managerial, design, and engineering occupations or those with postgraduate education-did quite well. So did manufacturing productivity. From 1980 to 2007, just before the financial crisis, annual labor productivity growth in manufacturing was, on average, 3.9 percent, compared with 2.2 percent in the decades before. The scene was set for a major decoupling between productivity and wages of college graduates on the one hand and the economic fortunes of workers without a college degree on the other.

Cost Disease and Manual Work If there is one economic term that you may not have heard of but you should have, it is "Baumol's cost disease," which has been integral to the emergence of the postindustrial economy. This notion, named after the economist William Baumol, goes like this: Imagine households want to consume two goods, cars and health care, and they would like to do this in a balanced manner. Too much health care and no cars would be no good. Nor would it be desirable to consume many cars but no health care. Now imagine what happens if there are rapid productivity improvements in the automotive industry but no productivity growth in health care. The output of the automotive industry will increase, and so consumers can have more cars. But then they would want more health care as well. If there is no reallocation of labor from car manufacturing to health care, the wishes of the consumers will be frustrated. The result is an increase in the price of health care services. This change in prices will ultimately cause a reallocation of labor away from the automotive sector toward health care, but such reallocation can never reverse the rise in the cost of health care. Hence the name "cost disease"-this process will always drive up health care prices and reduce the welfare of consumers relative to the scenario in which health care costs remained constant. The cost disease has much to do with health care being expensive around the world (though it is particularly expensive in the United States for reasons that go beyond Baumol's theory). Baumol's cost disease impacts services, such as health care and education, for several reasons. First, as we have seen, there are many ways of increasing productivity in manufacturing using digital automation, while only a few tasks in health care and education can currently be automated. Although some parts of financial services, insurance, and real estate have been automated, the extent of this is minuscule relative to automation in industrial sectors. This is not just because education, health care, and other services are highly labor intensive. It is also because many of the activities in these sectors are nonroutine, require social interactions, need a high degree of customization, and involve judgment and complex decision-making. These considerations limit the extent to which increasing investment in machinery can raise productivity in services. Maintaining quality is also a challenge when expanding services. This sets the stage for Baumol's cost disease. From 1980 to today, there has been little labor productivity growth in health and education, while manufacturing productivity growth has been very strong, as we have just seen. Concurrently, manufacturing employment has fallen by 7.5 million, while employment in health care has risen by more than 14 million. Education, financial services, and administration have added another 9 million jobs over the same time period. My description and the numbers in the previous paragraph may suggest that all that matters is the reallocation of total employment between sectors. In practice, however, education and health care, as well as financial services and administration, demand workers with specialized expertise and often higher education, such as doctors, professional managers, lawyers, and employees with strong quantitative skills. As a result, while workers who are being let go from manufacturing have lower education, the expanding service sector looks for workers with higher education. This adds to the growing demand for more educated employees and to the potential decline in the real wages of those without college education. Baumol's cost disease therefore acts as an amplifier of the inequalities between college-educated and noncollege workers. This, too, can be seen in the data. The share of manufacturing workers who have a college degree was 12 percent in 1980, compared to 25 percent in health care and 54 percent in education. Since then, the three numbers have increased to 34, 50, and 66 percent, respectively. They confirm that workers shed by manufacturing tend to have less education, but there isn't much demand for these workers from services, which have become even more college-heavy during the same period. Baumol's cost disease is not a new phenomenon. William Baumol wrote about it in 1965, and it has been going on for longer than that. But like computers and automation, it took on a bigger role during the last several decades in part because of rapid productivity growth in manufacturing coming from digital technologies. The cost disease further contributed to the erosion of shared prosperity and became a central part of the postindustrial economy. The premise of Baumol's cost disease is that as productivity growth diverges between the automotive and health care industries, consumers still want to have a balanced consumption basket of products from these two sectors. In reality, when consumers become more affluent-as they have done over the last century-they also tend to shift their desired consumption toward services, adding to the effects of the cost disease. Some goods-like food, clothing, and shelter-are "necessities." It is impossible or, at the very least, very difficult to live without them. Other goods are "luxuries," such as expensive cars, fancy electronics, summer houses, and personalized services. Consumers will start demanding these luxuries and will increase their desired spending on them as they become more affluent. This is part of the reason why 45 percent of all spending in the United States went to agricultural goods in 1830, but today that figure is only about 10 percent. Services such as education and health care are more like luxuries (at least after a certain level of health care), and their share of consumption increases with economic growth. While some manufacturing industries produce luxuries, many manufactured products are more like necessities. Most of the automotive sector does not produce Ferraris or Lamborghinis, and we all need clothes, food products, shelter, and basic appliances. This implies that as the economy grows, there will need to be some reallocation away from manufacturing (and agriculture) toward services-even without Baumol's mechanism based on productivity growth asymmetries. This is what we have seen over the last several decades as well. In the 1960s, 52 percent of consumption expenditure went to manufacturing and 13 percent went to health care, education, entertainment, and financial services. By the late 2010s, both of these numbers stood at approximately 31 percent, indicating a massive shift of consumption away from manufacturing toward services. Consequently, affluence, just like Baumol's cost disease, hasn't been kind to manufacturing. It has amplified the fault lines of the industrial system and the bias of the postindustrial economy toward college-educated workers throughout the industrialized world. While the dynamics unleashed by the cost disease and the broader sectoral reallocation of consumption are largely inevitable, how companies used digital technologies-predominantly for automating lower-education, blue-collar jobs and routine office jobs-was a choice, as I'll explain a little later and then in greater detail in chapter 9. How American and European economies became much more open to imports from lower-wage countries was also largely a matter of choice.

"Chinese Imports Decimating U.S. Industries" Globalization and trade with low-wage countries, especially China, have been other major contributors to the decline of manufacturing in the United States. Goods such as toys, shoes, clothes, and furniture are "tradable"-meaning Walmart and other retailers can source them from outside the United States. Many of the inputs that other manufacturing industries use, including car parts, glass, and metal components, are also tradable, allowing their production to be offshored overseas. In contrast, many services, like health care and education, can't be offshored. So as the United States gradually opened up to international trade, manufacturing was more heavily impacted than services. A turning point for U.S. manufacturing was the surge in imports from China, which was a consequence of rapid productivity growth and the powerful export drive in China. Policymakers also gave Chinese companies privileged access to the U.S. market and then supported the country's accession to the World Trade Organization. This reduced the slew of tariff and nontariff barriers that used to restrict the ability of Chinese companies to sell in the United States and Europe. The total number of U.S. jobs lost due to Chinese competition between 1999 and 2011 is estimated to be between 2 and 2.4 million. This was the background to Treasury Secretary Janet Yellen arguing in Beijing that cheap Chinese imports put "the viability of American ... firms ... into question" and that her government wouldn't "accept [a] reality" in which Chinese imports "flooded the global market and decimated [U.S.] industries." It wasn't just trade in goods that impacted U.S. manufacturing. Offshoring of manufacturing components has had similarly sweeping effects, as numerous jobs in car manufacturing and electronics shifted to lower-wage economies, including China and Mexico.

Automation, offshoring, and import competition from China have also impacted other advanced economies, but in more nuanced ways. Collective bargaining did not decline as much in most of Europe as in the United States. In the Nordic countries, for example, union coverage has remained quite high. This has kept real wages steady there, in contrast to the U.S. In Germany, as we'll see, after new machinery was introduced, companies often kept and retrained blue-collar workers rather than laying them off. These caveats notwithstanding, technology trends have been broadly similar across most industrialized countries. Most tellingly, jobs in blue-collar and clerical occupations have declined in almost all industrialized economies, complicating dreams of shared prosperity. U.S. inequality trends have counterparts in Western Europe as well-even if inequality in those countries did not explode as powerfully as it did in America and never reached U.S. levels. But the bottom line is similar: The industrial compact ended and a form of postindustrial economy and politics emerged throughout Europe, too.

The New Geography of Jobs Deindustrialization driven by automation, sectoral reallocation of consumption away from manufacturing, and globalization were big blows to the industrial compact. A more subtle but equally important fault line was opened by the changing geography of industry and jobs. Industrialization made small towns relevant. Henry Ford chose the city of Detroit as the home for his revolutionary car factories. General Motors followed and located in the same area. But cars need tires, braking systems, specialized fluids, seats, plastic and metal components, electronics, and lots of computer chips, as well as myriad engine parts. Component manufacturing was spread across smaller cities in the Midwest, not too far from the center of gravity of the automobile industry. For instance, Akron, Ohio, grew from a town of just a few thousand in 1910 to about 290,000 people in 1960, driven by the production of tires and rubber for nearby car factories in Detroit. Dayton, Ohio, became the spark plug capital of the United States and also contributed to car assembly, while Flint, Michigan, specialized in assembly, with almost half of its nearly 200,000 inhabitants working in car manufacturing. Nearby smaller cities were increasingly specialized in other car parts: Muncie, Indiana, for batteries and transmission systems; Anderson, Indiana, for electronics; Kenosha, Wisconsin, for engine production; and Janesville, Wisconsin, for seat making. Other industries, ranging from glassmaking to food processing, petrochemicals, textiles, apparel, furniture, and toys, were scattered throughout the country. Firms often avoided the biggest cities, where rents for industrial space were high and workers would require greater compensation to be able to afford housing and other products and services. Small and medium-sized towns made by industrialization were unmade by automation, globalization, and deindustrialization. Automation led to sizable employment declines-not just in the "auto towns" mentioned above but across a number of medium-sized cities specializing in heavy industry, such as Defiance, Ohio, and Beaumont, Texas. Other medium-sized cities, such as Martinsville, Virginia, and High Point and Hickory in North Carolina, that used to specialize in apparel and furniture were decimated by competition from cheap Chinese imports. Even when manufacturing employment survived in some companies in small cities, such as at Anchor Hocking in Lancaster, Ohio, wages were reduced because of automation, pressure from Chinese imports, or cost-cutting efforts imposed by managers and consultants. Manufacturing job losses from trade and technology put downward pressure on worker incomes throughout the local economy and reduced demand for retail, wholesale, housing, and other services, in many cases plunging entire regions into deep, long-lasting recessions. As a result of these changes and the broader deindustrialization process, small and medium-sized towns are now much less central to the national economy. In 1969, 21 percent of U.S. personal income was in small (nonmetropolitan) towns. Today, the same number stands at 10.5 percent, while the share of big cities has increased. Population movements have mirrored this, as people have moved out of small towns and migrated to big cities. The auto towns clustered around Detroit have lost much of their car-manufacturing employment and shrunk considerably in size. Dayton, Ohio, and Flint, Michigan, for instance, now have about half of the population they did in 1960. Population always fluctuates between cities, but this recent transformation has had much more concentrated economic consequences. Large cities are more expensive, especially for housing and labor-intensive services. They offer attractive jobs, but mostly for highly educated workers with specialized expertise. As the geography of jobs has shifted to megacities, opportunities for noncollege workers have declined further. Once upon a time, noncollege workers could also get good wages in cities, because of the need for manual work associated with manufacturing, as well as in transport, construction, and custodial and security work. That, too, has changed during this period, with the disappearance of manufacturing jobs for noncollege workers. Inflation-adjusted wages of noncollege workers in these metropolitan areas have lost more ground than throughout the rest of the country, while the earnings of college graduates and postgraduates in big cities have skyrocketed. For example, between 1979 and 2007, while the wages of postgraduate workers in the largest metropolitan areas increased by about 46 percent, the earnings of workers with high school or less in the same cities remained essentially constant-despite the very significant increase in the costs of living in these areas. This change is, once again, driven by automation (there is less need for manual work in cities), reallocation of industry (fewer industrial jobs in cities), and Baumol's cost disease (more demand for health care, education, and administrative services in cities). The fallout from these regional effects has gone beyond economics and shows us a microcosm of the problems facing U.S. communities more broadly. Local communities that used to be cohesive in smaller cities, such as Lancaster, Ohio, have been under duress as their economies have suffered stagnation and decline. Other examples abound. The meat-processing company Hormel, famous for Spam (the real, not the digital, kind), was a mainstay of the town of Austin, Minnesota. Hormel provided well-paid, stable jobs for workers, and its owners and managers were enmeshed in local civic life, attending the same churches, sporting events, and charities and sending their children to the same schools as their workers. They supported local activities and charities. When the company started automating and offshoring jobs and reducing wages in the 1980s, Austin's community life was also transformed. As jobs were lost and owners and managers moved to other cities or into gated communities, previously vibrant neighborhoods and houses became decrepit. As manufacturing jobs in smaller towns disappeared, social problems multiplied. Marriage rates fell, out-of-wedlock childbirth increased, and mental health problems rose in the worst-affected communities. Job losses and worsening economic opportunities, especially for Americans without a college degree in small and medium-sized towns, also appear to have been a major driver of the rise in what economists Anne Case and Angus Deaton call "deaths of despair"-premature deaths due to drugs, alcohol, and suicide. The health of Americans, which was already worse than that of people in other industrialized countries, has deteriorated further, and mortality rates of American adults have increased, which is unprecedented in advanced economies. While local communities in smaller towns declined, not many new, cohesive communities emerged in megacities. Neighborhoods that used to be home to the working class experienced a rapid rise in house prices as high-earning college graduates and postgraduates moved in and lower-education manual workers moved out. This has set the scene for another change I will emphasize in the next chapter: a huge increase in segregation of communities by education and income. These trends have been defining for the American economy and social life. They also have analogues in the United Kingdom and most of Europe, where, with automation, globalization, and sectoral reallocations, the economic center of gravity has shifted away from smaller towns and toward major cities, with similar social consequences.

Monopoly Most accounts of economic changes of the last five decades ignore monopoly-large firms that dominate their markets. Ford and General Motors were monopolies in the car market, and so were General Electric, U.S. Steel, AT&T, and other behemoths of the twentieth century. Standard Oil and Carnegie Steel, which cornered almost the entire oil and steel markets in the early twentieth century, are synonymous with monopoly. Monopoly is not good for consumers. It leads to higher prices and is sometimes a barrier to further technological change and dynamism. It has a mixed effect on shared prosperity. On the one hand, companies such as Ford and General Motors can afford to split some of their profits with their workers, especially when confronted with a well-organized labor movement. On the other hand, a monopoly might choose to serve only its shareholders and has the power to create countless impediments to higher wages. Most fundamentally, monopoly isn't good for workers, because, as it reduces production to keep prices high, it depresses the demand for labor. As John Sherman, the author of the Sherman Antitrust Act of 1890, put it, "[Monopoly] commands the price of labor without fear of strikes, for in its field it allows no competitors." Monopoly in manufacturing has always been an issue, but a number of factors used to limit the broader impacts of market power. Foreign competition was a looming threat, and large firms also wanted to reach foreign markets, as car companies, General Electric, Boeing, and Nike, among others, have periodically attempted to do. This meant that manufacturing monopolies could not completely turn their backs on innovation or productivity improvements. The shift from manufacturing to services in the postindustrial era has created new kinds of monopoly. On the one hand, we have witnessed the emergence of tech giants, with market capitalization levels unparalleled in history. Before its breakup in one of the most famous cases of antitrust, the market capitalization of Standard Oil was about $36 billion in today's dollars-just a fraction of what Alphabet (Google), Amazon, Microsoft, NVIDIA, and Apple are worth, as of January 2025: $2.4 trillion, $2.4 trillion, $3.2 trillion, $3.4 trillion, and $3.5 trillion, respectively. Other tech giants, such as Meta (Facebook) and Tesla, are close behind. It isn't only in the tech industry that we have seen the rise of dominant firms. The huge U.S. insurance industry is now defined by UnitedHealth, which has a market share of 16.4 percent. Some 68.2 percent of all airline travel in the United States is in the hands of four companies-American Airlines, Delta, Southwest, and United Airlines-which have over time merged with several of their rivals. The situation in banking is similar, with Bank of America, Citibank, JPMorganChase, and Wells Fargo accounting for almost 50 percent of the banking sector after a series of mergers over the last twenty years. Management consulting is dominated by Bain, Boston Consulting Group, and McKinsey. Comcast controls 24 percent of cable television, and mobile telephony is even more concentrated, with AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon making up 96 percent of the sector's sales. These monopolies concentrate their employment in major cities. And they tend to employ highly educated workers, with whom they share part of their profits and comforts. Think of the gobs of facilities and luxuries offered to engineers at Google, while lower-skilled warehouse workers at Amazon don't get on-demand sushi or kombucha. Monopoly has therefore been another force that has skewed the distribution of economic gains over the last several decades. Workers with specialized expertise and postgraduate degrees, living in megacities, have benefited from these monopolies. The rest of Americans have largely lost out because they have borne the brunt of higher prices and lower demand for regular workers brought by rising market power.

Political Economy of Inequality None of this happened in a vacuum. A series of institutional changes in the United States and other industrialized nations facilitated the rise of monopoly, the decline of manufacturing, and the emergence of a low-wage economy, especially for noncollege workers. Institutional changes came about as the children of the Thatcher-Reagan revolution and the drive for deregulation originating on the Right. In a sense, this could be seen as a pushback to several aspects of the left-liberal agenda of the postwar years. In his victory speech, Ronald Reagan mentioned free- market icons Milton Friedman, Friedrich Hayek, and Ludwig von Mises, as well as Barry Goldwater, as his inspirations. Reagan's victory itself was the culmination of a decades-long project-financed by business associations and several rich Americans-of reining in organized labor, rolling back government regulations, and letting markets work more freely. A 1978 manifesto for this movement called it "a massive and unprecedented mobilization of the moral, intellectual and financial resources" aimed at elevating capitalism. Yet in some sense, even this pushback was embedded in the liberal tradition. Neither Thatcher nor Reagan nor any of the other leading conservative figures of the era repudiated liberal democracy. They still stood for liberal values, even if they wanted more markets, less regulation, and a weaker labor movement. There was no questioning of freedom of speech, freedom of religion, or freedom of assembly. General progress was viewed as feasible, almost inevitable. More inequality could be tolerated, and, according to their worldview, economic growth would be the tide that would lift all boats. These politicians did not really dismantle the redistributive state that had formed, either. What's more, some of their policy ideas were soon taken up by the center Left, which also supported deregulation and market-based solutions to a panoply of social problems. Nevertheless, these institutional changes mattered. Deregulation and the shift in the philosophy of antitrust, led by right-leaning scholars and judges such as Robert Bork, helped lay the groundwork for the greater monopolization of the economy. Bork influentially argued that mergers, even those between large firms, could be good for consumers and should be fought only if it could be demonstrated that they increased prices. Without this major shift in the courts' approach to large companies, it would have been difficult for leading companies in the technology, financial, airline, or communication industries to reach the scale they achieved over the last several decades. For example, the technology sector became highly consolidated partly because tech giants were given free rein to acquire smaller competitors and related companies to grow their reach and dominance. If Instagram and WhatsApp had not been acquired by Meta, Meta would not have had the same dominant position in social media in the 2010s. Similarly, if Google had not acquired YouTube and Waze, it would not be a monopoly in video and navigation services in the way that it is now. In the financial sector, Bank of America, JPMorganChase, Citibank, and Wells Fargo wouldn't have been able to increase their market share from 35 percent in 2000 to 50 percent today without acquiring many of their rivals. These were institutional choices that the United States made, and they influenced not just the U.S. market but the European, Canadian, and global ones as well. Equally important was the change in attitudes toward organized labor. Margaret Thatcher and Ronald Reagan were particularly troubled by what they perceived as the growing power of unions, though by the time either of them had been elected, peak unionization was long past. In 1945, 33 percent of the U.S. workforce was unionized; this number was already down to 23 percent in 1980. But Reagan did his best to make sure the union movement was decimated. The Professional Air Traffic Controllers Organization went on strike in 1981, even though industrial action by government employees was illegal. Reagan did not miss the opportunity. Labeling striking workers a "peril to national safety," he fired them, delivering a big blow to organized labor in one fell swoop.
