Private businesses followed Reagan's example and took a tougher stance against strikes in the 1980s. As a result, the number of strikes per year, which was around 250 toward the end of the 1970s, declined to 50 per year by the end of the 1980s. In the late 2010s, there were just a handful of strikes every year. Unionization rates duly declined as well, and today less than 10 percent of the U.S. workforce is unionized. Similar trends ensued in the U.K. during and after Thatcher's government. The weakening of unions meant less collective bargaining for higher wages, contributing to the widening gap between the shares of capital and labor in national income. Perhaps even more importantly, without unions in the picture, there was no counterweight against the automation drive of businesses and no pushback against massive flows of cheap imports and offshoring. Overall, these changes ultimately became incorporated into the policy agenda of the liberal establishment, including the Democratic Party in the United States. This created an environment auspicious for the transformative effects of automation, Baumol's cost disease, and globalization. Although institutional changes didn't make the postindustrial economy, they greatly facilitated its emergence.

Choice and Necessity in Technology The fact that digital technologies have been a key driver of the rise of postindustrial economics and the concurrent collapse of the shared prosperity experienced in previous decades does not suggest any of this was predestined. There is sometimes a tendency in the discussions of experts and laypeople alike to equate technology with inevitable forces. This couldn't be further from the truth. Technology is a choice about how we use our collective knowledge. There are always many directions in which technology can be developed. It is true that digital tools enabled new forms of automation. But they also opened up myriad possibilities that could have bolstered new pathways for shared prosperity. These possibilities included the decentralization of the production process, which many computer pioneers in the 1960s and '70s had anticipated and welcomed. Digital technologies could also have created new tasks for workers of different skills. It was a choice that these paths remained unexplored while industrial and office automation became the priority. The most telling piece of evidence is the fact that inequality trends have differed quite considerably across countries. While there is a common trend toward greater inequality, the extent and details of it vary greatly. By and large, all technologies used by U.S. companies are available to companies in Nordic countries, Germany, or France. But inequality has increased much more in the United States than in any of these other countries. In Sweden, Norway, and France, wage inequality, and especially the gap between high-education and low-education workers, has remained relatively stable. These differences are partly because of different institutional choices-in particular, the role of unions, norms about wage setting, minimum wages, and other regulations in the labor market. They are also about technological choices. German manufacturing firms have introduced industrial robots considerably faster than their American counterparts. The number of robots per industrial worker in Germany is about twice that in the United States. But German manufacturing companies must negotiate with worker representatives and unions when making these choices, and their blue-collar workers are often very well trained, having gone through two or three years of structured apprenticeships. Consequently, while in the 1990s and 2000s American companies deployed digital technologies mostly for automation, displacing and laying off blue-collar workers whenever they could, German manufacturing firms adopted a very different strategy. When introducing new technologies, they retrained their blue-collar workers for technical and supervisory occupations. Via programs such as Industry 4.0 or Digital Factory, German firms also installed computer-assisted design and computer-aided quality-control tools that could be used by trained technical workers without a college degree. These were applications of ideas that several technologists and computer scientists had advocated, such as the inventor Douglas Engelbart, who is responsible for many of the technologies we depend on today, including the computer mouse, graphical user interfaces, and hypertext. Engelbart argued that digital technologies could be "complementary to humans" and used for "boosting mankind's capacity for coping with complex, urgent problems." Engelbart also provided powerful templates for doing so with his inventions. As a result of these types of human-complementary uses of new technology, robot adoption in Germany was not associated with large employment drops-blue-collar work declined, but there were concurrent increases in technical occupations. Signaling the importance of institutional choices, in establishments where labor was better organized, there were less steep employment declines and more pronounced efforts to reallocate blue-collar workers to technical tasks. Comparable adjustments were made in Japan, Finland, the Netherlands, Norway, and Sweden, with similar outcomes-less inequality and less displacement of blue-collar workers. Choices about automation can amplify the disruptive, inequality-boosting effects of transformative technologies, as they did in the U.S. during the 1980s, 1990s, and 2000s. But they can also serve the rebuilding of shared prosperity, as I explain later in the book.

Toward Postindustrial Politics Liberal democracy's success in bolstering shared prosperity during the decades that followed World War II was a product of industrial economics and industrial politics. Liberalism's crisis cannot be understood without recognizing the fundamental change toward postindustrial economics, which impaired the political economy of liberal success. This was partly driven by the inevitable reallocation of consumption away from industry to services. But more importantly, it was a consequence of the way digital technologies were developed and deployed and the type of globalization that was adopted. These changes led to the disappearance of good jobs that used to be the bulwark of shared prosperity, severed the link between productivity growth and wage growth, and weakened community-level and national political participation. But major changes didn't solely originate from economics. The postindustrial economy also altered political and social dynamics and paved the way for a new politics, as we will now see.

Chapter 7

Postindustrial Politics

Progressive focus on identity inequality overlooks massive working-class wage stagnation. Meritocratic narratives undermine solidarity and obscure structural changes benefiting educated elites.

▼

Postindustrial Politics

In the United States at this time liberalism is not only the dominant but even the sole intellectual tradition. For it is the plain fact that nowadays there are no conservative or reactionary ideas in general circulation. -Lionel Trilling

The meritocratic conviction that people deserve whatever riches the market bestows on their talents makes solidarity an almost impossible project. -Michael Sandel

Shortly after pandemic-era travel restrictions eased, I flew to Washington, DC, for a meeting with leaders from left-leaning foundations. The topic was close to my heart: combating inequality and constructing a new narrative about reversing huge and widening income disparities. I expected the focus to be on the jarring increase in earnings inequality over the last four decades, led by the declining wages of workers, and especially men, without college degrees. To my surprise, nobody seemed to comment on the stagnation and decline in the wages of noncollege workers. Nor was there any discussion of how inequality between residents of small cities and large metropolitan areas had surged. Instead, commentator after commentator talked about gender inequality, racial inequality, and inequality affecting sexual minorities. These are all important topics, of course, but if our aim was to understand the surge in inequality at the aggregate level, much of the story was about jobs and opportunities for Americans without college education. As I was traveling back to Boston, another event crystallized my unease about American political trends. Seeing that my flight was delayed by an hour, I decided to get a drink. At the bar by my gate, the only opening was next to a burly gentleman around my age. As I ordered a beer, he asked me whether my flight was also delayed. I nodded ruefully and said it wasn't too bad. He proceeded to talk about football, a topic I know little about. But I welcomed the distraction, and over the next hour, we broached several other topics: the annoyances of air travel, beer, the challenges of the small business he was running, politics, and basketball, about which I knew a little more than football.

It was only later that I realized how rare such an event had become, where I spoke to somebody with a very different education profile, occupation, and interests from mine. This made me consider what would have happened if, on my other side at the bar, there had been somebody with a PhD and an occupation similar to mine (academic, financier, consultant, lawyer, etc.). Whom would I have chosen to initiate a conversation with? Whom would the participants in the workshop I attended have chosen? The answer seemed almost too obvious, and not completely unrelated to why the plight of manual workers was less than top of mind for many college-educated policymakers, activists, and civil servants. I later embellished the choice a little and offered it to several friends and participants at various conferences: If stranded at an airport, with a professional with a PhD from a faraway land (China, India, Brazil, Nigeria) on one side and an American high school graduate on the other, to whom would you look forward to talking for the next hour? Many took the question to be rhetorical, because the answer was, again, too obvious. There is nothing natural about the educational segregation that has emerged in the United States. But it has become a reality, with far- reaching consequences for postindustrial politics. It is emblematic of weakening ties within communities-and within the nation. It may explain how, as they became more politically influential and assertive, the well educated became less understanding of the woes of those with lower levels of education. It is a big part of the story of how left-leaning, well-educated liberal elites started deviating from the premises of liberalism that had generated its past success.

This chapter explains how a liberal establishment in which highly educated experts have become more influential and the college educated command a dominant position has emerged-both in the United States and elsewhere in the industrialized world. The political rise of the college educated is related to the phenomenal growth in the number of people with college degrees. But even more important, it is a consequence of the rising status of the college educated, which is itself rooted in their meteoric economic climb-while automation, offshoring, globalization, and related sectoral trends have reduced the availability of good jobs for noncollege workers. As the college educated have become politically more powerful, they have also diverged further from the less educated in their geographic locations and their social lives, reducing their ability to empathize with the latter's problems and creating a major cultural rift between the two groups. In the process, a coalition between different segments of the college educated and the tech sector has formed, and this coalition has completely turned its back on the economic priorities of the less educated and has come to focus on cultural and global issues. Cultural politics, promoted from both left and right, has further alienated the working class. The Rise of a New Elite Politics in the United States and other industrialized nations was never fully democratic. Nor did politicians and those with influence truly internalize the problems of the rest of the population. But American politics in the twentieth century developed some level of pluralism, with many groups vying for influence on issues that they cared about. The owners and managers of large corporations and the wealthy were, of course, highly powerful, but so were labor unions, local communities, and various interest groups-think of the United Auto Workers, the American Medical Association, and Ralph Nader and his "raiders" taking on automotive and pharmaceutical companies. Highly educated experts always had some degree of power as well. The term "Boston Brahmin"-coined by Oliver Wendell Holmes Sr., who was himself a consummate member of this class as a leading physician, poet, and writer, and the father of future Supreme Court Justice Oliver Wendell Holmes Jr .- has been part of American culture for over a century. The Brahmin families were influential not only because of their wealth and lineage going back to colonial times but also because of their elite education and claim to expertise. At times, for example during the New Deal and World War II, such expertise was highly sought-after and conferred considerable status on its holders. But the power of the educated class was contingent and depended on its coalition with other groups. For instance, in the context of the New Deal and the early postwar decades, segments of the highly educated experts influenced policy in part because they had joined forces with organized labor. In the postindustrial era, the highly educated became an autonomous, more numerous, and more powerful elite. The term "elite" is overused in social sciences and public discussions but still connotes a (somewhat) coherent group with high prestige, social status, and political power. The concept of "power elite" was introduced by the American sociologist C. Wright Mills to designate those who "occupy positions in American society from which they can look down upon, so to speak, and by their decisions mightily affect, the everyday world of ordinary men and women." According to this definition, segments of the educated did become a power elite-or simply elite. Their rise is the defining feature of postindustrial politics. But the story is not simply one in which the more educated become influential relative to the rest of society. Those with higher education have themselves changed fundamentally in the process.

"A Change in the Scope of Public Responsibility" During the nineteenth century, liberal ideas made inroads throughout Europe and the United States. The major expansions of the voting franchise in Europe were not gifts given by landowners and industrialists to the middle class and workers out of the goodness of their hearts. Rather, it was the threat of revolution or, at the very least, the prospect of costly unrest that often triggered concessions. Nevertheless, liberal ideas mattered greatly, too (as I discussed in chapter 4). It became less and less tenable to talk of universal rights while treating the poor as fifth-class citizens. Liberal ideas were vital in providing a language to the disenfranchised, including women, to articulate and push for their demands. They were also important for the nascent notion that state institutions should protect citizens against harm, whether from pollution, disease, or economic hardship. Before the twentieth century, liberalism was never the main, unrivaled governing philosophy in either Europe or the United States. It had to contend with powerful conservative interests. The famous German Chancellor Otto von Bismarck's "state socialism" reforms are particularly telling, since they enshrined some key tenets of liberal ideas, such as health insurance, pensions, and worker protection. Bismarck articulated the case for these policies by stating:

The real grievance of the worker is the insecurity of his existence; he is not sure that he will always have work, he is not sure that he will always be healthy, and he foresees that he will one day be old and unfit to work. If he falls into poverty, even if only through a prolonged illness, he is then completely helpless, left to his own devices, and society does not currently recognize any real obligation toward him beyond the usual help for the poor, even if he has been working all the time ever so faithfully and diligently.

But Bismarck's agenda was strongly paternalist and antiliberal, intent on denying extensive individual freedoms and the possibility of true self-government to the working classes. He simultaneously suppressed the social democrats and waged an anti-Catholic Kulturkampf. His reforms were nonetheless an outgrowth of liberal ideas in the sense that they were a reaction and a partial concession to them-even if with strong conservative overtones. The situation was not much different in Britain, where many reforms were spearheaded by conservatives under the "one nation conservatism" banner, or in the United States, where liberal and conservative ideas, as well as drives for centralization and decentralization, often clashed and fused in the process of building new institutions. The New Deal was a turning point for the emergence of a liberal establishment in the United States. The Progressive Era had brought important regulations, especially in antitrust, and major steps toward a more representative democracy with the direct election of senators. But the emergence of a clear governing philosophy based on liberal ideas did not materialize until Franklin Delano Roosevelt started grappling with the Great Depression-the deepest recession the country had witnessed since its founding-and the specter of fascism and communism. Against this current, the New Deal articulated a new philosophy based on liberalism's main tenets: basic freedoms and a commitment to helping the disadvantaged and the downtrodden. As Lyndon Johnson's vice president, Hubert Humphrey, put it: "The New Deal was a change in the scope of public responsibility"-and so was the governing philosophy of the emerging liberal establishment. Roosevelt, himself a descendant of elite Brahmin families-the Delanos, the Brinleys, and the Lymans-did not become president to upend American institutions. He turned into "a traitor to his class," as some came to describe him, not so much out of choice but out of necessity. Roosevelt responded to the challenging circumstances he inherited by taking steps to build a new institutional structure. Three pillars supported his agenda. The first was the labor movement, which made important advances during Roosevelt's long presidency, especially with the 1935 Wagner Act, which enshrined the right to join unions, to engage in collective negotiations, and to strike. The act also established fair labor practices. The second pillar consisted of Southern Democrats ("Dixiecrats"), who lent support to Roosevelt in return for concessions on issues of race and segregation and influenced the form of his signature legislation, such as the Agricultural Adjustment Act (AAA), adopted to stabilize and raise prices of farm products. The third was Roosevelt's "brain trust," composed of left-leaning, highly educated experts. At first, Southern Democrats and the labor movement were the two strongest pillars. But the experts showed greater staying power. The hold of the Democratic Party on the South and, together with it, the Dixiecrat influence, ultimately waned. Labor's power was partially curbed after World War II, particularly with the Taft-Hartley Act of 1947, which restricted the ability of unions to engage in wildcat and solidarity strikes and secondary boycotts and to impose "closed shop" arrangements in which only union members could be hired. Taft-Hartley also paved the way for "right-to-work" laws around the country, further restricting union power. In contrast, the reach of the experts grew with World War II mobilization, planning, and administration. They became even more sought-after during the Kennedy-Johnson administration, starting with John F. Kennedy's version of the brain trust, made up of young, smart, and ambitious experts, the "best and brightest." The New Deal acknowledged the necessity of regulating the market economy for stability and fairness-exemplified by landmark legislation such as the National Industrial Recovery Act of 1933, which restricted unfettered competition in both the product and the labor markets; the Social Security Act of 1935, which introduced the Social Security program; the AAA; as well as the "alphabet soup of agencies" founded to carry out regulations and administration. The driving force of these regulations was protecting the disempowered-a liberal idea. The engine of recovery and economic growth would be regulated markets, with regulations aiming to ensure that market dynamics did not leave workers and farmers too far behind-yet another liberal idea. The way Roosevelt presented his reforms was as a democratic alternative to fascism and communism-another bedrock liberal idea. The emergent administrative state greatly elevated expertise, too, which is consistent with liberalism's emphasis on progress and science. World War II mobilization, and the critical role of the government in that process, further cemented not just the role of experts but also the policies the New Deal had adopted to combat the Great Depression. These ideas became a core part of the country's governing philosophy. Even Republicans had to abide by their main elements. Dwight Eisenhower defined himself as a "modern Republican" to emphasize that he accepted much of the regulatory approach and objectives of the New Deal. Even Richard Nixon, a consummate provocateur for Democratic lawmakers and activists, followed in Roosevelt's and Eisenhower's footsteps in building and strengthening the administrative state. He relied on the same type of experts as the architects and implementers of crucial regulations. Liberalism was no longer in opposition, denouncing abuses and demanding improvements, as it had been in much of Europe during the nineteenth century. It was no longer just sharing power with conservatives, as it had done in Germany under Bismarck and the United States until the Progressive Era. As the conservative American critic Stanton Evans noted in 1965, "The chief point about the Liberal Establishment is that it is in control." What Evans meant was, of course, left-leaning liberalism, as also described in the famous literary critic Lionel Trilling's quote at the beginning of this chapter. But there were major challenges ahead for liberalism. Standing up for individual rights and progress and criticizing antiliberal policies wouldn't be enough. Liberalism would have to grow up to be in power. Who would dictate its agenda?

Politics Shifts Shape Professional expertise, exercised by highly educated Americans, became central not just because Roosevelt empowered his brain trust or because Kennedy relied on his best and brightest. This change also reflected the evolving role of education in society. In 1949, there were 5.4 million Americans with a four-year college degree. Today, that same number is around 93 million. This numerical increase alone would confer greater power on them. This is part of the story, especially because college education typically imparts common values and interests-respect for knowledge and science, a more cosmopolitan outlook, new connections to an influential social network, and valuable social and technical skills. It is therefore a natural conduit for generating a group with shared ideas and preferences. More important than sheer numbers is the rise in the status of the educated. In much of the West, and especially in the United States, wealth generates social status. No wonder that The Great Gatsby, in which social standing (and tragedy) follows from money, is still viewed as the iconic American novel. The earnings of college-educated workers rose considerably over the last eight decades. In 1939, the average college graduate earned about 66 percent more than the average high school graduate. With the rapid wage growth of manual workers in manufacturing (see chapter 4), economic opportunities for noncollege workers improved and this premium decreased to 47 percent by 1979. Since then, it has surged. Today, college-educated workers earn almost 90 percent more than high school-educated Americans. The college/noncollege distinction may mask the real extent of this divergence. If we instead compare the average earnings of manual workers (computed as the average of blue-collar manufacturing, construction, transport, custodial, and clerical workers, as well as craft workers, such as electricians, carpenters, and plumbers) with those in professional occupations (such as engineering, management, medicine, law, accounting, and economics), the divergence is even more pronounced. The earnings difference between these groups was 78 percent in 1939 and fell to 52 percent in 1979 but has ballooned to 110 percent today. This reflects the myriad economic opportunities created for people with valuable college degrees and analytical training. It is also a result of the declining prospects for manual workers. This wide gulf between workers with and without a college degree, or those in manual and professional occupations, meant not just diverging lifestyles but also a huge shift in social status. This was exacerbated by the meritocratic tilt of American culture, viewing economic success as "just deserts"-a deserved reward for talent. This perspective became even more ingrained as economic fortunes started flowing toward those with technical skills and education, making the meritocratic interpretation of the divergence more palatable. Thus, in the eyes of many Americans (though not always those in communities living through hardship), workers receiving high wages were deserving, and the ones left behind would have to show greater grit and make similar investments to move into highly rewarded occupations themselves. Survey responses show that prestige now lies with well-educated business leaders, computer programmers, management consultants, bankers, surgeons, and lawyers. People in both the U.S. and the U.K. assign lower prestige to occupations such as store clerk, taxi driver, and cook than they did in the late 1940s. The widening gap between blue- and white-collar work is a consequence of the economic changes detailed in chapter 6. Digital technologies boosted the demand for professional work and for college-educated workers. As we have seen, early computers were used mainly by the college educated and enabled them to do things that were not possible before-including more sophisticated inventory control, financial planning, and design and better communication. More important, these trends arose because digital tools were used to automate manual tasks in factories as well as some office tasks typically performed by noncollege workers, such as secretarial, simple accounting, and sales functions. It was this automation drive that more fundamentally damaged the social status of noncollege workers. Americans without a college education not only watched college graduates race ahead of them but also saw their own jobs disappear and their earnings slip. Baumol's cost disease contributed. The economy shifted toward services, such as health care, education, and administration, where the skills of the college educated were in greater demand. This combined with personal computers and automation to reduce the economic and social standing of the less educated, while the prestige and influence of the college educated multiplied. Every categorization is a social construct. We continually categorize one another and interpret our differences through the constructs we have manufactured. In reality, no two college-educated people are alike. Neither are any two workers with a high school degree. Hence, when we talk of Americans with and without a college degree, we are engaging in massive simplification. Nevertheless, to the extent that different groups start acquiring more common characteristics that distinguish them from others, categories take on real meaning. This period witnessed such a social change. In the 1950s, there were many commonalities in the way that the college-educated and noncollege groups viewed the world. Over the next seven decades, cracks emerged between these groups, including on immigration, religious attendance, trust in news media, and the relative importance of global priorities compared to domestic and local ones. The two groups also diverged in terms of their life expectancy, health, marriage and divorce rates, and life satisfaction. Even on topics in which there has been no divergence, such as homosexuality, new divisions emerged, for example, in terms of views on transgender rights. Cultural attitudes became more important as the three crucial pillars of the New Deal-workers, Southern Democrats, and left-leaning college-educated professionals and intellectuals-began to diverge in their political influence as well as in their views and values.

The Working Class, Abandoned Alongside their beliefs, the experiences of the college- and non-college-educated grew apart starting in the 1960s. People of different education levels fought together in World War II, with almost 80 percent of American men of draft age going to war. The college-educated were actually more likely to enlist than those without college education. By the time of the Korean War in the early 1950s, the situation was very different. New policies meant that the majority of college students could defer military service. But an even bigger divergence was to come with the Vietnam War. Many of the leaders of the labor movement were not hostile to the war, in part because it was spearheaded by President Lyndon Johnson, who was viewed as friendly to labor, and also because the U.S. labor movement leadership was generally anticommunist and patriotic. College students had a very different stance. Campus protests against the war started in 1964 and became a constant across several universities in the late 1960s and early '70s. Many students saw the protests as a principled stand against an unnecessary, imperialist war. Many workers saw it as students wanting to get out of serving in Vietnam. Reality was somewhere in between. Students hated the idea of fighting in Indochina's jungles, and college enrollment rates skyrocketed as young people sought to get deferments. By the end of the 1970s, men with a college degree were only one-third as likely to serve as those without a degree. By this point, "new left ideas"-which prioritized issues of identity and self-expression rather than more traditional leftist topics such as labor concerns, inequality, and jobs-had become popular on campuses, driving a further wedge between students and workers. The simmering tensions came to a head in the Hard Hat Riot in New York in 1970, where construction workers attacked antiwar protesters. These events were a prelude to a much bigger change: the transformation of the bastion of left-leaning liberalism in the United States, the Democratic Party-as well as similar left-of-center parties in other industrialized nations. From the New Deal up until at least the 1970s, the Democratic Party was the home of manual, unionized workers. Strengthening unions was one of Roosevelt's key policies, and Democrats continued these efforts in the 1950s and '60s. The Democratic Wagner Act expanded union power, and the Taft-Hartley Act, drafted by a Republican senator and a Republican congressman, curbed it. The law was vetoed by the Democratic President Harry Truman, though his veto was overridden with support from some Democratic lawmakers. Given this background, Americans without a college degree were significantly more likely to vote for the Democratic Party. In the 1970s, around 60 percent of them did so. The same number was just 44 percent for the college educated. Today, the picture is reversed. Only 48 percent of Americans without a college degree now vote Democrat, and Democrats have a solid lead among the college educated, receiving 60 percent of their vote. Similar trends are visible across the industrialized world. In the U.K., around 23 percent of those with a college degree voted for the center- left Labour Party in the 1970s. That number now stands at 43 percent. In France, the trend is similar, with the percentage of those with a college degree who support the socialists and other left-wing parties rising from 48 percent to 62 percent today. In Germany, the turnaround is even starker: In the 1970s, only 29 percent of the college educated voted for the Left. Now it is 52 percent. This global trend follows from the eclipse of industrial politics and the rise of postindustrial cleavages. But left-leaning parties have also changed. A recent study finds a notable divergence between the economic policy preferences of less-educated Americans and the Democratic Party and a simultaneous convergence between the party's (and its lawmakers') positions and what the college educated support. The less educated consistently ask for a higher minimum wage, greater union power, protectionism against unrestricted flows of cheap foreign imports, and programs that provide jobs. College-educated Americans are not opposed to helping the disadvantaged. But they favor using taxes and spending to achieve this objective. The former policies were supported by Democratic lawmakers in the 1950s, '60s, and '70s. But since the late 1970s, the Democratic Party's support has shifted away from such policies toward taxes and spending. A clear wedge is visible between what less- educated, working-class Americans want and what the Democratic Party offers. Perhaps predictably, this shift has coincided with other major changes in the party's structure and sources of support. The party now receives significantly more donations from geographic areas with more college graduates, signaling that it has indeed become the party of college-educated and postgraduate Americans. The background of its leadership has also transmuted. Left-leaning experts were a constant fixture of the New Deal coalition and the Kennedy-Johnson administration, as we have seen. But Ivy League graduates were still more likely to be seen among Republican lawmakers. From the 1940s to the mid-1970s, about 30 percent of Republican congressmembers were products of the Ivy League. These numbers were less than 20 percent for Democrats. This picture, too, has reversed. Today, the Democrats are the party of the Ivy League-educated experts, with more than 30 percent of their lawmakers coming from Ivy League universities, while for Republicans these numbers have fallen to single digits. Similar trends hold for senators. The change started with the "Watergate babies"-the class of new, young, and highly educated Democrats elected in the aftermath of the Watergate scandal. It accelerated with the founding of the Democratic Leadership Council (DLC), which aimed to infuse "modern thinking" into Democratic policies and became particularly influential during Bill Clinton's presidency. The DLC was hostile to minimum wages, trade protectionism, and public works programs. For example, in the words of one of its founding members, "The bottom line was that the country and economy had changed, and the minimum wage, so important in the New Deal, ceased to be an effective way to help the working poor." The DLC's views on protectionism and public works programs were similar. In the meantime, the preferences of the less educated have remained remarkably consistent. The growing wedge between the Democratic Party and their preferences has not escaped the attention of working-class Americans, who used to report that the Democratic Party was better for keeping the country prosperous. They no longer do. This is what Senator Bernie Sanders, Democratic presidential candidate in 2016 and 2020, captured when he posted on Twitter after the 2024 general election: "It should come as no great surprise that a Democratic Party which has abandoned working class people would find that the working class has abandoned them." Except that the same is true for most left parties, not just the Democrats in the U.S. The divergence between Democrats and the working class has been even more jarring on cultural matters. Recent research documents that Democrats have also been losing votes from lower-education Americans because of their stances on cultural issues, which are growing further apart from these voters' preferences. The cultural and economic separation between the Democratic Party and the working class is sometimes blamed on the rise of "identity politics"-meaning politics based on ethnic, religious, sexual, or other identities that are often viewed as separate and opposed, for example between oppressed and oppressing identities. While there is much truth in this diagnosis, it is also possible to view the rise of identity politics as an outcome of postindustrial social dynamics. The emergence of the college educated as a major force within the Left, but separated from working classes and community, brought about the end of policy platforms that appealed to broad segments of the population, such as the New Deal coalition. In their place, it elevated issues that appealed to powerful subsets of college-graduate activists who were economically well-off and more concerned with specific cultural issues. Identity politics may thus be a consequence of broader economic and political forces that shaped the postindustrial age. Simultaneously, the Right in most industrialized countries has shifted its language and policies to be more aligned with those of workers without college education. The first prominent American figure to articulate this shift may have been Patrick Buchanan, a onetime staffer for President Richard Nixon. In the second half of the 1970s, Buchanan argued that Republicans had to become "the party of the working class, not the party of the welfare class," and the way to achieve this was through "an agonizing reappraisal of our heretofore almost uncritical support for American business." These ideas became even more powerfully articulated by a thinker who anticipated many of the popular hard-right preoccupations of the 2010s, Samuel Francis. Francis, an adviser to Buchanan, pushed beyond Buchanan's ideas, arguing for a "Middle American Revolution" powered by Americans whose preferences and culture were ignored by the liberal establishment. Immigration was a particular focus for Buchanan and Francis. Throughout the 1990s and 2000s, views on immigration kept diverging between college-educated and noncollege Americans. In the 1980s, about 70 percent of white Americans, both with and without a college degree, had some concerns about aspects of overall immigration into the country. Since then, these attitudes have grown apart. In the late 2000s, noncollege Americans still had essentially the same view (63 percent), but only about 45 percent of college graduates agreed. As Republicans staked out anti-immigration and pro-religion policy positions, they became increasingly aligned with an important set of issues occupying the minds of working-class Americans. Another Republican strategy amplified this trend. Many left-leaning populist ideas, often in line with the sensibilities of working-class constituencies and small businesses, used to come from Southern Democrats. The Democratic Party's championing of civil rights and Richard Nixon's "Southern strategy," aimed at capitalizing on the discontent of the white Democratic base in the South by exploiting racial prejudice, altered that. Democratic support in the South waned, and the shift of lower-education voters to the Republican Party accelerated. Simultaneously, polarization and partisan wrangling between the two parties intensified, arguably starting during Newt Gingrich's speakership of the House of Representatives between 1995 and 1999. Gingrich, aided and abetted by right-wing talk show and TV personalities, spearheaded a strategy that shunned bipartisanship and fueled animosity between the two parties. As part of this strategy, the Right also began amplifying working-class voters' discontent with the Democrats. In sum, by the 1990s, Democratic lawmakers were mostly college graduates from prestigious universities, receiving support from like- educated and like-minded constituents. Their sensibilities, strongly shared by the majority of Ivy League graduates and postgraduates throughout the country, were increasingly at odds with the policies and cultural stances favored by the less-educated working class. The Democratic Party was still committed to improving the lot of poor Americans, but the way they went about this, the policies they offered, and the language they used no longer resonated with working-class communities. These differences were exaggerated and exploited by Republican influencers and leaders. The core constituency of the Left thus became the highly educated, whom many working-class Americans started viewing as out of touch, culturally distant, and shedding only crocodile tears for their plight. Although the details varied from country to country, what happened in the United States also transpired in other industrialized nations, where the center-Left parties started losing the working class, both because they were prioritizing cultural issues and because they were no longer seen as advocating economic policies that these voters desired. By the mid-2010s, noncollege voters had already become significantly more likely to support center-right and sometimes populist-right parties in much of Europe as well.

"How Can the Polls Be Neck and Neck?" Why did Democrats-and the Left in other industrialized nations-let the working classes abandon their party? Several factors appear to have contributed. The first is the changing values of Democratic leaders and activists, which we have already seen. The second is the role of the tech sector, which I will discuss further below. The third is related to the fact that the college-educated segments of the Democratic coalition came to interact less and less with the working class. Americans of all education levels used to live in similar neighborhoods. Approximating U.S. neighborhoods by census tracts containing about four thousand people, in the 1960s, about 22 percent of a typical college graduate's neighbors had a college degree. Today, that number stands at 46 percent. This is a result of the movement of college graduates to more expensive suburbs or into gated communities, leading to much less mixing by education or income. This segregation is equally sharp when we look at those with postgraduate degrees. In 1970, about 11 percent of a typical postgraduate's neighbors also had a postgraduate degree. Today, this number has increased to more than 22 percent. Those with and without a college degree also work in different places. The classic manufacturing firm was a melting pot, containing a large number of manual workers but also technicians, engineers, accountants, and managers with college education. Surviving manufacturing firms are much more education heavy, and most health care and education organizations are even more so. Sectors such as retail and fast food, on the other hand, are mostly composed of lower-education workers, with few employees with postgraduate or even college degrees. Our social lives have also diverged. In the U.S., marriage is particularly segregated by education. The likelihood that a college graduate married another college graduate was 27 percent in 1970 and has risen to 42 percent today. The likelihood that a postgraduate marries another postgraduate has increased from 26 percent to 44 percent during the same period. These changes mean that many people with and without a college degree are no longer part of the same community and share less and less of their social network. They live apart, in separate worlds. Since community is the conduit of information, trust, and mutual understanding, this separation by education (and income) has damaged trust and severed important links for creating potential solutions to our social problems. It has weakened bonds that tie different communities and people to a higher-level (e.g., national) shared understanding that is essential for compromises to be accepted and democracy to function. It has consequently made it much more likely that the college educated remain oblivious to the concerns and experiences of the less educated. It isn't only separation and divergent cultural values between the college educated and the rest. Across a number of industrialized countries, including the United States and United Kingdom, surveys show that the college educated view the less educated more negatively than other disadvantaged groups, such as the poor, and consider them to be responsible for their hardship. These tendencies are even more pronounced among college graduates with stronger beliefs in meritocracy. It is a consequence of these trends that in many universities most academics did not know anybody who voted for Trump in 2016, and wondered how half of the country did so. The playwright Arthur Miller captured the same sentiment when he stated in 2004: "How can the polls be neck and neck when I don't know one Bush supporter?" On the flip side, many low-income, low-education communities do not understand the priorities of their more affluent counterparts. They also increasingly think that policymakers and civil servants with fancy diplomas do not speak for them.

A common view among the left-leaning college-educated segments of the U.S. population is that the working class is fooled by media and rhetoric to lend its support to Republicans. According to this view, the Democrats are still the party of the working class and the poor, with an unwavering commitment to their betterment, and Republicans still prioritize millionaires, billionaires, and tax cuts instead of the interests of poor Americans. If poor people think that Democrats don't stand for them, this is a manufactured opinion, brought about by talk show hosts, misinformation on Fox News, and social media manipulation, financed by Republican billionaires. There is some truth to this claim, since the flames of cultural politics have more often been fanned by the Right than by the Left, and Republicans have simultaneously supported policies that are harmful to the working class (such as reducing taxes on the rich and opposing health care coverage for poor Americans). Nevertheless, the divergence between Democrats and working-class communities is also a reality. As I argued in chapters 2 and 3, liberalism must stand for community and self-government. In particular, it must support bottom-up political participation, the ability of communities to have a say in their organization and in policies that affect their lives, and the construction of higher-level shared identities that can bring different groups together. The Democratic Party, led by the college educated, abandoned these principles, replacing them with a top-down technocracy-experts know best and don't need to heed the economic or cultural preferences of the working class. Balancing expertise and self-government is difficult in the best of times. Of course, there are things that experts know and ordinary people may not-such as how to conduct monetary policy, how to deal with pandemics, which drugs work and which don't, and so on. But if, when technocrats' priorities and people's preferences clash, only the views of the former matter, grievances will set in. Americans today are much less likely to say that most people can be trusted than they were in the 1970s. They also have much less confidence in political institutions, the media, and experts. Many report that they have little control over government policies or their own lives and volunteer the opinion that "lawmakers will not speak for us or people like us." The general perception is that the basic pillars of self-government have been sacrificed and a nonresponsive technocracy has taken its place.

Waning Solidarity Postindustrial economics and politics have reduced the capacity for self-government among working-class Americans and Europeans, because they eroded community and solidarity. Solidarity-mutual respect, regard, and caring-is vital for the success of any social group. A community works better when its members understand and care for one another. Solidarity itself comes from shared community activities. People used to build solidarity as they played together, socialized together in churches, and contributed to their local schools and Little Leagues. Such solidarity was important for the cohesion of the community and bolstered better social outcomes for its members. Solidarity often starts at the local-community level, but when there are shared common values, moral principles, and communication between communities, such as those cultivated by patriotism or notions of citizenship of a republic, it can also exist at the national level. As I emphasized in chapter 3, such shared higher-level identities are important for liberal democracy to function and for liberalism's agenda to receive broad buy-in and support. That no longer seems to be possible. Community and solidarity are in decline throughout the United States and the industrialized world, and so is the prospect of a shared higher-level identity that can bring communities together. People have fewer friends and spend less time interacting with others in their communities, and there are fewer local associations shaping people's lives. People in the industrialized world also appear to care less about their fellow citizens. For example, in the United States, blood donations have declined to about one-third of their level in the early 1990s, indicating much less willingness to make even small sacrifices for others. The political scientist Robert Putnam has done more than anyone else to highlight the undoing of community. Recently, he has documented the decline of communal and cooperative language since around the 1980s. The usage in English-language books of words typically associated with community and associational life, such as "community,""association," "agreement," "compromise," and "unity," was on the rise from 1880 to the 1960s or the 1970s. Starting around 1980, it showed a sharp decline. The relative usage of "responsibility" compared to "rights" depicts the same pattern. Even more striking is the relative usage of "we" compared to "I," which increases steadily until around the 1970s and then decreases precipitously. It appears that people are much more concerned with themselves and sometimes with their families, rather than with their local community and certainly with their fellow citizens. This retreat of community has made educated Americans-including the left-leaning college graduates who are influential in the Democratic Party-not just isolated from but also much less capable of comprehending and empathizing with the plight of the less educated. This worry has been exacerbated by the meritocratic interpretation of economic success that the political philosopher Michael Sandel's quote at the beginning of this chapter highlights. Lack of solidarity has also worsened various social outcomes throughout low-income and low-education communities. For example, in neighborhoods that occupy the bottom quartile in terms of fraction of college graduates, there has been a striking deterioration in social outcomes. In 1970, these neighborhoods had teen pregnancy rates that were about 50 percent higher than the national average. Today, they are double the national average. In the same low-education areas, marriage rates have plummeted and out-of-wedlock births have increased rapidly. In 2016, half of births to mothers without a college degree were out of wedlock, which is a striking increase from 19 percent in 1978. There is no similar increase for out-of-wedlock births to college-graduate mothers. The share of deaths from opioid or other drug overdoses used to be around 0.1 percent for both college-educated and noncollege groups. By 2023, it had increased to over 3 percent for individuals without a college degree, compared to 0.8 percent for the college educated. A high-solidarity community has better ways of dealing with its social problems, for example by leveraging the support and intervention of cohesive social networks. Some of this may take the form of the cage of norms, as I discussed in chapter 4. But it can also be an effective tool to cope with periods of turbulence and disruption. The deteriorating social outcomes of many working-class communities are a symptom of what has gone wrong with the American economy. But they are a cause of further societal problems as well. As community and solidarity have declined, the working class has been less able to be its own advocate. The tip of the iceberg is electoral participation. In working-class neighborhoods, about 65 percent of the population voted in presidential elections in the 1970s. By the 2010s, this number had fallen to 47 percent. Lower political participation-coupled with many college-educated elites empathizing less with their problems, local newspapers being in decline, and leading national media outlets becoming less reflective of their concerns-has made it more difficult for the economic and cultural concerns of working-class Americans to be aired prominently. The erosion of solidarity has further impaired these communities' capability to engage in self-government. They are less organized and less likely to have their voices heard in local and national politics. Lack of solidarity has also contributed to the decline of the labor movement since the 1970s. The divide between college and noncollege groups has deepened in other ways as well. All too often, the explicit or implicit message from the media and experts has been one of self-decline: If communities that used to specialize in manual work and their members do not want to fall behind, they have to invest in their own education and skills. It then becomes almost a moral failing not to have done so. This has further reduced the empathy of the college educated for these communities and has piled up the pressure on them. It isn't just that their bread and butter has become less plentiful. It is also that that very bread and butter came to be viewed as unattractive and uninspiring. Sticking with it has become a fault. No wonder that self-government at the community level has been in crisis as well.

A Cultural Denominator Though the college/noncollege distinction is significant, college graduates are not themselves a monolithic entity. The differences within the college educated are pronounced and have shaped major aspects of postindustrial politics. Many divisions exist among the college educated, but probably none is more important than that between the "elite graduates" and the "mass college educated." Elite graduates consist of individuals with valuable skills in specialized areas, such as computer programming, engineering, law, medicine, and management, often with postgraduate degrees and training in selective universities (in the U.S., this refers to the top fifty or so). Their elite status is rooted in the fact that their skills are highly coveted. I'm using the term "mass college educated" for the rest to emphasize that this group is much larger than the elite graduates (making up more than 90 percent overall college graduates). The mass college educated do not quite have the same specialized skills that the market values so highly. It is the elite graduates who largely drive the higher average income of the college educated. These higher earnings, especially if one reaches the remarkable riches and high status of Silicon Valley cadres, are one motivator for youngsters to pursue university education. But most of those who attend less selective schools and study less quantitative disciplines will not sniff such earnings and prestige. A few comparisons illustrate this division. Postgraduates are not all elite graduates, but many elite graduates have more education than a bachelor's degree. Comparing their performance to those without postcollege degrees therefore gives a glimpse of the trends. American postgraduates now earn 179 percent more than workers with just a high school degree, while those with a college degree but without postgraduate education earn only 99 percent more than high school graduates. While postgraduates' earnings have been rising since the 1990s, we don't see a similarly robust increase for college graduates. For example, the inflation-adjusted earnings of male college graduates without a postgraduate degree increased only by 15 percent between 1979 and 2015. The occupational picture is similar. Occupations employing most elite graduates-engineering, computer science and related occupations, law, medicine, and management-earn roughly 130 percent more than blue-collar workers. Those in non-university teaching and white-collar office jobs earn only about 40 percent more than blue-collar workers. These divergent experiences of elite graduates and the mass college educated set the scene for major grievances-and also boosted demand for social engineering, as we'll see in chapter 8. The mass college educated had similar aspirations to elite graduates and were often motivated by the sparkling success of that elite. Yet they couldn't approach those heights. Many jobs filled by the mass college educated paid hardly more than what electricians or plumbers could earn. Some college graduates even struggled to find such jobs and were pushed into marginal employment opportunities in coffee shops or ride-sharing. In a country where social status follows earnings, they also felt increasingly powerless. In sum, the two core groups within the increasingly influential college-educated segment had divergent economic trajectories. They also held different opinions on many social issues. In surveys, Americans in elite-graduate occupations are more likely to report that merit is key for success and that markets work well, by and large. The mass college educated are more negative toward markets. One episode that reveals the divide between elite graduates and the mass college educated took place during the financial crisis. The indignation that many in the nation felt about Wall Street bankers, who caused the financial meltdown, being bailed out and receiving their bonuses was one of the drivers of the Occupy Wall Street movement. Occupiers became a symbol of protest against the jarring inequities in early twenty-first-century America. The Occupy movement was not homogeneous, but the typical participant was young and very likely to have some college education (92 percent had graduated from or attended college). But the majority were not from elite schools, and almost half of participants had a yearly income less than $25,000. The reaction from some elite graduates, for example those working in well-paying finance jobs on Wall Street or in management consulting, was to sympathize with the small group of provocateur counterprotesters calling themselves the "Occupy a desk" movement, which argued that Occupiers should just get a job. We can see similar divides in U.S. academia, especially between the humanities and engineering fields. Surveys find that, among those with a political affiliation, only about 3 percent of humanities faculty and 8 percent of social science faculty identify as Republican. On the other hand, among engineering faculty, which trains some of the elite graduates, 38 percent report being conservative. With such economic and ideological differences, there would appear to be little in common between elite graduates and the mass college educated. A political coalition between these two groups might seem to be a long shot. But a powerful common denominator soon emerged. Both groups agreed on certain cultural issues and had a strong stance on the quintessential dilemmas of liberalism (see chapter 5). Intolerance and ignorance were the scourges of our time, according to most college- educated people. While the two groups disagreed on the importance of merit and the virtues of markets, they had much more congruent views on immigration, gay marriage, abortion, the value of education, and globalization (including openness to international influences, cosmopolitan values, and global concerns). Both groups were also less concerned with community. Thus, the two wings of the college educated segment united on cultural issues, especially those that challenged and attempted to alter ignorance and intolerance of individuals and communities. The emphasis on cultural issues was rooted in these groups' belief that cultural change would enable a better, fairer, and more tolerant society to be built. Yet there was more to this postindustrial agreement than might first meet the eye. An even more powerful constituency would sign on and shape the agenda of postindustrial liberalism: the tech sector. The tech sector was influential in both Democratic and Republican politics from the get-go. The Watergate babies-the young Democrats aspiring to transform their party in a more market-friendly direction, discussed previously-soon came to be referred to as "Atari Democrats" because of their emphasis on high tech as the engine of the economy (and their deprioritization of working-class concerns). The group included several rising stars, like Gary Hart and Al Gore. Simultaneously, right-leaning technology enthusiasts, such as George Gilder, and tech-infused libertarian ideas became popular in Republican politics.

Tech-Sector Values The tech sector holds a unique place in the postindustrial era. It is the designer and producer of the technologies that have created the postindustrial economy, and it has itself been the main beneficiary of the new economics that these technologies created. For instance, the share of the computer and information technology industry (excluding telecoms) in national income doubled from about 3.2 percent in the early 1970s to 6.4 percent in 2023. The tech industry is also the employer of a significant fraction of elite graduates. Today, almost 30 percent of innovators (defined as those receiving or contributing to an innovation that is granted a patent) are in the information and communication technology sector, and another 23 percent are employed by its sister industry, electronics and electrical equipment. Information and communication technology also employs 33 percent of all engineers in the United States, while electronics and electrical equipment employs another 8 percent. The tech sector had distinct values from the beginning. Libertarian ideas, which mix liberal notions with a strong free-market ideology, were always popular among tech enthusiasts and innovators, even if some tech leaders leaned center Left on issues such as redistribution and education. Culturally, there was much more in common between right- and left-leaning technologists. The elite graduates of the tech sector were quite self-aware and proud that their preoccupations and expertise were not shared with the rest of society. By its nature, the sector also strongly valued expertise and technocracy. After all, you were either a good engineer or you were not. Either you could program or design new products well, or you were of no use to the start-ups and the tech behemoths. It wasn't just the important role of technical knowledge that drove attitudes in the tech sector, however. The tech field was convinced of its own significance and also became enthralled by an extreme version of meritocracy-even as many elite graduates in the sector held some liberal views and supported the Democratic Party. Almost 80 percent of Silicon Valley workers with a college degree agreed with the statement that "the success of Silicon Valley's tech companies benefits everyone in the region," compared to less than 36 percent of nontech workers. They also came to believe that those who were talented deserved the just deserts they would often get because of their innovativeness. Statements such as "Very few are contributing enormous amounts to the greater good, be it by starting important companies or leading important causes" (as uttered by a start-up founder) capture what many in the industry believe. It would be unfair and against the ethos of capitalism, many elite graduates surmised, if the very few making such enormous contributions-themselves-weren't handsomely compensated. It thus came to be viewed as both ethical and good economics that there should emerge a new hierarchy based on meritocracy, or their version of meritocracy. It was only a small step from these ideas to the next set: The opinions and expertise of elite graduates (or the elite of the elite graduates) mattered a lot, and the rest shouldn't interfere. If there was a conflict between these ideas and the views of the less educated, it was only just and fair that the former set of ideas should prevail. A popular line of reasoning drew parallels between companies and countries. According to this perspective, companies flourished under powerful, visionary founders, with Apple under Steve Jobs often given as an example. Countries should move toward the "founder mode" as well, the argument proceeded, which meant a powerful, visionary leader or a cadre of talented experts should have more say in politics. Ideally, they should not be swayed by too many voices or too many cooks spoiling the broth. Either democracy had nothing to do with it-after all, the United States was a republic, and elite graduates becoming more influential within the republic was just natural-or if democracy got in the way, it was fine to sacrifice a little popular voice at the altar of better, more expert decisions. This hierarchical mindset also made the tech sector dismiss community and community-based ways of creating solidarity. Self-government certainly did not come up as relevant in most discussions in Silicon Valley. The tech sector had strong incentives for favoring certain economic and cultural policies. Anything that got in the way of tech innovators and entrepreneurs reaping the rewards of their success was a no-no. But it also had a very strong global outlook. Their market was often not the United States but the entire world economy, and the companies had an insatiable appetite for more engineers, who could only come from abroad, unless the United States were to completely revamp its education system. While this demand for necessary skills made the tech companies favor free immigration of skilled workers, for instance via the H-1B visa program, many in the tech sector also relied on and supported lower-skilled immigrants for personal services-as nannies, house help, gardeners, and service workers. Hence, the economic interests and overall values of the tech sector shared the common cultural denominator between elite graduates and the mass college educated. Even though there were major differences on other issues between these groups, cultural priorities were a powerful unifying factor. The tech sector's prestige and social power also mattered. The sector added a strongly technocratic stance to the coalition, making it more likely and more acceptable that any opposition to this agenda would be labeled intolerant and ignorant. The college educated had already diverged from the less educated in terms of where they lived, how they organized their lives, their social networks, and their cultural values, making it less likely that they would notice or care how this coalition left noncollege, working-class Americans behind. It soon started focusing more powerfully on cultural issues and took fateful steps toward social engineering. This meant abandoning key precepts of the liberalism that worked (chapter 4): Shared prosperity was forgotten, and variation in values and attitudes that undergirded experimentation and local governance started being viewed as a problem to be tackled.

Chapter 8

Social Engineering

Mao's Cultural Revolution shows how ideology imposed from above unleashes violence. Top-down schemes fail without community deliberation, buy-in, and democratic accountability.

▼

Social Engineering

It is harder to grasp why so many well-intended schemes to improve the human condition have gone so tragically awry. -James C. Scott

If liberty of speech is to be untrammeled from the grosser forms of constraint, then uniformity of opinion will be secured by a moral terrorism to which the respectability of society will give its thorough approval. -Charles Sanders Peirce

Human history is laced with repression, fear, and violence-dark currents that erupt with grim predictability. But even in the pantheon of brutal experiences spanning millennia, Mao Tse-tung's Cultural Revolution, launched in 1966, occupies a singular place. Its savagery was largely self-inflicted. More than two million Chinese were beaten and tortured to death, not by soldiers and police but by Red Guards drawn from their friends, neighbors, and fellow classmates. One survivor captures this era's lasting scar by saying, "Our society is ethically hollow .... If we trace these problems to their roots, we are likely to find them in the Cultural Revolution." This mind-boggling violence wasn't initiated to quell a rebellion or unrest. It was a campaign to control how people thought. Mao was the architect, encouraging ordinary people to police the thoughts and behavior of their friends and acquaintances. If anyone was observed to harbor counterrevolutionary tendencies, deviate from the strictest version of Mao's communist thought, or attempt to express their opinions, their community was free to exact uninhibited violence as punishment. Mao encouraged the first group of Red Guards to "be brutal" and "trample" his enemies. After witnessing one atrocity, he enthusiastically told one of the Red Guards responsible for killing her school headmistress: "Be violent!" For Mao, people were blank slates, and with the right amount of brutal control, they could be shaped to do anything. A former Red Guard remembered, "The control was as strict as if people were living inside an iron bucket," and offered some justification for her reluctance to speak out even after she realized the extreme violence against body and soul: "If you dared, you would be labelled as a counter- revolutionary and locked up or sent away to labour camps. Nobody dared to think, and nobody dared to speak." This was the intention of it all. It was the reason why Red Guards targeted symbols and mere thoughts: "Wherever we saw anything-like Buddhas, or good-luck couplets at the doors-we ordered store owners to take them down." The Cultural Revolution went after education in order to shape minds and habits. The focus was not new: Its forerunner, the Socialist Education Movement, had likewise put classrooms and intellectuals in the crosshairs. After 1949, communist policies greatly expanded formal schooling, and education naturally encouraged more independent thought. Better to control that, concluded Mao. As one of his followers put it, people should "read Chairman Mao's books, listen to Chairman Mao's words and follow Chairman Mao's instructions!" This was about controlling people's thoughts and behaviors. It started in schools, but it continued throughout one's life and via reeducation in labor camps if necessary. Mao was also very clear that art and culture had to be tools for thought policing and imposing social order, claiming: "There is in fact no such thing as art for art's sake, art that stands above classes, art that is detached from or independent of politics." One of the Cultural Revolution's sinister successes was persuading children to denounce their own parents as counterrevolutionaries. A boy who did so later explained, bluntly, that relentless propaganda in school had propelled him into this act: "Why I could send my mother to be shot-why I did such things-was because of the education I got." It is tempting to assume such indoctrination would fail-yet, for a while, it spread with frightening ease. The boy who condemned his parents was no anomaly. As another participant later confessed: "It turned people into beings without thoughts or feelings. You didn't think anything. You did what you were told." Despite its extreme, absurd violence, the Cultural Revolution is an example of a much more common phenomenon: social engineering, or efforts to impose uniform values and practices on people. A more benign but also backlash-prone type of social engineering emerged in the context of postindustrial politics, and I draw from other historical examples to distill the dynamics of this social engineering and the reactions to it. It is important to emphasize that the type of social engineering that took root among postindustrial liberals was an effort to grapple with real problems related to ignorance, intolerance, and the protection of disempowered groups (see chapter 5). It was neither violent nor totalitarian and left room for contrary views-and thus backlash. But it exhibited commonalities in its intentions and origins with less benign forms of social engineering. By tarnishing the commitment of liberalism to freedom of expression, it also opened the way to more baleful restrictions on freedoms from antiliberal circles.

A Softer Straitjacket By the twenty-first century, many left-leaning liberals had intensified their focus on cultural politics and become convinced that protecting minorities and vulnerable groups and building a fairer economy required silencing those with opposite viewpoints. Social transformation, they believed, could be brought about by imposing a set of common values on people. Achieving greater freedoms for everybody required changing hearts and minds through education, social sanctions, and institutionalization of rules of speech and conduct in education and in corporations. None of this was violent, and some of it was in reaction to provocation by right-wing politicians and media personalities. Nevertheless, with the most egregious cases being aggressively advertised by right-wing media, many Americans perceived it as the forceful imposition of values by liberal elites on them and their children. Telling examples come from universities. Before 2012, freedom of expression was under threat from antiliberal and some conservative groups on U.S. campuses, especially in religious universities, attempting to silence and deplatform leftist speech. However, from 2012 on, a left-progressive campaign to deplatform conservatives gained steam. From 2016 to 2023, progressive deplatforming efforts far exceeded similar attempts from the Right. In a 2025 survey, one in three university students reported that resorting to violence could be acceptable to stop undesirable speech on campus. The progressive argument against the primacy of freedom of expression is centered around how certain forms of speech can constitute microaggressions and cause harm to various groups. Even though this movement sharply deviated from liberalism's core precepts, self-described left liberals were often at its forefront and it came to be identified with liberalism. At my own university, MIT, freedom of expression has never been as curtailed as on many other American campuses. Nonetheless, MIT publicly revoked an invitation to Dorian Abbot, professor of geophysics at the University of Chicago, to give the John Carlson Lecture, a prestigious talk aimed at public science outreach. The reason: Abbot's views on diversity efforts on college campuses. In a Newsweek magazine editorial, written with a Stanford colleague, Abbot had criticized diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI) policies of leading universities as treating "people as members of a group rather than as individuals, repeating the mistake that made possible the atrocities of the 20th century." Abbot went further, comparing the DEI initiative to the racial ideology of Nazi Germany, which "drove many of the best scholars out [of universities]." Although these statements were provocative and arguably insensitive, freedom of speech never belonged only to the people with whom we agree, as George Orwell pithily observed: "If liberty means anything at all, it means the right to tell people what they do not want to hear." Whether Abbot's comparison was sound or misguided is irrelevant to its protection under free speech. Yet MIT still withdrew his Carlson Lecture invitation-for the simple reason that his views were unpopular. By this point, DEI programs, which had started with laudable aims and some success in reducing discrimination against women and racial and sexual minorities, had already morphed into restrictions on the speech of people who had different opinions from what the standard DEI programs maintained. Led by human resource departments, compulsory trainings sought to impose uniform interpretations and practices. In surveys in the early 2020s, a majority of Americans reported that their employer had compulsory DEI training, and many students, academics, and corporate employees reported that these programs limited their freedom of expression and that they felt compelled to say things that they did not really believe. According to a University of Michigan professor, "You cannot question DEI, cannot reason against DEI, or speak about anything related to DEI (unless you constantly virtue signal and support it unquestionably)." The apogee came with new hiring practices among several leading academic institutions. In 2018, the University of California, Berkeley, birthplace of the free speech movement, introduced a specific rubric for assessing faculty candidates' commitment to DEI. In essence, one couldn't get a job at Berkeley without favorably repeating the standard DEI lines. In one life sciences faculty search process, 679 out of the 893 candidates who had the basic qualifications were eliminated because they did not meet the required criteria for "contributions to diversity, equity and inclusion." This conclusion was reached on the basis of their deidentified diversity statements. The bottom line: The next Charles Darwin or Ronald Fisher wouldn't be able to get a faculty position at Berkeley life sciences unless they unconditionally agreed with all of the official DEI statements. DEI emphasis also became common in secondary education. Several schools took it to the next level, using books by authors such as Ibram X. Kendi that emphasize the systemic and widespread nature of racism in the United States-which many white, middle-class parents perceived as a charge of racism leveled against them. In 2021, California passed the controversial Assembly Bill 101, mandating that all high school students complete a semester-long ethnic studies course to graduate. Although a statewide curriculum has not been adopted as of 2025, two dozen school districts have opted for a curriculum that heavily emphasizes issues of ongoing repression of ethnic minorities, racism, and white supremacy.
