Books such as Howard Zinn's A People's History of the United States, which provides an "anti-history," interpreting the American past as an uninterrupted process of exploitation and suppression of marginalized groups, were adopted as history textbooks across the U.S. Among other things, the book challenges all of the common narratives of the American past. It argues that the origin story of the United States is genocidal, and rather than standard histories that praise figures like Abraham Lincoln for ending slavery, it emphasizes Lincoln's racism and inadequacies on issues of race. The American Textbook Council called the book's influence in secondary education "possibly unparalleled," and over 170,000 teachers now subscribe to the newsletter of the Zinn Education Project, which prepares teaching materials based on the book. While presenting different viewpoints to students is an important part of teaching critical thinking, many parents came to view this turn in American education as an imposition on their children of a uniform set of values, often opposed to their own. Restrictions on freedom of expression can be seen beyond educational institutions. The New York Times is the standard-bearer of left-leaning liberal ideas in the United States, with influence extending not just nationally but globally. In a number of cases, New York Times journalists and editors were dismissed because they used the wrong words (sometimes when quoting sources); because they allowed op-eds by conservatives with controversial views (in particular, U.S. Senator Tom Cotton) to be published; or because they themselves held conservative opinions. Extreme interpretations of gender and transgender issues also took root, forcing out individuals who questioned them, even when they were themselves from the LGBT community or were established feminist scholars (for example, Kathleen Stock in the U.K. or Kathleen Lowrey in Canada). Debate on even ostensibly scientific issues, such as the origins of COVID and the usefulness of school closures to slow down the virus, became impossible because of mounting intolerance on both the Left and the Right. Monitoring of thought and speech intensified with social media, as I discuss in the next chapter. Attacks on those straying from accepted speech, language, and views became commonplace with a movement that some started calling "cancel culture." Those deviating from the progressive script could be shunned, be verbally attacked, and sometimes lose friends and jobs. This dynamic was much exacerbated by right-wing provocateurs and the emergence of a hard-right subculture online. While the Left used "canceling" and other forms of ostracism, hard-right activists resorted to online threats of violence, which sometimes turned into actual violence. The social engineering of educated elites, often rooted in ideals of creating a better, fairer, and more tolerant society, is light-years away from the violent stamping out of all independent thought during the Cultural Revolution. It was nevertheless the imposition of one set of values and viewpoints on the rest of society without deliberation or attempts to activate self-government -a hallmark of social engineering. How did this type of social engineering emerge in the U.S. and Western Europe? "A New Cultural Unity" "Social engineering" refers to efforts to shape the beliefs, values, and priorities of society. One important leg of social engineering is indoctrination, which takes place in the formative years of individuals and in the education system, as well as in families. Indoctrination strives to inculcate certain values in people-without critical thinking or questioning. It isn't a coincidence that the first focus of the Cultural Revolution was schools and teachers. The other major leg of social engineering is thought policing. I use this term to refer to efforts to ensure that people do not depart from accepted speech, expression of thoughts, ideas, allegiances, and other forms of symbolic behavior. While indoctrination takes place mostly during schooling, thought policing takes place when individuals, including adults, are engaged in their daily lives. The objective isn't to hide certain types of unaccepted speech and behavior patterns. As with indoctrination, the fundamental aim of thought policing is to instill values by ensuring that people become accustomed to publicly, and then privately, proclaiming things that they did not believe in previously. Both indoctrination and thought policing eschew any kind of critical thinking (where people would have to weigh the pros and cons of what is presented to them) and deliberation (where, in line with the ideas of self-government, individuals and communities would have a say on the values imposed on them). This is what distinguishes social engineering from other methods of education and debate aimed at convincing people of ideas or viewpoints. Every society, not just communist China, relies on social engineering to some extent. To live in any society means communicating and cooperating with others. In almost all societies, it also means adherence to norms and institutions. Regimes throughout the ages have worried that without inculcation of a shared language and value system, society will break down, institutions will atrophy, and those holding power will be dethroned. Social engineering is often the path of least resistance in achieving these objectives. It is only natural that a liberal society should also strive to inculcate its values in students and citizens. In contrast to autocratic regimes, liberal society can attempt to do this in a way that is consistent with critical thinking and deliberation. Yet the urge for social engineering often creeps in among liberal elites as well, and it becomes stronger because of the dilemmas of liberalism we encountered in chapter 5. Certain social currents can turbocharge the impulse to engineer minds, especially when powered by a Rousseau-style belief in the supremacy of a general will that needs to be imposed on society so that true social freedom can be realized. The postindustrial political settlement has increasingly buckled under that pressure. As the divide between the college educated and the rest widened, and as cultural issues became more important, the desire of the ascendant college educated to impose their values and push for the kind of cultural change that they thought beneficial intensified. These social engineering efforts also boosted regulations in order to achieve the same objectives. Procedures to ensure that corporations, communities, and individuals abided by the priorities of the college educated, ranging from antidiscrimination rules protecting various groups to environmental standards, multiplied. There was a top-down element in these efforts, as indoctrination, thought policing, and regulations were imposed on individuals and communities without deliberation. But it wasn't typically state led. Often, nongovernmental organizations, teachers, intellectuals, and activists were leading the charge, lending a more diffuse character to postindustrial social engineering.

I next present a general discussion of social engineering. I start with the "demand" for these efforts stemming from the wishes of regimes and political elites to bolster their rule, and the corresponding "supply" of social engineering, provided by individuals who possess the right qualifications and ideology to serve in such roles. My purpose is to document notable commonalities that hold across diverse social engineering efforts. Many such efforts start with a top-down demand, which leads to the recruitment of relatively skilled functionaries who are or become committed to the cause. This then paves the way to the supply-driven phase of social engineering, led by these functionaries, who are motivated by both ideology and a desire to maintain or boost their own social power. Postindustrial social engineering was led by a well-educated and committed cadre, recruited over time to administrative, managerial, and teaching positions. With the rising importance of cultural issues, it was only natural that this cadre would wish to pursue its convictions on how to improve society's values and attempt to increase its own social power in the process. I additionally explore how social engineering tries to create greater uniformity and what the influential political scientist James C. Scott called "legibility" as part of its efforts to control thoughts and values. Uniformity and legibility are thus almost necessary conditions for control from above -whether it comes from the state or segments of civil society. Scott describes this in the context of French efforts to impose administrative uniformity: "Establishing uniform standards of weight and measurement across the kingdom had a greater purpose than just making trade easier; the new standards were intended both to express and to promote a new cultural unity." This demand for uniformity also generates an affinity between social engineering and regulations. Setting rules and procedures helps ensure uniformity, and the greater the uniformity social engineering can impose, the more effective these rules will be. Hence, the quest for uniformity is synergistic with regulation. I conclude with a discussion of how, despite all of its capable tools, social engineering often generates backlash, because communities are not as malleable and powerless as they are sometimes presumed to be by social engineers.

"War Is Peace" Many rulers and statespeople have strived to indoctrinate "the most savage creature on earth," as Plato called the uneducated. They also would have agreed with Thomas Jefferson, who advised: "Educate and inform the whole mass of the people, enable them to see that it is their interest to preserve peace and order, and they will preserve it." However, they differed from Jefferson in one crucial respect: They cared less about peace and order and more about preserving their regime. That impulse is why every government practices some degree of social engineering-and why present- day institutions and those still to come will be no exception. If you can inculcate certain values in students in schools, you will gain adherence for life to a regime-or at least so goes the thinking. In most cases, there is no alternative to indoctrination. If you want the youth to believe that the emperor is ruling with the mandate of heaven, as Chinese emperors claimed to have done, then what's the use of critical thinking and questioning of proffered narratives? Students exposed to critical-thinking skills might start asking whether the killing of innocent civilians is something that heaven would condone, or how the Manchus, who used to be viewed as barbarians, suddenly received the mandate of heaven as they became the Qing dynasty's ruling elite. Better not to go there. For liberalism, the situation is more nuanced. Since liberalism generates better ideas, critical thinking and debate can help its ideas become accepted. Since much of liberalism is about freethinking, questioning dogmas should be celebrated, not discouraged. Nevertheless, as we saw in chapter 5, even liberal and democratic regimes have at times turned to the tools of indoctrination. Indoctrination is complemented by thought policing. Thought policing works by ruling out certain forms of speech, thought, and symbolic behavior. In their place, people are induced to use other words or express other views. The idea is that, although the policy begins as an external imposition, people will gradually internalize the prescribed beliefs. The policing can take the form of heavy-handed tactics-those who use the wrong language can be banished or imprisoned, or even worse, as in the Cultural Revolution. Tactics like peer pressure, ostracism, and disapproval are softer but still effective. Both forms of social engineering strive to do what George Orwell identified in Nineteen Eighty- Four, his dystopian novel inspired by Soviet totalitarianism. Orwell wrote: "Power is in tearing human minds to pieces and putting them together again in new shapes of your own choosing." Orwell coined the term "thought policing," as well as "thought crime" and "doublethink." The last concept captures the idea that individuals can (and are expected to) believe two contradictory things at the same time, as illustrated by the regime's slogan "War Is Peace, Freedom Is Slavery, Ignorance Is Strength." This is a first step toward thought policing becoming effective, since people will start professing and believing the opposite of their private truths in their public statements. Franz Kafka recognized the same dynamic in The Trial when he wrote: "The right understanding of any matter and a misunderstanding of the same matter do not wholly exclude each other." This is a process that can make people ultimately accept that "2 + 2 = 5," as they did in Orwell's novel. In Nineteen Eighty-Four's harrowing world, it is ultimately repression that maintains the system. But Orwell also recognized the role of peer pressure and other, less violent forms of thought policing. The Soviet Union deployed these tactics, although perhaps in slightly less absurd form than in Nineteen Eighty-Four and Mao's Cultural Revolution. This is how heroes of the revolution and former allies of Stalin were turned into enemies, and everybody had to accept it, hook, line, and sinker. For example, Lev Kamenev and Grigory Zinovyev, who formed the ruling troika with Stalin and engineered the marginalization of his main rival, Leon Trotsky, were later expelled from the party and ultimately executed as enemies of the regime. Of the forty-two members of the Politburo appointed between 1919 and 1952, only eleven survived to 1952. The overwhelming majority were executed, were assassinated, died in prison, or committed suicide. All party members and in fact all citizens had to hold the view that these, just like Kamenev and Zinovyev, were enemies of the regime. This is the reason why the opposition to Soviet rule came to refer to life under the regime as "living a lie." For a while, it was effective.

Supply and Demand for Social Engineering The demand for social engineering has a clear logic. Rulers and elites need it to bolster the regime and their special status within it. This is the reason education systems in the nineteenth century were introduced by authoritarian, and sometimes even democratic, governments to ensure that the masses remained loyal to the regime and to the hierarchy that it entailed. The Soviet system's need for thought policing, both in reality and in Orwell's fiction, had the same objectives. For traditional regimes in the nineteenth century, there was a powerful reason for worrying about these issues: the specter of socialism. This, too, is a more general phenomenon. Demand for social engineering is boosted when there are powerful threats to a regime-or dilemmas that it must grapple with. Social engineering is a labor-intensive activity. It needs a supply of functionaries who will devote their labor and ideally also their enthusiasm to this task. Schools need teachers. The Catholic Church needs priests. Thought policing requires credulous converts as well as snitches, as the East German secret police, the Stasi, made abundantly clear. When the files of the Stasi were made public, it was revealed that the organization employed upward of 270,000 people and spied on an estimated 6 million Germans, making it one of the most extensive surveillance networks in history. The Stasi's tactics give a glimpse of another conduit of social engineering: the community. Once efforts at thought policing leverage people's neighbors and acquaintances and form a powerful cage of norms, they become much more stultifying. The community is the chief avenue through which norms imposed by thought policing are propagated outward. This is what the Stasi achieved by recruiting so many regular people into its network of informants. A convincing ideology for the functionaries to rally around-religion, improving the human lot, or creating a utopian society-is also key to the success of social engineering. In most cases, functionaries are genuinely motivated by a desire to do good for society. Enthusiasm may appear to be optional in social engineering, but in reality, beliefs espoused by consummate cynics are unlikely to be as effective. This, too, was understood by Orwell, who wrote: "If you want to keep a secret, you must also hide it from yourself." But it's more than that. Social engineering is greatly facilitated and intensified when those engaged in it believe that they are enlightened and it is incumbent upon them to act according to that enlightenment-as can be seen in examples of religious social engineering but also, to some extent, in postindustrial social engineering. Most functionaries also need to be skilled. Teaching, other forms of indoctrination, and thought policing require education and the ability to convince others. Snitching, which could be done by anybody, may be the exception that proves the rule here. These considerations suggest that social engineering is most likely to be carried out by those who are well educated (at least relative to the standards of the society in which they live); have good communication skills; and strongly believe in their agenda. When mass schooling began in Europe, there were few teachers around. But the supply quickly followed the demand. This is an instance of the demand for social engineering creating its supply over time. In Britain, for example, the number of teachers went from fewer than 10,000 in 1800 to over 135,000 in 1900. The increase in France was even more pronounced, from also fewer than 10,000 to over 150,000 during the same period. Once demand creates a supply of functionaries, supply can drive a more bottom-up version of social engineering, going well beyond the initial demand. When there are enthusiastic people with the requisite knowledge, communication skills, and dedication, they may come to relish their role. This is not just because functionaries come to believe in their cause. It is also because the ability to impose values, beliefs, and practices on the population confers on them significant status and power. It can even be a source of income and livelihood. Hence, across a number of religions and other ideological projects, once priests, preachers, clerics, teachers, and intellectuals are recruited and empowered to engage in social engineering, they tend to want more of it. Throughout the ages, supply-driven social engineering has been as common as the demand-driven sort. This supply-driven dynamic is perhaps best illustrated by the status of the priestly Brahman class in the Indian caste system. The roots of this caste structure are still disputed, but there is little doubt that they go back at least 2,500 years. In the book Arthashastra, Kautilya, a leading adviser to the Mauryan emperor of the subcontinent in the fourth century BC, describes the caste system in a way that is still visible in India. Indians are divided into four varnas, Brahman, Kshatriya, Vaishya, and Shudra, and then there are the Dalit, or the "untouchables," considered so inferior that they are left out of the caste system altogether. Each varna is further subdivided into jatis that determine the exact occupations and social roles of their members. You may have thought that the highest status and power would be conferred on the warriors making up the Kshatriya, especially during the tumultuous times when many different powers were fighting for control of the Indian subcontinent. Yet the greatest power is in the hands of those who are engaged in the caste system's version of social engineering: the priestly Brahman, whose role is described by Kautilya as "study, teaching, performance of sacrifice, officiating in others' sacrificial performance, and the giving and receiving of gifts." Indoctrination and thought policing are powerful tools, even more so in contemporary industrialized nations with no clear warrior elite. In modern society, social engineering could just be the ticket to power.

"Reconstruct Society on an Imaginary Plan" In his seminal analysis of state-led schemes, James Scott emphasized how such top-down efforts first have to make society legible. "Legibility" means making local, complex practices more comprehensible to state functionaries and enabling their regulation. In the context of the introduction of tax records, cadastral surveys, and registers, Scott describes this as follows:

In each case, officials took exceptionally complex, illegible, and local social practices, such as land tenure customs or naming customs, and created a standard grid whereby it could be centrally recorded and monitored. For social engineering, imposing uniformity and legibility plays another important role: It enables the state and its functionaries to levy procedures as a way of regulating behavior. When local customs and practices vary (as they tend to do because of the acquisition and sharing of useful local knowledge), regulating them is challenging-every rule will create different reactions when pressed on communities with distinct customs. Uniformity could homogenize these reactions and make regulations feasible. In turn, effective regulations cement homogeneity (as people follow regulations and act more similarly), and this contributes to legibility and further control. Thus, uniformity, legibility, and regulations are generally symbiotic. One place where this can be seen clearly is, again, in the Soviet Union, where, once local customs and practices were partially erased and communities were made legible to Soviet authorities- sometimes by great violence and force, as in Ukraine-most things started being done via top-down directives and detailed five-year plans, called pyatiletka, drafted by the Soviet planning committee. Five-year plans were so central to the system that an asteroid discovered by the Soviet astronomer Tamara Mikhailovna Smirnova in 1971 was named Pyatiletka in honor of the five-year plan. Scott clearly understood the potential costs of such procedures, quoting Pierre-Joseph Proudhon's comment on utopian socialists: "They would reconstruct society on an imaginary plan, much like the astronomers for their own calculation make over the system of the universe."

Catholic Engineering Beginning in 1231, Master Konrad von Marburg, empowered by Pope Innocent III's decision to punish heresy and Pope Gregory IX's subsequent authorization to actively seek heretics, started roaming the German countryside. Traveling from village to village, he interrogated people on their beliefs. Combining the church's desire to ensure true uniformity of beliefs with the medieval conviction that fire could smoke out satanic ideas and evil, he burned those who gave the wrong answers. In the words of a contemporary, "So many heretics were burned throughout Germany that their number could not be comprehended." There isn't a straight line from the origins of Christianity to an extreme form of social engineering where people are burned for heretical answers. To understand this journey, it is vital to recognize that, after a while, Catholic social engineering became supply driven, propelled by the church and the clergy, who firmly believed in their cause and wished to maintain and expand their power. Christianity was at first many things. It was a millenarian religion, heralding the coming fundamental transformation of society, for which Jesus Christ was the Messiah. It was a powerfully egalitarian set of beliefs, as illustrated by Christ's response to the question from a young man about how he could be saved. Christ answered, "If you want to be perfect, go, sell your possessions and give to the poor and you will have treasure in heaven. Then come, follow me," adding: "It is easier for a camel to go through the eye of a needle than for someone who is rich to enter the kingdom of God." It was this egalitarianism that made Christianity spread rapidly among urban poor populations. Equally, it was a religion of tolerance, as captured by Christ's well-known statements such as "Love your enemies" and "Turn the other cheek." Under Saint Paul's leadership, early Christianity began to draw in merchants. With Emperor Constantine's conversion, Christianity embarked on a path toward becoming a state religion. It turned into an arm of the Roman state, which gradually but fundamentally altered its character. Importantly, all of these aspects of Christianity coexisted, and so did many different local interpretations and fusions with other spiritual and religious traditions, well into the Middle Ages. The multitude of languages spoken across Christian communities only added to this rich variation. This diverse tapestry constrained the use of Christianity as a source of political and social power. Local practices were often not even comprehensible, and thus were by definition illegible, to church leaders. It should then be no surprise that there soon began efforts to impose uniformity over Christian beliefs and practices. This initial phase of social engineering was primarily driven by the demand for social control from Roman rulers. But church leaders and the clergy were indispensable, auguring the more supply-driven later phase. A first step in these efforts toward uniformity was the first (ecumenical) council of the entire Christian world, convened by Emperor Constantine's order in 325. Bishops from all Christian lands, but predominantly from the eastern Mediterranean, met at Nicaea, which is today the town of Iznik, Turkey. The main issue of the council was the "Arian controversy" initiated by a charismatic priest, Arius. Arius had challenged the Trinity, which maintains the unity between God the Father, God the Son, and God the Holy Spirit. He argued, instead, that because Jesus Christ, the Son, had been created at some point by God the Father, the two were not eternally coexistent and thus not at the same level. The bishop of Alexandria condemned Arius in 320 but was not able to subdue him. Arius continued to preach his message and mobilized supporters throughout the eastern Mediterranean to propagate his rival interpretation. Arius's flouting of the ecclesiastic hierarchy made the status quo ante, where there was toleration of different interpretations and no universally recognized canon (accepted sacred text), no longer tenable. The outcome at the council, which received almost unanimous support from the bishops (except two Libyan participants) was the Nicene Creed. The creed stated: "We believe ... in one Lord, Jesus Christ, the Son of God, begotten from the Father ... of one substance with the Father ... " In short, the Trinity was now church doctrine. At Nicaea, twenty church canons were also recognized, and this was followed by the declaration of the four Gospels of the New Testament as the official version of Christian teaching, whereas previously there were scores of different Gospel narratives. Further uniformity was later brought by agreeing on the official date for Easter, which was another controversial issue at the time. Still, the church did not have enough power to fully enforce homogeneous practices or ensure legibility against the background of widely varying local conditions and practices. At this point, only half of the Roman Empire's cities had a bishop at all, and even those that had a bishop were not under the control of the church hierarchy. Indeed, the Nicene Creed did not stop the Arian controversy. Many populations and even some subsequent emperors did not sign up to the Trinity and continued to flirt with Arian or other heretical beliefs. Nevertheless, it is telling that this agenda at Nicaea was initiated by Emperor Constantine in order to make Christianity part of the Roman state apparatus. These efforts continued after the fall of the Roman Empire, especially with the conversion of Clovis, the king of the Germanic tribe the Franks, to Christianity, together with all of his army. Clovis's move was as much strategic as religious. He wanted to combine the ecclesiastical hierarchy with his powerful Frankish army in his attempt to take control of much of the lands of the former Roman Empire. Attempts to impose uniformity were intensified under Clovis. His main rival for control over Roman lands, King Huneric of the Vandals and the Alans, had already taken control of much of North Africa, including Carthage. Huneric distinguished himself by departing from the Nicene Creed. He enthusiastically sent bishops advocating for it into exile, torture, or worse. What started with Clovis continued with another powerful Frankish leader, Charlemagne, who crowned himself emperor in Rome on Christmas Day 800. Charlemagne came to be known as the "sword and shield of the church." Charlemagne's forced conversions of defeated populations and efforts to establish an accepted canon for the church were also ways of projecting his own power over much of Europe. This is the reason Charlemagne cared as much about church building and religious standardization as he did about military campaigns. What started with Charlemagne continued with the Crusades and the Teutonic wars, where mass conversions were intensified, including the forced conversion of the entire population of the Grand Duchy of Lithuania. Throughout this period, together with church expansion, there was a concomitant increase in the number of priests. The reason Charlemagne was so concerned about uniformity of worship practices in new churches was that many of the new priests were local men, mixing their pagan beliefs and local customs with the church's teachings. Charlemagne feared that without tight control over priests' teachings, all this church expansion would weaken the link between Christianity and empire. Another round of imposition of legibility and uniformity was needed. Saint Augustine, who made several efforts to introduce uniform interpretations in church doctrine, suggested in the fifth century that the Roman state and the church were distinct entities but could sometimes work together. In his celebrated work City of God, he argued that the Christian church and belief were predestined to conquer the "City of Man." Although the reality at this point was that the Roman state was the dominant partner, Augustine would prove to be right. The City of God became more independent and the main seat of Catholic social engineering after the early Middle Ages. Several of the controversies that required homogenization and regulation were internal to the church, including the issue of simony (the sale of sacraments and ecclesiastical offices), celibacy of priests (whether or not it was optional), the acceptance of the standard canon, and, most importantly, papal authority. Four hundred years later, Pope Innocent III would mention in his invitation to a new council that its aim was to "eradicate vices and to plant virtues, to correct faults and to reform morals, to remove heresies and to strengthen faith, to settle discords and to establish peace." This was followed by other popes who continued with attempts to strengthen the church's authority relative to the City of Man. At a time when most people had no formal schooling, Catholic social engineering delegated indoctrination and thought policing to clerics. People's beliefs were formed in the church, and the local priest was also in charge of ensuring that they did not deviate from the prescribed modes of thought and behavior later in their lives. The institution of confession was one aspect of this control. The 1215 decree by Pope Innocent III and the Fourth Lateran Council demanded annual confession from all Christians and enabled priests to impose penance and other punishments for transgressions, deepening the control that the priests exercised over people's daily lives. It wasn't just confession. Many other formal rules and regulations, and all the rituals that went with them, were also introduced and imposed as far as the church's power extended. The history of early Christianity is one where top-down demand created its own supply and then social engineering became supply driven. All of this teaching and imposition of uniform standards required functionaries. Over time, the total number of church members increased severalfold. Priests, especially those from various orders such as the Dominicans, Franciscans, and Jesuits, were sincere believers in their own enlightenment and became enthusiastic enforcers of standard practices. They were joined by other monks, friars, and nuns. Many local priests were at first trying to deal with complex problems in terms of ensuring cooperation and cohesion in their communities, and did so by building on local norms. But with intensifying social engineering, priests got into the business of imposing uniform norms and beliefs on their flocks. They did, of course, firmly believe in Christianity's message and its ability to save people's souls-but social engineering had also become a major source of power for them. The nadir of social engineering was the Inquisition, as exemplified by Master Konrad's burning of heretics. This was part of a much larger effort driven by church functionaries-not by rulers trying to defend their regimes. In the centuries after the Reformation, following Martin Luther's influential Ninety-Five Theses that launched Protestantism, the Catholic Church doubled down on indoctrination and thought policing. Beginning with the Council of Trent (1545-63), the church tried to stop the spread of Protestantism by more closely policing religious practice and thought. Recent scholarship suggests that these efforts did more than slow down the advance of the dangerous ideas of Protestantism; they stopped scientific inquiry and even the sharing of scientific findings.

The Ulema Forbid the Wrong After assuming power in the early eighteenth century, the founder of the Saudi state, Muhammad ibn Saud, formed a fateful alliance with the conservative cleric Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab. Abd al-Wahhab maintained a rigid interpretation of Islam, viewing all sorts of activities as sinful. He advocated holy war against Muslims who had strayed from the teachings of the prophet. This made him a useful partner in ibn Saud's efforts to establish his dominance over the entire Arabian Peninsula. The Saudi-Wahhabi alliance was successful not just militarily but also for social control. Throughout the following two centuries, Wahhabis collaborated with Saud's troops to conquer new lands and subdue populations. They advocated tight controls over these populations to ensure prayer, traditional interpretations of Islamic beliefs and norms, pilgrimage, general piety, and, most importantly, obedience to the Saudi-Wahhabi authority. They also imposed a particularly suffocating cage of norms, with everyone policing everyone else's behavior to avoid any deviation from their ultraconservative brand of Islam. There are passages in the Quran that support community-level efforts to curb vice and encourage virtue, for example, "Let there be one community of you, calling to good, and commanding right and forbidding wrong." Wahhabis took this to the next level, with patrols roaming the streets during prayer times to enforce participation. Over time, Wahhabi influence extended from thought policing to indoctrination as conservative clerics started taking over schools as well as the teaching of religion to boys. (Until 1955, there was no public education for girls.) Education, such as it was under the Wahhabi influence, focused on rote learning and memorization of the Quran. Wahhabis were also the driving force behind the Committee for the Promotion of Virtue and Prevention of Vice, which is the religious police force of modern Saudi Arabia in charge of ensuring that people follow the regime's brand of Islam to the letter, with a strict dress code for all women. These were the police who forced fourteen middle school girls to burn to death inside a Mecca school in 2002 because the girls were not sufficiently well covered while trying to escape the fire. Wahhabi social engineering in the service of Saud rule is the peak of an even more striking example of supply-driven social control than the Catholic version: the strict imposition of a rigid, traditionalist interpretation of Islam on Muslim populations by the ulema (literally, the "learned men") starting in the eleventh century. As in the Saudi-Wahhabi example, it enabled an alliance between the ulema and state institutions, beginning with the Abbasid caliphate and then with subsequent dynasties, including the Turkic-Islamic empires of Seljuks and Ottomans, all the way to the Sauds. During the first three centuries of Islam, scholars played an important role in interpreting the teachings of the Quran and the pronouncements of the Prophet Mohammed, the hadith. But they generally stayed separate from the secular authority, including that of the ruler of the Islamic world, the caliph. During this period, Islam expanded rapidly and showed some openness to science and new knowledge-certainly compared to medieval Europe, which was often deeply suspicious of any idea that could threaten church dogma. In the ninth century, during the Abbasid caliphate, cracks began opening. A major debate broke out between scholars, centering on the question of whether the Quran was created by God or is coeternal with God (not unlike the debates on the Trinity in Christianity). One school, which came to be known as the "rationalists," took the former position, while another school, the "traditionalists," adopted the latter and maintained that only the most traditional, unquestionable version was allowed. No openness to new ideas would be permitted, according to the traditionalist interpretation. Abbasid caliphs inserted themselves into the divide between the traditionalists and rationalists, oscillating between the two across different reigns. But the critical change came as the Abbasids were losing their grip on the large Islamic Empire in the early eleventh century. Anything to increase their control would be welcome at this point, and social engineering to impose greater uniformity on religious beliefs and transform them into a method of control was an obvious step. Caliph al- Qadir issued the Qadiri Creed in 1017, siding with the traditionalists. The creed condemned and all but eliminated the rationalist school. From now on, the traditionalist interpretation, offered by the ulema, was essentially state religion. The triumph of the traditionalists increased the power of the ulema and ushered in a stricter regime of indoctrinating children in a rigid interpretation of Islam and, more crucially, a zealous atmosphere of thought policing. In this environment, intellectual openness-whether in the form of new ideas, scientific inquiry, or interpretive flexibility-came to be seen as sinful. The traditionalists did much more than temporarily solidify Abbasid rule. They established the ulema as the sole authority with the power to interpret religious texts and precedents. Before the eleventh century, although no controversy was resolved democratically, there was a process called ijma, through which the ulema could debate and reach a sort of consensus. The traditionalists stifled ijma, claiming that consensus had already been reached on most issues. The immutability of Islamic teachings and the learned men's monopoly over interpreting them also implied that the ulema and the rulers they supported would be much harder to challenge. When the Seljuks conquered much of the Middle East, including the critical cities of Baghdad and Damascus and large parts of Anatolia, and set up their own Islamic Empire, one might have expected the traditionalist influences to subside. After all, Seljuk practices were as far from the rigid traditionalist interpretations as one could have imagined. On earlier ceramics, Seljuk women are shown with uncovered hair and playing musical instruments. But Seljuks, as non-Arab outsiders, sorely needed legitimacy, which the ulema could confer. Thus, the power of the ulema was amplified as it helped stabilize the social order. In this shift, social engineering grew harsher: The Seljuks expanded the ulema's role beyond theology to policing public order and enforcing strict conformity with Islamic practice. The Seljuk-ulema alliance also took thought policing a step further by launching severe persecutions of sects that deviated from the traditionalist interpretation. The Ottomans, who, like the Seljuks, wished to brandish their Islamic credentials, continued and in some ways even intensified the same practices. Throughout these centuries, the ulema were the main driver of indoctrination and thought policing and derived considerable social power from this. Several scholars interpret the alliance between the ulema and the rulers as the critical factor in the rise of enduring authoritarianism in Islamic lands-no opposition to the rulers could form in the cage of norms bolstered by the powerful ulema. Overall, the purification of Islam was a supply-driven social engineering effort, led by a group with relatively good education and specialized skills. Like the social engineers of the Catholic Church and the postindustrial era, these intellectuals had a coherent set of values and believed firmly that they were the enlightened ones. They were devoted to their cause, even if they also sought to increase their own social power and control over society. The strictures they imposed endured for the next nine centuries. At the beginning of the twentieth century, the power of the ulema was still palpable, not just in Arabian deserts but throughout the vast territories of the Ottoman Empire.

The Power of the Exam The mandarin, bureaucrats selected via the imperial examination system called the keju, commanded considerable status and power in Imperial China. They were at the forefront of designing and implementing regulations and policing the population's values and practices, especially with the advent of neo-Confucianism, which became state philosophy starting in the tenth and eleventh centuries. The keju was first introduced in 604 during the Sui dynasty. It had an overtly political aim: The noble clans wielded considerable power against the emperor, both administratively and militarily, and frequently intervened in state affairs, sometimes violently deposing emperors. During the Northern Wei dynasty, 76 percent of ministers were from the great clan families. In case of a disagreement between these families and the emperor, these ministers would side with the former. The keju altered this equilibrium in two ways. First, it created a ready supply of bureaucrats, so that the emperor did not have to rely on members of the noble clans for administrative tasks. Second, the exam system, especially during the Tang dynasty, built up an excess supply of potential functionaries for bureaucratic appointments, strengthening the hand of the imperial court, which could replace any threatening or disloyal bureaucrats immediately. These features are also responsible for introducing the meritocratic character of the exam system-potential functionaries had to be well trained and correctly selected. Tang Emperor Taizong understood the power thus conferred on him when he stated: "The world's men of unusual ambitions have thus been trapped in my bag." The power of the mandarin class as an autonomous force was subsequently amplified during the violent Tang-Song transition. The more recognizable form of the keju took shape during the Northern Song dynasty, with three rounds of exams, major preparations for each round, and a generally meritocratic emphasis. It was also after this stage that it became essentially the only way of entering the imperial bureaucracy. Over time, the subject of the exams became narrower, focusing almost entirely on rote memorization of Confucian texts and teachings. This did not undo the meritocratic character of the exam but limited the skill set that successful exam takers needed to master. They did not acquire other skills that could turn them into businesspeople or independent power holders. In the neo- Confucian synthesis that emerged, bureaucrats were completely dependent on imperial power and the emperor could fire or even execute them at will. But increasingly, imperial power also depended on the bureaucrats, who became indispensable in setting rules and policing society and more committed to playing this role. Hence, even in one of the most top-down social engineering schemes in history, the functionaries responsible for these engineering efforts became its main engine. Exam passers, especially ones appointed in various branches of the bureaucracy, acquired a huge amount of influence. They regulated and monitored behavior across the vast empire. In the words of a nineteenth-century observer, a bureaucrat "may not be capable of assisting the emperor, but he is more than resourceful in oppressing the commoners." This oppression took the classic social engineering form, with indoctrination (the top-down character of the whole system decreed what children anywhere could learn) and thought and behavior policing (not just words but deeds, dress codes, and hairstyles were all imposed from the top). Imperial bureaucrats also introduced myriad regulations for everything. Imperial China was far ahead of contemporary civilizations in its recordkeeping, and a lot of these records were about detailed laws, procedures, and regulations. Over time, the keju became not just a channel of upward mobility but the channel, which people of all backgrounds coveted. Preparing boys for the exam starting from an early age became a booming business. Merchant families, rather than grooming their sons to take over the business, began educating them for the exam. A contemporary summed up the keju's effects as follows: Since the introduction of the examination system ... scholars have forsaken their studies, peasants the ploughs, artisans their crafts, and merchants their trades; all have turned their attention to but one thing-government office.

As a result, efforts to pass the exam became a lifelong affair. We have information about twelve thousand keju takers during the Ming dynasty. The average age of passing the final stage of the exam was thirty-two, because many took the exam multiple times, undeterred by early failures. Some were able to pass the final stage of the exam only in their late fifties. When the keju was abolished in 1905, it triggered a massive revolt that played an important role in bringing down the entire Chinese Empire. Before this abolition, the number of people who would pass the exam from different prefectures was determined by a quota system. Some prefectures had a much larger quota relative to their population, and their exam takers were thus more hopeful about eventual success. After the abolition, prefectures with a generous quota, where there were more would-be elite bureaucrats frustrated about losing their potential social power, generated more intense revolutionary activity against the state during the 1911 uprisings. The rebellion was led by candidates who would have previously enjoyed all the social status and economic benefits of imperial appointments. Even in this setting, where the origins of social engineering are most clearly top-down and demand driven, we see how the functionaries themselves became its drivers and the main force propagating the system.

Social Engineering Zeal James Scott highlights four elements that combine to make top-down schemes most intense. The first is the presence of modern statecraft and administrative tools. Before the presence of such tools, the ability of state institutions to penetrate society and forcefully reorder it was limited. Hence, czarist Russia's ability to extract grain and wreak havoc in its hinterland was more circumscribed than that of Soviet Russia under Stalin. The second element Scott calls "high-modernist ideology," which he identifies as a firm belief in scientific and technical progress and its application to influence (and improve) all human affairs. Scott points out that

the troubling features of high modernism derive, for the most part, from its claim to speak about the improvement of human condition with the authority of scientific knowledge and its tendency to disallow other competing sources of judgment.

In fact, there are two aspects packed in Scott's high modernism: a conviction on the part of social engineers in their own enlightenment, so that their way will improve the human condition, and a belief in their ability to use technology and scientific knowledge in this project. The third element is the presence of an authoritarian state that can use coercion to effectuate this high-modernist agenda without significant institutional checks. The fourth is the weakness of civil society, making it unable to resist such impositions. Scott summarizes these four elements as follows:

In sum, the legibility of a society provides the capacity for large-scale social engineering, high-modernist ideology provides the desire, the authoritarian state provides the determination to act on that desire, and an incapacitated civil society provides the leveled social terrain on which to build.

To Scott's list we should add a fifth element: the presence of a genuine set of problems and dilemmas. Scott himself anticipates this in the quotation I started this chapter with-many social engineering efforts have well-intentioned roots. The dynamics of social engineering, especially the zeal to impose science, technology, and better practices on an otherwise less enlightened or stubbornly traditional population, can open the door to intense indoctrination and thought policing. You can see all five of these elements in some of the more intense examples of social engineering, such as the Soviet attempts to reshape society. The Soviets leveraged a highly authoritarian institutional structure and a weak civil society. They were dealing with complex problems associated with establishing central planning and extracting enough output to fuel industrialization and the war effort during World War II. They had their own high-modernist ideology, with a fierce belief in their enlightened communism and their way of using science and technology. The Chinese Cultural Revolution was equally zealous in believing in its own enlightenment but lacked the scientific aspect of high modernism, highlighting that all five elements are not strictly necessary for disastrous social engineering. Several of the elements, including a conviction in their own enlightenment but not the tools of modern statecraft and the technological aspects of high modernism, are also present in the example of Catholic social engineering and the purification of Islam under the leadership of the ulema. And a number of these elements apply to our own postindustrial society.

Postindustrial Social Engineering We are very far from the violence of the Inquisition, the cruel absurdity of the Cultural Revolution, and the frightening thought policing of the Wahhabi. Nevertheless, the United States and several other industrialized countries have experienced their own version of social engineering that regulates language, speech, and thought. The college graduates entering various administrative, information technology, human resource, teaching, and thought-leadership roles were the functionaries responding to growing demand for this type of work from governments and corporations during the digital age. Once they started occupying these positions, the college educated became the supply that drove social engineering, not unlike the historical examples we have seen. As in several of the other examples, the college educated were also convinced of their own enlightenment-in this case, based on their superior values and skills. The fact that the college educated were mostly right in their concerns about the social costs of ignorance and intolerance didn't make their social engineering efforts any less intrusive. The new element here is that this college-educated group is heterogeneous, and the deep divide within it shaped the nature of the social engineering effort. The majority of those who obtained college education-the mass college educated-did not have the stratospheric incomes we see in the tech sector or on Wall Street. Nor did they enjoy the same social status or veneer of meritocratic success. Partly as a result of these differences, they also developed distinct values and emphases. Elite graduates-the small group within the college educated that enjoyed the biggest economic rewards and the most visible social status-favored free markets and entrepreneurship. The more numerous mass college educated were more likely to lean toward redistribution and state intervention. This unstable state of affairs could be rectified if there was a common denominator around which the majority of the college educated could rally. It would be a big bonus if this agenda could boost the social power and status of parts of the mass-college-educated group. Cultural issues provided this common denominator, and the fact that the dilemmas of liberalism were there and becoming more important in a globalizing, rapidly changing society made them true problems that needed tackling. This, I argue, is the origin of postindustrial social engineering. That this is an example of supply-driven social engineering can be seen from the fact that its impetus didn't come from a ruler or a regime that needed legitimization. The efforts were driven by a group with a coherent set of values, the right kind of education, and strong communication skills-and a desire to increase its status and social power. The coherent set of values was rooted in the educational experience of many of the college graduates, which emphasized the primacy of science, basic freedoms, certain universal values, and the elevation of cosmopolitan values and global cultures relative to local ones. The first of Scott's five elements-the ability of the state and its employees to use administrative tools-is clearly present in postindustrial social engineering. The reach of the administrative state and, together with it, the involvement of the college educated in public administration, had significantly expanded across all industrialized nations over the twentieth century. The college educated were also already penetrating every aspect of nongovernmental organizations and corporate hierarchies, especially for managerial and administrative tasks. Hence, they were already at home in using state (and corporate) power for data collection and processing, control, and regulation. The fifth element is very strong. The dilemmas of liberalism are real, and the solution that the liberal college-educated groups came up with was not a chimera: Many philosophers had advocated a zero-tolerance strategy toward intolerance and tackling ignorance via education (including indoctrination). The second element-a type of high-modernist ideology-is also clear and significant. The dilemmas of liberalism had turned thornier as society became more diverse and thoroughly globalized. Issues of tolerance now cut close to the bone, and college graduates had developed an affinity for cosmopolitan values and global problems, which they had been exposed to thanks to their university training, business experiences, entertainment diets, and travels. These dilemmas could no longer be relegated to the back burner, as they had been during the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries. In the eyes of the educated group, their solution was to apply science, technology, and their own enlightened, universal values. The tip of the spear would be those with the requisite education and enthusiasm for these values-that is, themselves. This high-modernist instinct became amplified once the cultural agenda started being shared with the tech sector, which was viewed as the apex of the application of science and technology. Thus, in one corner were the college educated and the tech sector, with enlightenment, expertise, science, and ingenuity on their side. The other corner was made up of those still clinging to ignorance and intolerance (or so thought many of the college educated). The geographic, economic, and social separation of these two groups over the last several decades widened the gulf- and reduced communication and empathy-between them. Viewed in this light, Hillary Clinton's infamous comment calling Trump supporters "deplorables" doesn't seem so out of whack with the culture of the times. The third element of Scott's list-an authoritarian state-is missing from postindustrial social engineering. This is why efforts in advanced economies have focused on persuasion and argumentation, relying on appeals to scientific ideas, peer pressure, and sometimes ostracism, rather than coercive tools. This does not necessarily render them less potent, as Charles Sanders Peirce's quotation at the beginning of this chapter anticipated. This is also what made postindustrial social engineering nonviolent and potentially more prone to backlash. The fourth element is more complex. Civil society in the United States and Europe has been strong and active by historical standards. But then, civil society was the main driver of postindustrial social engineering. The more intense aspects of social engineering, as the above examples illustrate, came from teacher unions, universities, philanthropic foundations, nongovernmental organizations, and the media. This implied that, rather than being a check on social engineering, civil society may have created a novel cage of norms imposing thought policing on the population and sowing the seeds of new indoctrination in the schooling system. And this became amplified by social media, as we will see in the next chapter. This combination shaped the nature of postindustrial social engineering. It focused on education, beginning in elementary and middle school and continuing all the way to university. Social engineering had to start early and crescendo into a new set of values in higher education, preferably not just in a top-down manner but via the effects of peers and the general environment, which was becoming more open to liberal values. The emphasis on education would also produce more social power for those engaged in teaching, which included many college educated. But education, or indoctrination, is never enough for social engineering. Hence, the hope that more tolerance could be achieved and ignorance could be defeated via the right kind of instruction and encouragement in schooling made way for various forms of thought policing. Academics, university administrators, student bodies, respectable media outlets, and sometimes corporate leaders would decide what language and ideas were permissible, and which ones were signs of bigotry or microaggressions and should thus be disallowed. This type of thought policing would also boost the social power of those who were at the forefront of defining values and imposing them on society. It was a bonus that this group would largely be drawn from the mass college educated, who were dissatisfied with their income and social status (especially as compared to elite graduates). The same considerations also increased the perceived usefulness of regulations. If only the right set of regulations could be imposed on society, this would ensure fair and just outcomes and society would be well ordered, even if some intolerance and ignorance remained. The proliferation of regulations, just as in other instances of social engineering, was highly synergistic with the agenda of making complex practices legible. In many cases, regulations also appeared to be an expedient solution to the difficulty of building a democratic coalition for passing legislation. So instead of a carbon tax and more robust subsidies for clean innovations, college-educated elites and activists could resort to complex environmental regulations. Rather than dealing with root causes of economic disadvantage among minorities or social problems in poor neighborhoods, imposing more procedures and antidiscrimination regulations on companies and federal contractors appeared to be the path of least resistance, congruent with social engineering. This equilibrium had the virtue of not using any coercion. However, this also made the whole postindustrial social engineering agenda much more vulnerable to backlash, particularly once right- wing political entrepreneurs, media personalities, and social media influencers started exploiting the discontent it was creating.

Backlash Even when social engineering is most intense, and even when it has state institutions to back it up, there is no guarantee of success, because those with different values will resist indoctrination and especially thought policing. This resistance might take many different forms. Citizens of the Soviet Union and Eastern Europe did not have the luxury of openly espousing anticommunist ideas. But they could privately believe different things from what the public discourse maintained. This is the reason why when the Berlin Wall and then later the Soviet Union fell, there was a very rapid-arguably discontinuous-change in public statements and visible enthusiasm for democracy and the market system. A common form of social engineering, especially in the context of building allegiance to a nation- state, centers on language policies. This, too, was anticipated by George Orwell in Nineteen Eighty- Four, where the regime had its own language, Newspeak. Many new nations, as they were being concocted by fusing together previously disparate communities, used language as a tool for ensuring uniformity. In France, the imposition of a common language on regions that spoke not just different dialects but entirely different tongues was a crucial step in successful nation building. When associated with coercion or during periods of war, such as the Napoleonic Wars in France, this worked. As a common saying in the field holds, "A language is a dialect with an army and a navy." But there are many examples in which the backlash has been palpable. The English were never able to erase the Scottish, Welsh, or Irish languages or cultures-and they did try. The Turkish state founded after the fall of the Ottoman Empire created its own language and imposed it throughout its territories, and yet Kurdish survived and remained the locals' main language in the least economically developed part of the new country. The American experience also illustrates the backlash. During World War I, anti-German feelings flared. Already during the war, German speakers were treated with suspicion. Many were harassed, and some were arrested as spies. There was a campaign to remove German words from daily usage, and hamburgers became "liberty steaks." After the war, several states took the next step. Before the U.S. entry into the war in 1917, German bilingual education had been common, as Germans were the largest group of immigrants at the time. After the war ended, several states outright banned German as a language of instruction, declaring that "all private and parochial schools ... shall be taught in the English language." This social engineering was meant to reshape the culture and allegiance of German Americans and accelerate their assimilation into American society. In the event, the opposite happened. There was a powerful counterreaction. German immigrants started choosing more distinctive German names for their offspring, and their rate of intermarriage with natives declined significantly. Two decades later, the rate of volunteering for the U.S. Army in World War II was lower in the affected German communities as a result of the backlash. We know this counterreaction was aimed specifically at the social engineering efforts-and was not merely a by-product of broader social trends-because two "control groups" showed no comparable shift: people who completed their education before the bans took effect and those who grew up in states that never imposed such restrictions. Moreover, Germans who were already in mixed marriages didn't exhibit the same reaction, either. An important element in such backlash is, again, the community. An individual in isolation is much less likely to exhibit a powerful backlash than a community of like-minded people who can leverage solidarity among themselves in order to withstand social engineering. This account holds clues about why the postindustrial social engineering efforts, which went against communities that were deemed to be ignorant or intolerant, were likely to generate a powerful counterreaction. Backlash created a vicious circle for postindustrial social engineering. The urge for social engineering increased as the problems became increasingly complicated. Issues around cultural change and immigration, rather than being quickly resolved via a consensus around the values of liberalism, drew heightened controversy. They turned much more confrontational and emotive among both the social engineers and their targets, with provocation from right-wing media and leaders. In response, doubling down on social engineering seemed natural to many college-educated liberals. But this only deepened the backlash. Other factors exacerbated the problem. Most notably, social media intensified incentives for supply-driven social engineering by bringing together people intent on thought policing and expanding the possibilities for highly visible forms of norm enforcement, as I discuss in the next chapter. Social media also provided a vehicle for amplifying and organizing grievances against social engineering, aggravating the vicious circle. The backlash and the grievances set the scene for a more ominous set of attacks on all sorts of freedoms from antiliberal quarters. Today, we are witnessing renewed restrictions on freedom of expression and even research in universities in the United States. State legislatures in Florida, Texas, and elsewhere have passed laws that ban teachers from discussing "divisive concepts" related to race or gender, and have threatened educators with dismissal for exploring uncomfortable history. The federal government is following suit. Talk radio and some TV networks have purged hosts who express doubt about the claims that Joe Biden's victory in the 2020 presidential election was fraudulent. The antiliberal social engineering may yet become more coercive. Social engineering begets social engineering.

Chapter 9

Liberalism in the Age of Algorithms

Social media algorithms amplify outrage and enable mass public shaming without context. Digital platforms corrode deliberation and undermine reasoned liberal discourse.

▼

Liberalism in the Age of Algorithms

Social media is biased, not to the Left or the Right, but downward. -Jaron Lanier

The hope is that, in not too many years, human brains and computing machines will be coupled together very tightly, and that the resulting partnership will think as no human brain has ever thought and process data in a way not approached by the information-handling machines we know today. -J. C. R. Licklider

On a December evening in 2013, Justine Sacco, then the director of corporate communications for the New York-based media firm IAC, boarded a flight from Heathrow to Cape Town. Before takeoff, she sent a deadpan joke to her 170 followers on Twitter: "Going to Africa. Hope I don't get AIDS. Just kidding. I'm white!" Phone off. While she slept on the plane, the tweet began rocketing across the globe. In just eleven hours, Sacco shot up to the number one trending topic on Twitter. Bloggers, celebrities, and strangers she would never meet denounced her as an incorrigible racist. The hashtag #HasJustineLandedYet circulated widely as tens of thousands of people waited online in anticipation of her reckoning. One Twitter user gloated: "We are about to watch this Justine Sacco bitch get fired, in real time, before she even knows she's being fired." Colleagues joined in, too. One tweeted: "I'm an IAC employee and I don't want @JustineSacco doing any communication on our behalf ever again. Ever." By the time Sacco set foot in Cape Town, she had been crowned the internet villain of the week -and was now unemployed to boot. Her employer, bowing to the wishes of the online community, had swiftly fired her in absentia. Later, she would explain that her intention was to "mimic and mock what an actual racist, ignorant person would say." But the internet spared no time for explanations. What began with a handful of people noticing and commenting on the tweet soon snowballed when Twitter's algorithms amplified it into a trending topic. When journalist Jon Ronson wrote about Sacco in The New York Times, highlighting the absurdity of the public campaign against her and the damage that it had done to her mental health and reputation, he himself became the subject of social media attacks. Call it a tone-deaf satire, call it flatly offensive-either way, Sacco fired off one tweet to her 170 followers. The internet fired back an artillery barrage. By the time her plane landed, she had been shamed before a global audience and lost her job. Platforms that connect us also amplify outrage; in this new ecosystem, crowds can mete out punishment faster than any court. Sacco's ordeal was an early warning of how high the dial on social enforcement could now go. Sacco's case is not an isolated incident. Democratic pollsters, Silicon Valley engineers, teachers, authors, and academics have suffered similar attacks and ostracism, and have sometimes been fired or forced out of their jobs, because of social media storms. This is just one facet of our age of algorithms. Social media algorithms that boost the circulation of certain online content complicate free speech and create new cages of norms-with sometimes fiercer effects on freedom than the traditional communities and likely none of the potential benefits.

Algorithms do much more than build a new social media cage of norms. They reshape the production process and our social lives; they alter the distribution of income and economic opportunities; they transmute communication more broadly, beyond social media; and they impact how information is collected, processed, and aggregated. They can suppress or boost liberty. And they can potentially transform the benefits and dilemmas of liberalism. This chapter focuses on two aspects of the age of algorithms: first, the impacts of social media on communities and communication, and second, the promise of artificial intelligence to revolutionize every aspect of our lives, including which jobs are left for human workers and how we acquire and use knowledge. My bottom line is that algorithms do not change the social value of liberalism or the kind of liberal democracy we need. But they complicate the freedom of speech and intensify threats to it. We cannot do without them, either. Algorithms are interwoven with the future of work and wages, and thus with shared prosperity. Algorithms can serve as crucial engines for job creation, but currently, they are more likely to destroy jobs on a whim and deepen existing inequalities. They could facilitate the process of acquiring and disseminating collective knowledge-but not easily, and not on their current path. For all of these reasons, any attempt to rebuild liberalism and strengthen liberal democracy must grapple with shaping the direction of technology to be consistent with liberty and shared prosperity.

"Puppets Controlled by the Strings of Society" Community norms play a useful role in the sharing of information, in self-government, and in harnessing a sense of belonging and solidarity. The same norms can also restrict what community members may do, abridging freedoms. Different communities impose different cages, and some are much worse than others. Some communities may also impose cages with little benefit. While communities' power of policing is sometimes rooted in their ability to threaten violence, it works more often through peer pressure, the threat of ostracism, and suasion coming from those with social status and authority. Take one of the most famous experiments in social psychology, conducted by Stanley Milgram in 1961. This was the year in which Adolf Eichmann, the Nazi officer and bureaucrat in charge of the logistics of the Holocaust, stood trial in Jerusalem. The question on many people's minds was whether, if we found ourselves in similar circumstances, most of us would act in that barbaric fashion. Milgram designed a clever, if dubiously ethical, experiment. (Male) volunteers were recruited to play the role of a teacher or a subject, purportedly for an experiment on the effects of punishment on memory. In addition to a teacher and a subject, each group had an experimenter in a gray technician's coat, who would be running the experiment. Volunteers were told that they would be randomly assigned to the role of teacher or subject. Unbeknownst to them, they would always be assigned to be the teacher, and the person playing the subject was a confederate of Milgram's. The experiment wasn't about punishment and memory but about the willingness of ordinary people to follow authority, even when it was questionable and cruel. The teacher read pairs of words from a card the experimenter supplied. He then quizzed the subject, mentioning one of the words and asking him to supply the other. The teacher was also told that he should administer electric shocks of increasing voltage whenever the subject gave incorrect answers. The teacher himself was given an electric shock to show the effect (painful, but not too bad, since it was low voltage). He was also told that electric shocks would be painful to the subject, but there would be no permanent tissue or other damage. The subject was strapped to an electric chair and the experiment got underway. The subject, Milgram's confederate, would get the answers wrong, and the teacher would administer the electric shock. Cries followed. Many of the volunteers playing the role of the teacher wanted to stop at this point, but they were encouraged to go on by the experimenter. This is where authority came in: A technician in a gray coat, with knowledge and command of the situation, encouraged them to administer higher and higher voltage electric shocks, even as the subject was crying and begging the teacher to stop. Shockingly, about 65 percent of the teachers went up all the way to 450 volts, which, had it actually been administered, would have been almost deadly. The experiment is sometimes interpreted as showing that people can behave in awful ways once they have power over somebody else-like the power that the teacher held over the subjects or Nazi officers had over Jews, homosexuals, and leftists in concentration camps. Yet this wasn't Milgram's interpretation. In fact, most teachers did not want to go on after the first few electric shocks. They had to be coaxed or even pushed to do so. Several were so uncomfortable that they started sweating, twitching, and showing other signs of extreme discomfort. But a great majority still followed authority. It is striking how many people were willing to do this, even when the authority was asking them to do something patently wrong, cruel, and dangerous. Milgram himself concluded, "It may be that we are puppets-puppets controlled by the strings of society." Authority does not always come from people in technicians' coats or positions of power. The cage of norms is often enforced by the authority of the community-sometimes the elders, sometimes those with charisma, and more frequently, just the consensus of all the people who form the trusted group of an individual. Many people internalize the norms of the communities in which they live, because they want to fit in. They may also find it compelling or even exhilarating to impose the same norms on those who stray too far. This type of norm enforcement is one of the reasons why peer pressure and moral suasion are so effective.

The Algorithmic Cage Many communities in Africa have strong norms for sharing food and even money with both extended family and members of the community more broadly. People are not rich. It would make sense to save some of the excess food or income, as many in the West would do. So why do they act so generously? Part of it is that norms of sharing have been internalized. But equally important is peer pressure and a fear of ostracism. A housewife from southern Zambia explains:

It is not safe to deny them. You saw me give grain to that woman who came the other day. How could I refuse when she asked me for grain? Perhaps she would do nothing, but I could not tell. The only thing to do is to give.

She was afraid that if she didn't give the grain, there would be rumors spread about her. Perhaps she would be subject to sorcery or even worse. The social networks that instill such fear are exactly the same ones that are sometimes helpful. The same housewife is likely to need food at some future date, and when that happens, the process will work in reverse: Somebody else's fear of being ostracized will convince them to give her food, thus acting as an insurance mechanism. The glue that holds the community together will also help her get valuable information and share her concerns with others. In the age of algorithms, we have new online communities. It would be natural to expect them to play similar roles as those of working-class or ethnic communities in modern Western societies or the communities mandating tight-knit behaviors and sharing in many premodern societies. Perhaps they add another useful layer to the networks that each of us belongs to, enabling better sharing of information or valuable insurance? Reality is more complex, however. The data suggest that online communities do not complement or add to but replace real-world social networks. Today, Americans have many fewer friends than they did just a few decades ago. In 1990, 33 percent of those surveyed said they had ten or more friends. In the 2020s, this number is down to just 13 percent. Many more Americans today have fewer than three friends. Time spent in real-world social interactions has also declined sharply, especially for younger people, who tend to be more active online. Over the last twenty years, in- person social engagement with friends has decreased from about 60 minutes to about 20 minutes per day. For people between ages fifteen and twenty-four, the situation is truly dire: In-person engagement has declined from about 150 minutes to 40 minutes per day over the same period. Online communities, it seems, destroy real-world ones. There is a lot of information shared across any social media platform, and people undoubtedly learn from their online communities. However, much online information may be unreliable. The tweets about Justine Sacco spread not because of their information value but because they were sensational and entertaining. They also triggered many users to play the role of norm enforcers and thought police. This is the general pattern of communication on social media. Unreliable, sensational, and often downright incorrect information can be very entertaining. Pope Francis expressing disapproval of Donald Trump's callous comments toward women, immigrants, and the poor is what most people would have expected.
