Similarly, the right AI tools can boost the capabilities of nurses, as I argued in chapter 9, and nurses can additionally benefit from more specialized education targeting their profession, mediated by AI. Other service sectors can also be reorganized with the help of AI in order to increase the contribution of workers. Finally, if AI were to increase productivity in services more broadly, it would also dull the impact of Baumol's cost disease-since there would no longer be a productivity growth asymmetry between manufacturing and services activating this effect. Pro-worker AI is feasible. Yet it will not happen by itself. A key pillar of working-class liberalism, therefore, is practical policies to encourage it. The heart of such an approach would be to redirect technological change away from a focus on AGI and toward pro-worker AI-more emphasis on domain-specific applications aimed at providing greater capabilities and better information to workers and less emphasis on mimicking human cognition. There is no silver-bullet policy that can achieve such an outcome, but policy options are available. Even before policies come aspirations. Any governing ideology, including liberalism in its heyday, works by inspiring people. Working-class liberalism must likewise change the narrative away from the desirability or inevitability of an elite-driven future in which workers are sidelined, toward one in which AI and digital technology empower workers to play an essential role in production and in society. Several policy levers can play complementary roles in achieving this objective. First, the most effective policies are likely to be those that provide direct incentives for creating pro- worker technologies. We need new AI agencies (instituted, for example, at the U.S. federal and European levels) tasked with disseminating information throughout society and supporting a socially beneficial direction for AI. Incentives for new pro-worker technologies to be developed and commercialized could be established through subsidy programs or competitions that reward the best solutions to well-posed problems, overseen by these agencies. We must also get better at identifying pro-worker technologies. Some of the characteristics of these technologies were outlined in the previous chapter, but a more systematic measurement framework is needed. Such a framework would help policymakers decide which innovations to support. Inevitably, it will be imperfect-sometimes labeling a labor-saving tool as worker-friendly. That is not a showstopper. The goal is not to block automation but to ensure that worker-enhancing technologies advance alongside it. If some subsidies meant for pro-worker AI end up supporting automation, that is a tolerable slippage. A second policy lever relates to the tax code. Tax laws in many industrialized economies encourage automation. In the United States, for example, labor income is taxed more heavily than capital income-with a wedge of about 25 percent. This means that paying workers $100 generates tax and spending obligations of about $30 for businesses, while paying $100 for automation equipment leads to a tax burden of less than $5. Consequently, even if automation is considerably less productive than labor, companies may make money by automating. What's worse, the additional automation that this tax asymmetry induces tends to be so-so-not that productive or revolutionary, but just enough to displace workers from their jobs. This is because the asymmetry of the tax code triggers the kind of automation that companies would not have found profitable without the tax inducement. Some of this 25 percent wedge is because employers have to pay payroll taxes and contribute to health insurance and other fringe benefits for workers. But much of it has arisen over the last three and a half decades, thanks to accelerated depreciation allowances and special provisions that allow companies to write off spending on computer and digital equipment from their tax obligations. Removing these distortions in the tax system-for example, by returning to the tax laws of 1990-would create a more level playing field for pro-worker technologies. It would encourage employers to hire human workers when they can do the same jobs as productively as machines and algorithms. The greater demand for labor generated by the removal of these distortions would also encourage the development of more pro-worker technologies. A third and related policy goes back to worker skills. New tasks most often require new skills. Hence, worker training is again critical. By "training" here, I'm not referring to the unrealistic aim of turning coal miners into computer programmers. Rather, training should aim to allow workers, including manual and craft workers, to boost their skills and expertise using new technologies, so that society can produce better electricians, plumbers, nurses, and educators. Some of this training can be achieved through new institutions of higher learning, as I have already discussed. Often, though, the most direct way to build worker skills is with on-the-job training programs. Yet employers in the United States and many European nations underinvest in worker training, focusing instead on reducing labor costs through automation. (Germany is a partial exception, as mentioned above.) Employers also do not believe they can fully capture the productivity gains that such investments generate. The aforementioned distortions further contribute to the lack of adequate incentives to invest in workers. Encouraging worker training programs would be one way of redressing these distortions in the tax code, though there are dangers in adding too many exemptions from tax obligations. For example, allowing tax deductions for both capital and training expenditures might be worse than removing all such deductions. Redirecting technological change toward pro-worker digital tools and AI must be at the heart of a working-class liberal policy agenda. But there are other secondary objectives that are both valuable in and of themselves and complementary to the objective of redirecting technological change. Chief among them is reducing the dominance of technology monopolies. Tech behemoths have an outsize effect on the current direction of technology, and their influence is pushing AI toward faster automation, AGI, surveillance, and monetization of people's data via digital ads. Creating a more competitive environment would be synergistic with achieving pro-worker AI. This is because monopoly in the tech sector discourages new directions of innovation. A more competitive landscape would create more opportunities for new companies to experiment with pro- worker approaches. These monopolies have also created another dimension of inequality between college and noncollege workers (especially favoring elite graduates, as we saw in chapter 6). Overall, reducing these monopolies' hold on the future of technology could produce a more level playing field in the labor market and the economy. Working-class liberalism should additionally give support to the labor movement, so that unions can protect workers against abuses by powerful employers and rise to the challenge of promoting a pro-worker AI agenda. But the labor movement itself must change in the process as well. In an age defined by AI, algorithms, and robotics, bargaining just for higher wages and hoping automation will be slow is not a realistic strategy. In fact, when there are plenty of automation technologies on offer, most companies would meet higher wage demands by choosing more automation. This is the reason why the labor movement has to adopt a different stance and strive to work with management in order to encourage a pro-worker direction for technology and work organization. This means, in particular, union leaders themselves becoming sufficiently well-versed in AI and digital technologies to be effective in these negotiations. In this task, unions can leverage the fact that the workers themselves best understand their work-certainly better than tech companies do, and often better than their management does.

Challenge 5: Drains for the Twenty-First Century The success of liberalism was intertwined with better public services-roads, infrastructure, social safety net, health care, education. Today, the crisis of liberal democracy is also a crisis of drains and dole. Working-class liberalism must make a renewed commitment to the social safety net and the public services that many working-class communities so badly need. The emphasis on pro-worker AI and good jobs doesn't obviate the need for a strong social safety net and redistribution. My emphasis on pro-worker AI is rooted in the belief that a better social safety net and more redistribution, though critical, will not be sufficient by themselves. All the same, a robust safety net is core to the working-class liberal agenda. Everyone should have reliable access to health care; workers who lose their jobs should receive swift assistance that helps them retrain and reenter the labor market; and people facing lasting disadvantages or disabilities should be guaranteed stable, long-term support. In short, no one-whether contending with a temporary setback or a permanent limitation-should be left to fall through the cracks. Because all arbitrary hierarchies are inimical to experimentation and thus to liberalism, redistribution and the right kind of regulations aimed at creating a level playing field are vital for the liberal agenda. To the extent possible, working-class liberalism should dismantle any artificial sources of inequity of opportunity and inequality of outcome. These include monopolies as well as distortions in current laws, for example the asymmetry of the tax code between capital and labor discussed previously. However, the exact extent of redistribution should be determined by weighing the main trade- offs, for example between the disincentive and inequality-reducing effects of redistribution. It would be wrong to declare that liberalism, per se, favors the amount of progressive taxation and redistribution in the Nordic countries more than that in the United States, or vice versa. Each liberal democracy has to work out these trade-offs itself, according to the dynamics of self-government. But some general principles should be clear: In a highly unequal society, liberalism cannot shy away from redistribution and the provision of a public safety net. Conversely, tax rates on entrepreneurs and businesses that are so high as to prevent investment and innovation would betray liberalism's commitment to experimentation and collective knowledge building. And redistribution should be appropriately targeted. For example, it should never bypass the needy in order to curry favor with the middle class, as some redistributive efforts in the United States and Europe currently do. When it comes to providing high-quality services, there are several distinct issues to consider. The first is that education and health care have become more challenging since the low-hanging fruit of expanding coverage was already picked in the early postwar decades. This problem is complicated by the fact that many of the relevant services, especially in education, are provided by local governments, and coordinating between federal and local governments is not always easy. Second, the speed and effectiveness of public service provision have diminished while the cost of these services has ballooned thanks to heavy regulation, especially rules that increase the number of stakeholders that need to be convinced and the procedural steps that need to be taken ex ante-meaning that these are steps that need to be cleared before a project can be initiated. Many liberal democracies opted for such ex ante steps because of a desire to ensure equal access and equality before the law and because, all else equal, it is beneficial and more democratic to convince stakeholders that they will suffer no harm before a project is initiated. But this potentially introduces too many "veto players" who can block or significantly slow down public and private projects, especially those that impact members of various communities (such as urban redesign, roadbuilding, or new housing construction). I have also suggested that some of these procedures are a consequence of the social engineering agenda-since procedures and regulations are intertwined with efforts to control and change society from the top down. Cost-effective public service provision necessitates removing many of these procedures. Critically, this should not be a full-throated deregulation agenda. Deregulating nonessential aspects of local and federal government functions must go hand in hand with government intervention in vital areas such as the direction of AI and social media. Put differently, we need a sharper focus on truly critical regulations and fewer hurdles in nonessential areas-for example, eliminating licensing requirements for hairdressers and massage therapists, reducing rules that pursue broad social objectives such as diversity quotas, and trimming redundant environmental reviews and other procedural steps. This is both to unleash liberalism's much-needed potential to deliver public services and because regulations are often an ineffective way of achieving broad social objectives. For example, it would be much more impactful to combat climate change via carbon taxes and encouragements for green technologies than through endless procedures to evaluate environmental impact. We also need a regulatory approach based on a different conceptualization of public accountability. The alternative to ex ante regulations is ex post accountability, which I believe needs to be more widely used-though, for AI, social media, and certain issues relating to health and safety, ex ante regulations would also be necessary. An example of ex post accountability is the mandate given to the Federal Reserve. The Fed is not beyond reproach, and one could agree or disagree with its policies during the financial crisis of 2008. But the system works, by and large. The Fed chairman and the Fed board make decisions without interference and the need to satisfy outside stakeholders but can then be questioned by the legislature, oversight agencies, and civil society about the appropriateness of their responses. In cases of gross abuse (such as corruption), an ex post accountability regime can impose heavy penalties and even jail time on transgressors. Clarifying what will be considered transgressions and how they will be dealt with is a necessary step for a more systematic ex post accountability regime. The advantage of such a system is ensuring that ex ante requirements will not paralyze initiative and slow down the provision of public services. Third, it is also possible that a socially beneficial direction of development for AI would be synergistic with the agenda of providing better public services and a more effective social safety net. AI can help identify how to best distribute public services. Much more important is the possibility that pro-worker AI could boost productivity in health care and education, the areas with the greatest need for cost-effective, high-quality public services. Fourth, public service provision has to be married with the community-level self-government agenda. Federalism is a useful tool in this regard. "Federalism" refers to an institutional structure where powers and responsibilities are allocated between different levels of government-federal, regional, and local. The United States, Australia, India, and Brazil are examples of federal structures. Federalism is closely intertwined with local self-government, since when powers are highly centralized in the national government, local self-government becomes impossible. Federalism also allows different local units to pursue their own policy preferences, which supports community-level experimentation. It is essential that federalism be combined with the other priorities I have described in this chapter. Otherwise, a separation of responsibilities and powers between federal, regional, and local governments might pile up more procedures and ex ante steps, further complicating the provision of cost-effective, high-quality public services. Finally, liberal values and aspirations for self-government also need to be leveraged in order to bring more people into public office who are committed to serving their communities. This objective is complementary to building a higher-level shared identity and agreement on general moral principles that can organize and mobilize the population at large. At its peak, liberalism lit a moral fire-rallying ordinary citizens to defend freedom and stand up for one another. Working-class liberalism must rekindle that spark, urging people who share these values to enter public life. When government is left to power-hungry opportunists and zealots, true self-government withers. Liberal democracy needs men and women inspired by its values to step forward.

Immigrant Battles Arguably, no other topic has been as polarizing as immigration in both the United States and Europe. As Trump's experience in New Hampshire illustrates, in many instances voters have demanded anti- immigrant rhetoric. Here, as in other issues involving communities and their values, there are no hard-and-fast truths. Liberalism has to deal with gray areas. Given its importance, the issue of immigration provides a test case for evaluating how working- class liberalism can navigate the challenges I have outlined. First, as already mentioned in chapter 1, economic research suggests that immigration-both skilled and unskilled-does not have major adverse effects on the job prospects or wages of native- born workers. This is not to say that the cultural concerns of many native-born, white, working-class communities are illegitimate. Cultural preferences are no less fundamental than economic ones. Liberalism's commitment to respecting communities' preferences must extend to their cultural preferences as well-provided that this does not conflict with its even greater commitment to basic liberties. Often it is communities with very few immigrants (like those in New Hampshire) that have the strongest anti-immigrant attitudes. One way out of this quagmire is to build local policies -but not necessarily national policies-on local preferences. This principle is also consistent with notions that emphasize that, whenever possible, problems should be dealt with by groups and communities that are closest to them. Hence, communities' cultural preferences should be important in determining whether immigrants or refugees should be induced to integrate into these communities (for example, whether they should receive housing subsidies to settle in these communities), but not necessarily for national policies. The government can also use further incentives to influence local communities. National governments receive much of the additional tax revenue that immigrants generate, and part of this can be redistributed to local communities that welcome immigrants. Second, liberal society owes an implicit debt to immigrant communities that have settled in the country-in the same way that it should recognize the community preferences of native-born ethnic majority and minority groups. Immigrants who have come into a country legally or have been in the country for an extended period of time should be protected, and when they form communities, these communities should enjoy the same rights and respect for their priorities as native communities. But it isn't just about rights. Responsibilities also matter, and the most important ones in liberal democracy center on a commitment to not violate the freedoms of others. Therefore, immigrant communities should not be allowed to limit the education of their members, take away their right to exit, or inflict violence or threats of violence on women or sexual minorities within them. Neither should native communities, of course. Third, citizenship matters. Solidarity needs to be cultivated not just for communities but also nationally, and this requires a broader notion of citizenship and belonging at this higher level. Therefore, for shared moral principles to guide national politics and democratic governance, immigrants should be able to become citizens, with all the rights, responsibilities, and solidarity that this confers. Critically, however, this must be accompanied by immigrant communities (at least partially) accepting the common narrative that creates citizenship. In turn, this necessitates that the common narrative be inclusive enough to embrace immigrants, like the message of Lincoln's Gettysburg Address. In contrast, a racist or supremacist narrative would not allow such a path to citizenship (beyond being generally inconsistent with liberal values). Any immigration policy that eschews the need to build citizenship will ultimately damage democracy and self-government. Creating a pathway to citizenship may become more difficult for new immigrants coming from a very different culture. There is no perfect recipe in such cases, and the most important elements may be to listen to the democratic process, at the local and national level, and to different viewpoints respectfully and without silencing them. Fourth, I additionally believe that liberal democracy owes special consideration to the people in the direst need. Hence, refugees fleeing threats against their lives and serious persecution should be given priority. No country can be expected to accept an unlimited number of refugees, but there could be special dispensations for the most vulnerable people seeking true refuge, who are distinct from those trying to immigrate for economic betterment. It is vital, however, to make sure that even on issues involving refugees, there is a broad dialogue and different viewpoints are heard without denigration. If certain local communities wish to express views opposed to permissive refugee policies (without violating anybody's basic liberties), these should be respected. When conflicts arise between certain community preferences and liberal priorities on immigration or other issues, deliberation and compromise based on shared moral principles provide the best path forward. Put differently, liberalism must strive to get broad buy-in from society for its policy priorities. In fact, in a liberal democracy, sufficiently broad buy-in, both from citizens and from self-governing communities, is necessary for a stable policy on any topic, and certainly on contentious ones like immigration. But what does "broad buy-in" mean in this context? It would be unrealistic to expect that everybody in society or a majority in almost every community would agree on any given policy. Hence, "broad buy-in" must be interpreted as meaning a sufficient plurality of citizens as a whole and a majority of self-governing communities accepting the relevant policies and institutions. Societies themselves should decide what the exact thresholds should be, once they take into account the interplay between community and freedom. Finally, shared prosperity, good jobs, and adequate public services would help resolve some of the problems related to immigration. Times of scarcity (of jobs and public services) are likely to make people less generous and less welcoming of others. The core commitment of working-class liberalism to producing good jobs and useful public services for communities can indirectly aid with the difficulties surrounding immigration. Why is this approach to immigration in line with working-class priorities? After all, some might argue that the principles I have outlined here are "too pro-immigrant" relative to the views of several working-class communities in the U.S. and Europe. Nevertheless, I believe that such an approach would be aligned with working-class liberalism for three reasons. To start with, it completely eschews social engineering efforts and recognizes the validity of the cultural concerns of working- class communities. Additionally, providing voice to local communities-which can influence local and national debates-is critical and can dispel the notion that their concerns and views are being suppressed. It also recognizes that charity and responsibilities start at home. Finally, the emphasis on self-government is important to many communities and is foundational to working-class liberalism.

New Difficulties and Opportunities I now discuss five areas that create particular challenges and opportunities for working-class liberalism: social media, the role of money in politics, demographic change, climate change, and globalization. I argued that liberalism's crisis is intimately linked to its failure to adapt to a changing world. So as not to repeat the same mistakes, it is important to start the conversation on how liberalism should deal with major oncoming trends. While there is much to be said on each of them, I will keep my discussion to essentials for the sake of brevity. Social media: Social media is a vexing test case for liberalism. On the one hand, any restriction on social media can be construed as a limit on freedom of expression. On the other hand, liberalism also has a strong interest in ensuring that people's perceptions are reasonably well aligned with reality -otherwise effective citizenship, which is essential for self-government, becomes challenging. Social media not only spreads misinformation but also has several negative social consequences, as discussed in the previous chapter. It has created a mental health crisis, especially for the youth. It has diminished real-world social networks and experiences, causing negative impacts on community, solidarity, and democratic politics. And it has helped propagate intense norm enforcement, thought policing, and denigration of alternative views. On balance, I believe that liberal democracy must regulate social media, but it must do so in a way that does not violate freedom of expression. The sweet spot is to allow all opinions, even unpopular and offensive ones, to be freely expressed on social media, but then regulate how social media platforms promote different content. A lot of the harm of social media comes from the desire of platforms to monetize people's participation and data by capturing their intense attention and emotions and then sending them digital ads. Given this context, regulation of social media can proceed on three fronts. First, in the same way that the ACLU fought for the right of neo-Nazis to hold a rally, we should not restrict the right of offensive and even racist ideas to exist on social media. But there can and should be restrictions on the ways platforms algorithmically promote content in order to increase circulation and generate additional digital ad revenue. A simple way of accomplishing this would be to completely stop algorithmic feeds, so that people experience social media content only from their friends and selected sources in chronological order. If eliminating all algorithmic feeds is too radical, another way of achieving this balance would be to hold platforms to the same standards as traditional media outlets for how reliable and truthful the material they promote should be. Put differently, a social media platform, say, Facebook, should be judged legally and by public opinion for the content it promotes in the same way that The New York Times and The Wall Street Journal are judged for their reporting, editorials, and op-ed selections. On the other hand, it should have no such obligations for posted content that is not promoted. Second, there should be regulation of how and when children can access social media. Many of the negative effects on the formation of real-world social networks and much of the potential mental health damage originate with children and teens. In the same way that children can be given special protection in a liberal society and are not allowed to purchase tobacco or drugs, their ability to use certain social media platforms could be restricted. At a minimum, social media access should be prevented during school hours. There could additionally be limits on the total number of hours children and teenagers can spend on social media. Policy could also legislate that individuals under the age of eighteen can access only versions of social media sites with less addictive content or fewer digital ads. Third, since monetization via digital ads is intertwined with many of the negative effects of social media, liberal governments could impose digital ad taxes. These taxes would reduce the profitability of the prevailing business model, which is based on capturing attention by presenting ever more emotionally charged and addictive content. In its place, digital ad taxes would encourage alternative business models that may have fewer negative consequences-think of the business models of Wikipedia (based on volunteers and donations) or Netflix (based on subscriptions) versus those of Facebook, Instagram, and TikTok. Regulating social media isn't just about curbing abuse; if such regulation nudges us off our screens and back into real-world connection, it fortifies community and solidarity-a win on every front. Money in politics: The principle I invoked in my discussion of social media is that freedom of expression applies to individuals but not necessarily to platforms and corporations. Platforms are not neutral repositories when they algorithmically boost different content-this is when they should be held responsible for the content they promote. Individuals, regardless of whether they do or do not work for a platform, should have freedom of expression. In the same way, corporations should not have freedom of expression, despite the fateful Citizens United Supreme Court decision, which gave them such a privilege in the United States. Liberal societies must limit the amount of money coming from corporations into politics, which creates a nonlevel playing field and potential abuses of (economic) power. Limiting campaign contributions or lobbying expenditures should be done-and can be done-in a way that does not abridge anybody's freedom of expression. A billionaire can still express their opinion, even if there are limits to how much money they can put in the pockets of politicians. Demographic change: An additional factor shaping the world in which liberalism will have to function is demographic change. The world has already started a rapid process of aging, and many countries will soon have declining populations. Economists are worried about the macroeconomic consequences of such demographic change. Shortages of certain types of workers, the presence of fewer young entrepreneurs taking risks, and lower demand for services and goods coming from younger cohorts could create myriad imbalances. While many of these fears are valid, several countries, including Germany, Japan, and South Korea, have already successfully adapted to demographic change. In fact, looking beyond these difficulties, demographic change also creates four potential opportunities for liberal societies. First, shortage of labor can facilitate the creation of good jobs for workers of diverse skills. Germany, Japan, and South Korea have already lived through the scarcity of younger and middle- aged workers who used to perform heavy, manual tasks, and this enabled them to combine robotics technology with other strategies for increasing labor productivity. Their experiences could act as a template for liberal democracies to build on new technologies, including AI, in order to achieve higher productivity and good jobs for workers. Second, demographic change generates demand for new goods and services, which could be synergistic with the agenda of redirecting AI away from automation and AGI and toward new human competencies and tasks, because new goods and services are best dreamed of by leveraging human judgment and creativity, and also because they will tend to create novel functions for human workers. Third, the current demographic change is also leading to longer, healthier lives for most people in the industrialized world. This can facilitate greater investment in training and lifelong learning for all workers, and these additional skills can help the agenda of creating better jobs. Fourth, population declines can ultimately start changing attitudes toward immigration. To put it simply, many Western countries will need immigrants both to fill jobs for which they do not have enough domestic workers and to create a more balanced age profile in their workforce-which is necessary, among other reasons, for the viability of their social security and other public programs. Climate change: Heavy industry and coal plants anchor many working-class towns. When policymakers push for a rapid green transition, these communities see the bill landing on their doorstep-while college-educated city dwellers escape most of the cost. The reality is that climate change will affect people of all backgrounds. There is no reason working-class communities should be opposed to climate change mitigation, especially if such policies are not seen as being imposed on them and are coupled with other provisions so that the costs and benefits are more equally distributed in society. One way of doing this would be to commit to building new clean-energy facilities and other job opportunities related to the energy transition in exactly the same places where industry and fossil fuel jobs will disappear. Increasing trust in experts and improving the role of community as a vehicle for information sharing should also help to remove misinformation targeting climate change issues. One of working-class liberalism's advantages is in building such trust, owing to its commitment to respecting community values and to self-government. Policy should also focus on climate adaptation, which will be necessary even if the energy transition takes place. Such adaptation will have to involve new goods and services and urban redesigns, and many of these could be coupled with pro-worker AI and jobs in sectors such as construction. Tying climate change policy to border tax adjustments (or "carbon tariffs") would also be useful. A carbon tax imposed, say, in the United States creates a disadvantage for American corporations and workers and an advantage for exporting countries that do not have similar carbon taxes. Carbon tariffs tackle this problem by imposing additional taxes on imports into a country commensurate with the amount of carbon that these imports contain. Such tariffs therefore eliminate one of business owners' and workers' most salient concerns about the negative consequences of carbon taxes. Globalization: Globalization can generate economic benefits by enabling countries to specialize in sectors in which they have an advantage in terms of productivity or the availability of labor. Yet globalization has powerfully contributed to inequalities over the last several decades, as I explained in chapter 6. This was largely because the globalization of the last four decades has favored capital ahead of labor, as companies got access to cheap labor overseas. It also favored multinational corporations relative to domestic economies, because these corporations became the main beneficiaries of cheap foreign labor and new tax-evasion opportunities. Working-class liberalism can aim for a new kind of globalization that is more pro-worker. To start with, it should strive for treaties that prevent tax evasion. Another useful policy would be the analogue of carbon tariffs but applied on goods according to differences in labor standards across countries. Hence, imports from countries that do not respect labor safety or the right of labor to organize could be subject to tariffs. A Working-Class Liberalism for the Future Liberalism of the future has to make renewed commitments. These commitments should be to both new ideologies and novel policy agendas. These are not just compromises that liberalism has to make in order to garner more support. They would make liberalism better and stronger-and more in line with the pillars I defended in chapter 2. History shows that when liberalism falters, its enemies rush in. We are only at the beginning of this unraveling. Across nations, illiberal forces are moving swiftly-silencing speech, twisting the law, and reimposing hierarchies long thought buried. Violence against the vulnerable is no longer a distant fear; it is a shadow on our doorstep. To expose illiberal hypocrisy is not enough. To wait for antidemocratic movements to exhaust themselves is folly. If liberalism is to endure, it must be rebuilt. That rebirth will not come from elite debates or technocratic fixes. It will definitely not come from social engineering. It can come only from returning liberalism to its foundational values-freedom of expression, community, and a firm commitment to shared prosperity. In short, it must come from working-class liberalism. This book is not only about why liberal democracy gives us the best institutions for pursuing our social good or understanding liberalism's successes and failures. It's also an invitation. The defense of liberty falls to us, especially those aligned with the basic values of liberalism as I have outlined them in this book. And if we rise to the task-if we build a working-class liberalism strong enough to speak to the many, not just the few-then liberalism can thrive again: not as a relic of the past but as a common inheritance of freedom, equality, and democracy for generations to come. If liberalism is to rise again, it must rise with the working class at its heart.
